BEST & GOES FARTHEST.

ft h

them in the ranks of temperance when they le_ iands of hope and went to work. The various temperance federations up and down the country were, none too quickly, awakening to the fact that they needed to have an organization asactive,as far-reaching, as carefully devised -Ind systematically arranged as the organizations connected with the Licensed Victuallers' Associations. In addition they had a large number of men in all the churches who were capable even of influencing that unfortunate creation of modern Toryism,—the modern Parliament—and making it possible for it to remember that there was a great body of people in the country who would not be bamboozled into surrendering their temperance principles and prerogatives, and who were determined to see justice done between man and man, especially when the good of the community was concerned. There were now many newspapers which, while not necessarily symPathetic with total abstinence principles, were beginning to see that the •root 9f a great many social wrongs of our day mightbe definitely traced to the liquor traffic and that unlessiParliament was continually urged to work upon the lines of Lord Peel's minority report the drink traffic would stranglethe best manhood of the country. Mr. Matthews quoted several provisions which, it was stated in certain newspapers, would probably be included in a forthcoming Govern- ment Licensing Bill. It would, it NOW stated, be left to licensed victuallers to frame their own compensa- tion scheme, but for a period of five years the magis- trates wotild be deprived•of their power to take away licences on the mere ground that they were not required by the necessities of the neighbourhood. Mr. Matthews strongly objected to the latter provision and said the magisterial right must not be allowed to go. Another proposal was, it was reported, that in future no new licences would be granted unless two old licences were surrendered in return, but the area from which these confiscated licences were to be taken must be con- siderably extended. That minimised the value of the clause. A fourth clause was that the ante-1869 beer- houses would come under the ordinary law and be sur- rendered at-the disoretion of the magistrates! It was not yet decided whether any compensation would be granted. In conclusion Mr. Matthews made a powerful appeal to temperance workers not to allow the magis- serial right to refuse the renewal of licenses to be ,surrendered, and said he was not a pessimist in regard to the temperance outlook. Mr. BORELAND asked his hearers not to place much reliance on the forecast of the Licensing Bill. The resolution was unanimously carried. During the evening solos were rendered by Mrs. Moores, and the trio " The Social Glass" was con- tributed by Messrs. C. J. Sheath, T. Hughes, and A. P. Walmsley. A vote of thanks was accorded to all who had assisted, on the proposition of PastorGnAov, seconded by Mr. J. W. ROBERTS.

THE TEMPERANCE OUTLOOK.

Rev. J. F. Matthews Reviews the Situation.

MR. E. BORELAND AND THE COMPENSA- TION QUESTION.

Under the auspices of the Warrington Temperance Federation a public meeting was held in Ebenezer Church, Buttermarket-street, on Wednesday evening, Councillor H. Thornton presiding over a fair attend- ance. He was supported by the Rev. J. F'. Matthews and Mr. E. Boreland, of Liverpool (United Kingdom Alliance agent). The CHAIRMAN, in speaking of his efforts in the temperance cause, said that if he told them tlfe amount of money he had spent in lifting up people who had been knocked down by the terrible monster of intemper- ance, they would hardly credit it. He intended now to direct his workings in another channel, and get at the roots Of the evil in the hope that lasting goodl might be done. .Altbough such a vast sum was spent by the people of this country in drink,_ the" trade " had the impudence to ask for compensation for confiscated licences. That, he contended, was out of the question. (Applause). Mr. BORELAND moved the following resolution : "That, inasmuchfas the Prime Minister has declared on behalf of the Government that it is their intention to pass in the comino, session of Parliament a measure de- signed to deprive the magistrates of the full discretion which they now possess over the renewal of licences for the sale of intoxicating liquors, this meeting emphat- ically protests against such retrograde legislation and hereby pledges itself to the utmost extent of its power." Mr. Boreland dealt at some length with the Compensa- tion Bill introduced by Mr. Butcher. He agreed that if compensation was to be given the trade should provide the fund. It was not, however, equally true that if the trade were prepared to find the money temperance reformers ought to agree to compensation. The proposals contained in the Bill would have put the hand of the temperance clock back. According to Mr. T. P. Whittaker, M.P., the eminent statistician the total amount that could be raised by Levying "the trade" on the scale laid down in the Bill was £500,000 per annum. Assuming that the average value of a licence was £2,000 Ithere would only be money enough to compensate for the non-renewal of 250 licences per year. In order to get rid of those licences they would have to give the liquor trade what they never had before—and what they were longing for—a vested interest in licences. Therefore they would be paying a price far in excess of the value they obtained in return. If Mr. Butcher's Bill had contained proposals for the advancement of temper- ance, such as local veto, there would have been two sides to the bargain, but at present only the liquor trade would benefit. The number of licences taken away in England and Wales without compensation last year was 220 on the simple question of "no longer re- quired" and 218 on the issue of "not required' and a very moderate offence against the law. Compensation was but a small thing compared with the establishment of a vested interest and it would fix the liquor traffic round the necks of the people for ever SincelB99 no fewer than 418 licences had been done away with in Liverpool, an-1 he contended that if Mr. Butcher's bill had been law the brewers would not have been so ready to meet the wishes of the magistrates by surrendering;licences. If the trade required compensa- tion they might form an insurance fund, such as that established to meet claims under the Employers' Liability Act without troubling Parliament. At the pre- sent time at least half of the retail licences were in- sured against non-renewal, so why could not the trade insure the others? They would not, acdording to Mr. Whittaker, incur any more expenditure than would be' laid upon them if they took the measures provided in Mr. Butcher's bill. In concluzion he urged his hearers to vote on the temperance ques- tion at Parliamentary elections. The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS spoke on "The present temperance outlook "and at the outset expressed the hope that in days to come the work of the Temper- ance Federation in the sectional gatherings vaonld be takenup, emphasised, and assisted by the churches in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) He said that very feelingly because while most of their churches had bands of hope and various temperance agencies he did not think that on the whole the work of the Temperance Federation, which was a work of a representative character, re- ceived all the support it was entitled to. Now that it had been reconstituted and reconstructed from the base to the summit, he hoped it would have the warmhearted and loyal support of the whole of the Free Churches,at any rate, in Warrington. After a brief survey of the forces arrayed against temperance, the speaker con- tended that the brewing fraternity was never more alive than it was to-day, was never attempting more to dominate the whole civil and political situa- tion, and was never more determined to graft all the power it possessed upon the voting power of the country than it was to-day. With regard to the forces that temperance reformers had to face in Warrington, Mr. Thornton knew how difficult it was to get anything done by the Bench when the question of licences came up, and how extremely difficult it was to get a conviction against a publican when the police did their best to secure one. And what were the forces at their command? They had—and he was happy to think the work was growing in the town—the various bands of hope, and they could not do better than address themselves still more vigorously to the inculcation of total abstinence principles among the boys and girls. Might he suggest a change in their propaganda methods? He was sick and tired of the entertainments associated with the bands of hope, and he was looking forward to the time when the bands of hope would be societies wherethe best scientific teaching they could get would be used in training children in total abstinence principles. They wanted the children to know, from the scientific standpoint, the deleterious effect of alcohol upon the human system and they would be better able to retain

FOUND AT LAST.

For many years physicians have been seeking a remedy for Bright's Disease. That their efforts have been at last crowned with success will be good news to all who know how many precious lives have been lost through this hitherto incurable disease. In support of the claim that a perfect remedy for this fatal malady has at last been discovered, the case of Mrs. E. A. Jenkin, of Connor Downs, near Hayle, Cornwall, is quoted. Mrs. Jenkin makes this statement. "My Kidneys were bad for a long time, quite thre9 years. I suffered from Palpitation of the heart, ha, dry skin; my body seemed to burn at times and others I would feel cold and chilly. I found that I had Bright's Disease. I tried many remedies but did not seem to get any better. I began to use Dodd's Kidney Pills and, after a few doses, I felt better and was con- vinced that I had at last found the right medicine. I gave up all other medicine and kept on taking Dodd's Kidney Pills and they cured me. \Npw, I ani quite well and strong."

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL.

The Pending Appeal.

In this case, Crosfield and others v. the Manchester Ship Canal Company, Mr. Pickford, K.C., applied to the Court of Appeal, on Monday to fix a date for the hear- ing. On behalf of the defendants, he asked for February 18, and said the plaintiffs would not be prejudiced by delay, because no stay of execution had been granted in the court below, and operations the defendants had to perform—namely, the making of a channel in the Mersey, near Warrington, were going on, and the plaintiffs were in possession of as full rights now as they could be if the appeal had been heard and dis- missed with costs. The reason for asking fora date so far distant was that Mr. Fletcher Moulton and Mr. Cripps, who led him, would both be engaged before the Water Arbitration Court. Mr. Bousfield, K.C., for the plaintiffs, was not prepared to concede more than a fort- night, and the date was fixed for the 25th inst.

Mr. W. W. Jacobs once more makes his bow to readers of the "Strand Magazine" in the opening chapters of a serial story called "Dialstone Lane contributed to the January number. Most people have wondered at one time or another how the chromo- lithographs, with which everyone is so familiar, are produced—whether the colours are all printed together or two or three at a time, in,short, how it is done. All this is explained very fully and clearly by Mr. L. Gray Gower, and theresare no less than 19 illustrations showing a picture in the different stages. Sherlock Holmes is to the fore again with an " Adventure of the Solitary Cyclist," and Mr. Max Pemberton and Miss Winifred Graham, who also contribute to the month's fiction,assist to make this a strong number,and an excel- lent start for the twenty-seventh volume. An inter- view with M. Curie, the discoverer of radium, supplies the scientific interest in a popular manner ; and art, from the point of view of "Trousers in Sculpture," is a readable article to which the photographs lend ad- ditional interest.

The Examiner Saturday, January 16, 1904.

did 1 know that he *as a rich man, or that h regarded me in the light of his successor. I sup- pose I must run down and see the old fellow since he wishes it."

"You certainly will if you are wise. What luck some fellows have! Well, you're a good fellow, Tom, and nobody will grudge it you.. And you look as if a little fresh air would do you good. I only wish it was my luck to get the same. It's my part of the country down there. Don't I wish I could go!"

"Come with me," said Torn heartily, "I hate too much of my own company. I'll stand treat, and you shall take all the trouble. It's a bargain, mind! I'll drop you at your place, and stop the night there, and go on to my uncle's next day. It's only a matter of twelve miles or so, you say.'

And so it came about that a few days later, upon a lovely June evening, Tom and his friend were sitting perched upon some crumbling sand-cliffs, overlooking a wide expanse of level sand below, and beyond that the shining, sparkling sea and the glow of the sunset in the west.

They had been talking quietly on indifferent subjects when Arlington suddenly sprang half-up exclaiming: "Great Jehoshaphat Is she being run away with? Or can it be she does not know ? "

"What do you mean ? " asked Tom, alarmed by the look Upon his companion's face. nis eyes were fixed upon a girl rider, galloping along the yellow sands, and his cheek was growing pale.

"The quicksands! The quicksands!" cried Arlington, the drops gathering on his brow. "If she doesn't know; or if the horse is bolting with her, she'll be into them in a jiffy—you can see the shimmering line that divides them from the firm sand—and then no power on earth can save her. She'll be sucked down to a living death before our

That thought settled the matter, and as soon as Tom's summer substitute was installed in his house for the blank months of August and September, he made his way by the northern express up to that Highland Manse, where one bright boyhood's .summer had been spent, and which he had not visited since.

His uncle had been many years dead, but the present minister received him kindly, though he could tell him nothing as to the former residents at the Burnside Farm. The present owners had only been there five years, but that was longer than his own appointment to the little kirk. He helped Tom in his inquiries, but fourteen years had passed since the persons he inquired for had departed, and nobody knew where they had gone. They 'flirt have left quite a short while after his own visit. They had gone south, he heard, and that was all. Nobody in the place had heard anything of them for a long, long time : their very memory seemed to have been blotted out.

Tom scarcely knew whether he were disappointed of relieved by this check. He had hoped to find Molly a married woman with bairns of her own clustering about her, to laugh with her over their childish troth-plight, and receive back from her his mother's gold ring in exchange for some more substantial token of good-will. But then, again, had always come the doubt: Suppose she was one of those who remembered and kept a childish faith? How then? And at that thought his heart always sank so low that it was with as much relief as dis- appointment that he discovered the impossibility of tracing her.

And now, was he free? Was there any further need to think of that childish compact, solemnly made in all truth, yet with no real understanding of its nature in the mind of either child? He thought not. He thought he might dismiss it from his mind. And yet, as the train carried him south, he found that he could not dismiss it; the vibra- tions of the sleeping carriage seemed for ever repeating the same thing:

-"With this ring I thee wed, with my body I thee worship, with all my worldly goods I thee endow, in the Name-".

Sleep would not come to him. The vision of May's lovely face came between him and any thought of rest. He hungered for a sight of her. He recalled every soft sweet word that had fallen from her lips when he had sat beside her on summer evenings and had talked to her as he had never talked to any other woman; whilst she had listened with down-bent eyes, a soft flush upon her cheek, and a tender, dreamy smile upon her lips. He felt that he could win her if he but let himself go. If he had spoken no word of love, it was not because he feared that she would not listen. It was rather that he had always felt that possibly honour held him back—that if she knew all she would tell him to seek out Molly' first—and that he knew his own conscience was whispering the same counsel.

Well, now he had done that, he had done his best. If he had failed was that his fault? Was he not free? Could honour itself demand more? The voice within him told him yes, one thing more. Before he asked troth-plight of Lady May he must tell her all the tale, and let her know how far he had once pledged himself, even though he were but a twelve-year-old urchin at the time. The welcome he received from his uncle was very warm; but as they sat at table together the first evening, he said anxiously: "I hope, my boy, that you and May will come to some understanding soon. It does not do to be too leisurely a wooer. We both thought you were beginning—well, you know what—she as well, as you. But it does not do to be too long about it. Faint heart,' you know; and my old friend was talking to me about May the other day. He has a sort of fear lest she may not be quite as free and heart-whole as we hoped. Anyway, my dear boy, don't lose time. May is a very charming girl. She has her admirers, I can tell you." Tom went to bed that night in a whirl of feeling. Yes, it was very true; he must lose no more time. He must not lose her by tardiness. He would seek her out on the 'morrow, and tell her all. He would put. his fate into her hands; he would endure this suspense and uncertainty no more.

He found her at the sunset hour in a little eerie of her own perched high upon the cliffs, looking °vet the wide, glistening sea. Her chin was in her hanil, her eyes were dreamy and soft. She did not at ence mark his approach. He spoke hr name, and she started up, the colour flushing her face from brow to chin. He stood holding her hands in his, devouring her with his burning glances, his heart beating almost to suffocation.

"May,", he said, in a voice that sounded scarcely like his own, "I love you. I think I have loved you from the first moment I saw you. Do you know why I have not told you this before? Sit down, and let me explain. When you have heard all you shall judge whether I am free—whether I may ask you the question that I long to do. You shall decide whether I am bound in honour by what happetied so many, many years ago. There shall be no cloud of secrecy between us."

Her eyes were glowing beneath the drooping lashes; she let him hold her hand in his whilst he told•his tale.

"I was only a boy; I was twelve years old. It was in Scotland, end a little girl from a farm played with me all one long summer. We loved each other, boy and girl fashion. She was but eight years old. When I was going I promised tO come back and marry her; more than this, I swore it in the most solemn way I could think of. I took the marriage vow upon my lips , we plighted our troth upon the moor. I gave her my mother's wedding_ ring as a pledge. For years I held myself married. May, what am Ito do now? When I first knew that I loved, all this came back. I felt a qualm of conscience in case Molly should remember. I have been to look for her; I cannot find her; she is gone. Am Ito lose the happiness of my life for this scruple of honour ? Is the ghost of a boyish love—a boyish vow, to stand between me and my life's happiness ? " He spoke with agitation. Her answer meant so much to him. The colour was comicg and going in her face. She raised her eyes suddenly, and looked full at him ; her lips were quivering, there was the music of mingled laughter and tears in her voice. "Tom," she said, "wa; the ring that you gave to little Molly anything like this? " She held out her left hand. .A plain gold circlet was sparkling upon the third finger. He caught her hand, and a quick gasp broke from him. Something in her look and voice—had he ever noticed it before ?—had it ever been a link between them? Was he dreaming? It could not be! 'Nutt had come to him? Was he going mad?

"Molly! Molly! May!" he cried, and could say no more. ,

"Yes, Call me Molly, Tom; it lirings back the day when I was the little foster-child of the farm —happy and wild and free. 0 Toni! I knew you the first moment when our eyes met. Again and again I thought that you must surely remember me. lam your little wife Molly. And I have never forgotten!". "Molly, Molly! Oh, how can it be? A Scotch farmer's child ? "

"No, only foster-child. My mother nearly died when I was born; nobody thought I could live. They were in the Highlands shooting; the good farmer's wife came to their help. Her baby had died the day before. She took me, and the doctors all said the only chance for my growing up strong and healthy was keeping me there for the first eight or ten years of my life and letting me run barefoot and wild, like a child of the moor. I was ten years old before I was taken home; and I never fitted into the new groove. I was never quite as the others are. I had not the same tastes or pursuits. My great joy was always to 'get out into the air. When I Was quit of the schoolroom, uncle here made me his pet. I came often to visit him. At last he invited me here altogether, and they let me come. They did not. thinkl should ever make a good match ; and this was like a prospective pro- vision-for me. You see, nobody knew that I was married alreac,ly, and I began to think that my husband would ',lot come for me. !" "Molly, 0 Molly! and you knew me when we met? "

"Oh! yes • I knew. You have not changed much, Tom, though I know I have. I had always thought of you as Tom Macallister—the name of Cheston never recalled anything—but when I saw you I knew!"

"And you never told me! 'Why not, Molly?" The blush which dyed her face was almost answer enough. He looked at her a moment and put his arms about her. She laid her head upon his shoulder with a little sigh of contentment. "You will marry me then, my Molly?" "'or the third time? " she queried, with a delicious little laugh. "Don't you remember we wpm married twice that day, to make assuranu. doubly sure r Oh: ed, • • • ou, you dear blind old bat l And think how cheap it will be for you! You will not have to buy a wedding.. ring, for I will have none other but this!"

POIINT OF HOAOIJR.

By E. EVERETT-GREEN.

Author of "Golden Gwendolyn," "Dare Lorimer's Heritage," "Shut In," "St. Wynfrith's," &c.

"Molly, I am going away to-morrow. I am going back to London." • "What is London ? " asked eight-year-old Molly with wide-open eyes, as she looked at sturdy Tom, the corners of her lips already beginning to droop. "London is a great big immense place," answered Tom, spreading out his arms as if to express im-. mensity. "If it were set down here, Molly, it would cover all this moor, and miles and miles more. It's all houses, and streets and shops. Oh, you can't guess what it's like. You've never lived anywhere but at the farm yonder." "But why must you go back there, Tom?" "Why, because my father lives there, to be sure. He sent me away up to the Manse to Uncle Macallister because there was fever in our part, and he had to go everywhere amongst it (he's a doctor, you know, and I'm going to be one too, when I grow up), but it's all over now, and school term will be beginning directly. I missed the

summer term, but I mustn't miss the Michaelmas one too; so I'm to go away to-morrow. We got the letter this morning. It ought to have come two days ago; but Sandy forgot to leave it. We've got to say good-bye, Molly ; Aor I've got such lots of things to do." _ _

Big tears had gathered in Molly's eyes. She had played with Tom all through this long happy summer; she could not face the thought of the separation with the equanimity that he was trying to assume. He wanted to play the man, and he had the prospect of the excitement of the journey and the home-coming before him; yet, all the same for that, the thought of the parting with little Molly had been heavily weighing upon him, and those brimming eyes of hers did not make things easier.

"Now, don't cry. Molly, but listen to me. I've got to go away. We can't, either of us, help that; but I'll come back again, when I'm a man, and fetch you away too; and I'm going to marry you here to-day, before I go; so that you will always belong to me, and I shall be your husband wherever YOU are. Do you understand?" Molly's eyes were big and round. She had only the dimmest ideas on the subject of matrimony; but Tom's will was law to her.

"You would like to be my little wife, Molly?"

"Oh, yes, Toni!" "Very vell, then. I will marry you now, here. I have got my Prayer Book with me. I know all about it; and I have got the wedding-ring, too. It was mother's. Father gave it to me; and I shall marry you with it, and you will hang it round your neck, because it will be too big for your finger,- and whenever you look at it you will remember that you are my little wife." "I will remember," answered Molly obediently, staring very hard, and feeling a thrill of import- ance and pride.

Torn led her to a great boulder, one of many scattered upon the bleak hillside; and he found the place in his book, and propped it open so that ha could read the words quite easily. As he did so he explained to Molly in his own words the nature of an oath, and how solemn and binding it was. His uncle had read him a lecture upon this subject not many evenings ago apropos of something he was reading in the newspaper, and Torn had a faculty for grasping an idea and making it his own. _ _ .

- - When all was ready, he took Molly's hand in his, and said, with great gravity and earnestness: "I, Tom, take thee, Molly, to be my wedded wife, to have and to hold from this day forward, for better for worse, for richer for poorer, in sick- ness and in health, to love and to cherish, till death us do part, according to God's holy ordinance; and thereto I plight thee my troth."

Then, unclasping hands, and clasping them again, he made Molly repeat after him the corresponding troth-plight: Molly, take thee, Tom," her soft voice willingly repeating the words, her big, solemn eyes fixed earnestly on his face. After that was done, Tom paused for a moment, then he produced from his pocket an assortment of miscellaneous treasures two knives, one much broken, some marbles, a small box of chalks, a pencil and pencil-cutter, a good many nails stuck in a cork, and a variety of smaller possessions. Last of all, from an inner pocket, he produced a gold ring carefully wrapped up in soft paper. These articles he laid out in order upon the boulder, and then, taking the ring in his hand, he solemnly possessed himself of Molly's left hand.

- "With this ring I thee wed, with my body I thee worship, and with all my worldly goods I thee endow; in the Name-" The boyish voice dropped a little, and there was almost a note of awe in it, and he pronounced with real reverence the Threefold Name. Molly gazed the while at him with wide, wondering eyes.

• "Now, Molly, we're married!" cried Tom, after a moment's pause, a note of triumphant jubilation in his voice. "Here's a bit of string to tie the ring round your neck, till you're big enough to wear it on your finger. But mind you take care of it always, for it's your wedding-ring. We've been married now in the English way; but we'll make everything quite safe by being married as well in the Scotch way directly; only I want you to take these things first," and he pointed to the array upon the stone. _

"But they're yours,Tom ; they're not mine." "They're yours now; didn't you hear me say I'd endowed you with them ? " Tom spoke bravely; but his eyes rested wistfully upon the best knife, and Molly was woman enough to note it. "I'll 'give you them back—all of them, Tom, dear," she cried; and then, with a diplomacy worthy of riper years,she added: "You'd better take care of them for me. l've only one pocket, and it's often got a hole in it; and mammy doesn't like me having a knife." Tom's scruples were overcAsse, and he bundled back his treasures into his own pocket, only insist- ing that Molly should have the little case of chalks.

"You might write me a letter with them or draw me a picture," he said, and Molly thought perhaps she would, when she learnt how. But there was still something more to do. "Come along," said Tom, taking her by the hand, and leading her towards the spot where the old shepherd, Donald, with his boy, Sandy, sat watching the flock, with the old collie beside them.

"I've come to say good-bye," said Tom, going up and holding out a sun-browned hand. "I'm going to London to-morrow to my father. And I want to tell you that Molly is my wife. We were married this morning. I thought. I'd like to marry her before I went away, lust to make things safe, you know."

"A braw good plan, laddie," answered the old shepherd, with a twinkle in his eye; and when Tom had done his farewells, and was alone again with Molly, he said to her: • "That's how you get married in Scotland—at least, you can if you like. You just tell people that you're married, and that makes you married. So now, you see, we've been married twice; you mustn't ever forget that, Molly. You can't marry anybody else: and when I'm a man I'll come and fetch you, and you'll live with me always and be my little wife." This thought sweetened the parting a good deal, and Molly kissed Tom, and promised she would never,. never forget ; and was quite /sure she would never want to marry anybody else. So he went a little further with her, till the grey farm-house was full in sight; but he did not come inside, even to say good-bye to Molly's mammy; for he was afraid they might cry if he did, told Tom had made up his mind not to do that. Ile gave Molly a great many hugs, and. sent her off home, with tears in her eyes, whilst he took to his heels and dashed down the side of the hill towards the old Manse, half glad that this hardest of partings was over, and rather elated .with the feeling that he was a married man and a bridegroom!

"You lucky (log!" said Ar ington. "If ever there was a fellow born With a silver spoon in his mouth, that fellow is Tom Cheston." "It's a great surprise to me," said Tom, who was now known to the world as the rising young surgeon, whose skil in certain branches was likely to make him a marked man in the future, and a more successful one-than his successful father. "I just knew that I had an old uncle down in the west; but I think he must have picked a quarrel With my father, for he never spoke of him. Nor

===== 0002601_19040116_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
very eyes!"

• Toni was m his feet and dashing down the perilous sand-cliff before the words were out of his companion's mouth. Arlington was slightly crippled from a recent attack of acute rheumatism, and had neither strength nor power for such rapid descent; nor, indeed, had it occurred to him as possible that anybody could scale the treacherous crumbling cliff, save at the places where a path had been cut out.

Perhaps. in cold blood, Tom could not have done it. But it is marvellous what we can do when the blood is tingling in our veins, and it seems a race for life or death. Toni had strength of muscle, and the trained eye and hand so essential to one in his profession, as well as the iron nerve which had helped him to his present position. Springing, slipping, running, falling, but never losing his told on something, never growing dizzy, or failing to take advantage of the nature of the treacherous cliff wherever an advantage was to be gained from it, he reached the bottom with nothing worse than a wrenched shoulder and a rent-coat, and started off at full speed across the stands to warn the rider, who was still sweeping along. Plainly her horse was giving her trouble, but plainly, also, she had not been seeking seriously to check him till the shouts and gestures of the running figure and the signals of a man on the cliffs—little more than a speck to her—warned her that she was in some unknown peril. She pulled hard at her reins. She got the horse's head partly round towards Tom, and two minutes later, through this change of direction, he was able to seize the creature's bridle, and assist her in bringing him to a standstill. When the battle had ended and he looked up, it was to encounter the glance of such radiance and sweet archness and confusion, that his heart leaped within him, as it often does at the sight of some unexpected and brilliant beauty. He was looking at the loveliest girl he had ever seen in his life; and his heart gave a sudden bound as though some strange thing had happened to it. In a few moments he explained the danger he had been in, and her face grew pale as she listened and looked.

"Oh, I did not know. I had never been here before. lam trying a new horse; ho is so very strong. I brought him here because I thought a gallop on the sands would quiet him better than anything else. How can I thank you? " _ _

"Don't thank me. It was my friend yonder who told me of the peril you were in. lam a stranger in these parts. Now are you quite sure your horse is manageable? Will you let me walk beside you till you get him to the road yonder, just at the foot of the cliff? Then there will be no fear."

He paced beside her for a few hundred yards, talking commdn-places, and stealing glances at her lovely flushed face.. When he had seen her in safety and was turning away, she held out her little gloved hand and said half-archly, half- timidly: "Good-bye. I shan't try to thank you. I can't; but may I not know the name of my preserver? lam sure my uncle will scold •me if go home without it."

Tom handed her his card with a little bow. She glanced at it and flushed and, kindled in a bewitching fashion. "We shall meet again," she cried, waving her hand.

And Tom stood looking after her, feeling as though something strange and new had come into his life, which. would never be quite the same again. Two evenings later Tom 'came face to face with this girl once more in the draii ing-room of his uncle's quaint old Tudor house. She was dressed all in white. There were starry blossoms in her golden hair. Her eyes were shining with a shy, eager light. Mr. CheSton, with a smile on his face, pronounced the introduction. "Tom, let me present you to Lady May Renfrew, the niece of my good friend and neighbour, who has taken pity on his bachelor loneliness, and has come to keep house for. him. You young folks must help to make each other lively for as long as you can be persuaded to stay, Tom." Toni had met Mr. Renfrew that day out fishing. He and Mr. Cheston owned adjoining properties, and were friends of long standing. In the eveninc, they sat down to chess as. a natural thing, and the young people strayed out into the soft shadows of the garden, where the dying daylight and the summer moonlight made a witchery of conflicting lights and shadows. They talked as men and maidens have talked since the world began its murse, and walked as in a Garden of Eden of their own. No word of love passed Tom's lips, for,there came between him and this radiant girl a dim floating vision of a baby face, and of a troth plight made long years ago, which had almost been forgotten. He had sometimes thought of it as the _years fled by. Perhaps the memory had helped to keep him from those thoughts of love and marriage which come so easily to some, for Tom had never had a love affair in his life as yet. When Lady May was gone, he stood leaning over the terrace-wall, gazing towards the moon-lit sea, and he started when his uncle (to whom he had taken a great liking) came and laid a kindly hand upon his shoulder. "If you could only learn to love each other it would make two old men very happy, my boy. You are my heir. She is my old friend's heiress. He has adopted her; she is like the apple of his eye. Her people are of the cold, proud sort. She was never happy at home; here she is the life and sunshine cf the place. Ah! Tom, she would make you a sweet wife!"

For one week Toin and May were constantly thrown into each other's company. The days seemed to speed as on wings. They walked in that glamour of love's young dream which transforms the world and annihilates time, and makes the hours fly or drag according as they are spent in company or apart. And still no word of love passed Tom's lips, though there was that in his dark, deep-set eyes that told its own tale. Then suddenly he left, said he must go, but would return. when August had come; and Lady May bid him adieu with shining eyes in which the tear-drops glistened.

Was he bound in honour to that child's promise, or was he not? This was the thought which never left Tom by night or by day as he plunged into the vortex of his professonal work after the brief recess.

Every incident of that childish "wedding" was clear to him now; ; and again and again he sought to laugh the notion to scorn, whilst the voice of conscience said in his hearA: "You were old enough to know something of the nature of azi, oath. You made your promise with your whole understanding and mind. How do you know that she is not waiting for youi still? Child as she was, she may remember, too. Until you have seen her again and. have released yourself from that solemn pledge, you should not think of yourself as absolutely free. It may have been nothing but childish vow but a vow it was, and a nliehted word is not to be lightly broken. BY ILUG 11 LAW .

A great change has Hissel or 17.1-pguish thought and feeling with regard to the Near Easteran Question within the past twenty-live years. It seems now almost inemnprellensible. that popular clau.our should have raged violently against all, the Prince Con:,ort not excepted, who were even suspected of a desire to avoid the Crimean War: or that twenty- five years later, notwithstanding the Bulgarian atrocities, a large part of the nation sl7ould have enthusiastically supported Lord Beacons- field in threatening Russia with war failing her abandonment of the greater part of the fruits of eller dearly-bought victories. Few voices are now raised in outsfu ken advocacy of Turkish rule; and the atrocious maxim that English interests require the strengthening of the Turkish Empire at what-- ever cost to its subject Christian popula- tions is now seldom heard, at any rate from official lips.

There has, however, succeeded a very general feeling of indifference towards the whole subject. "What are the Macedonians to us, or we to the Macedonians ? " seems now to express- the attitude of the majority alike of politicians and people. Had this attitude been consistently maintained in the past it might have been no great matter. Twice over, within a space of twenty-five years. the Christian subjects of the Porte would have been freed had England merely remained neutral; and nothing we can say can now obliterate the fact that if Hilmi Pasha-and his troops, if the whole monstrous broort of tax- farmers and zapties and Bashi-Bazouks who make Turkish rule everywhere stink in the nostrils of the world, are in Macedonia to- day. it is primarily to .England that that result is due. ,In 1854 the destruction of the Turkish fleet at Sinope, and the evident superiority of the Russian armies, menaced the Turkish Empire with extinction. "Let us never forget." wrote the late Duke of Argyll, last survivor of the Cabinet which yawed the Crimean War, "that it was we— t- the British Government and people—who rescued Turkey .from . this immediate danger and this otherwise inevitable fate." "The Treaty of Paris," he continues, "which terminated the war of 1856, was a concentrated expression of the whole policy on which that war had been undertaken. It made us foremost as a -nation in a joint responsibility—by irre- vocable deeds and by definite transactions—for the very existence of the Turkish Government as a Power even pretending to independence." Nor were these transactions entered into under misapprehensions as to the nature of the Government which was thus given a new lease of life and power over the Christians of the East.

General Sir Fenck Williams. that gallant soldier whose wonderful defence of Kars rolled back the 'tide of Russian conquest in Asiatic Turkey, in the course- of his despatches, which were laid before the Cabinet, dencurced the whole machinery of Turkish government as "an engine of tyranny perhaps unequalled in the world," described the Turkish police as men the "infamy of whose life and character no language can portray," and described himself as "convinced that the Allies-who have fought and,bled to keep the Russians out of these fertile countries could not allow this triumph to be a barren one to the unhappy and oppressed Christian, nor to his fellow-subject, the Mohammedan cultivator.", Unhappily the Allies, with England at their head, did allow their victories to be barren in this respect. Reforms were indeed mooted, and set out in the Treaty of Paris with all the flourishes of the diplomatic draughtsman ; but the execu- tion of them was left to the Turks. The inevitable result followed. Turkish govern- ment proceeded along its accustomed paths, the Powers made the usual diplomatic protests whengver from time to time something more than usually scandalous occurred, and the subjects of the Porte suffered all manner of oppression and outrage as before. The `respite of twenty years which the Wood of French and English soldiers won. for ,Turkey was absolutely barren of reform. At last more than usually atrocious abomina- tions in Bosnia precipitated the Russo-Turkish War of 1878. The Turks were utterly beaten, and the Treaty of San Stefano was dictated under the very walls of Constantinople. Again the Turkish Empire lay in ruins, and again England set herself to rebuild it. She did not, it is true, declare war once more in its defence, but her Ministry threatened hostilities aaainst Russia, and the majority of the nation were undoubtedly at their backs. The' well- known lines, which have enriched the language , with a new word : We don't want to fight, but by jingo if we do, We've got the men, we've got the ships, we've got the,money, too, and which are part of a popular music-hall • song of that time, expressed the feeling of the man in the _street.

Russia. after the immense expenditure in blood and tieasure which the campaign just concluded had cost her, was in no condition to face a new Crimean War. British diplomacy triumphed. and the TreEty of San Stefano was submitted to the consideration of the Congress of Berlin. Into that Congress the British representatives went, as is now well known, bound hand and foot to Turkey by secret agreements. Not, indeed, that the policy of England was ever in doubt. Just as Lord Palmerston a quarter of a century before had Faid, "We support Turkey for our own sake and for our own interests," so now Lord Beaconsfield roundly declared, amidst the enthusiastic applause of Englishmen, that "everyone ought to have known that it was the policy of England to strengthen Turkey as much as possible." It is true that the Treaty of Berlin, substituted by the Congress for the Treaty of San Stefano, is a very different document from the earlier Treaty of Paris. The Turkish Empire in Europe was not restored in its integrity, for a new free principality was permitted to he established in Bulgaria, whilst in Eastern Roumelia the direct rule of the Sultan ceased. But what British diplomacy could do to rehabilitate Turkey it did, and the results were considered sufficient to secure the British envoys a magnificent reception on their return to England. it is quite beside the point to inquire whether the policy pursued by English states- men in 185 A and in 1878 was justifiable or not. It does not matter in the very least what view we take as to this. But it must be abundantly clear that these transactions and the policy upon which they were founded did inevitably create an immense national responsibility for the welfare of subject populations living in all and every part of the dominions of the empire which was thus, twice over saved and set up again by British intervention. But one particular feature of the Troaty of Berlin requires special notice. The Congress restored to Turkey the larger part of those very provinces of Macedonia which have been the scene of recent disturbances and massacres,

Soaking does half the wash, with Fels- Naptha. Half rubbing. No boiling. Half damage to clothes. Go by the book.

roaNaPtlin 39 Wilson street London E 0

and which had won their independence, anci would, by the Treaty of San Stefano, havP formed part of a greater Bulgaria. Surely this is a fact of tremendous import-. ance ! But for the Congress, in which England took the leading part, there would not to-day be a single Turkish soldier in those, the most considerable, potions of Macedonia. The murders, the torturings, the rapes which are ordinary incidents of Turkish rule over sub- ject populations—these horrors (and not those only committed since the insurrection, but those also which, since 1878, have been prepar- ing the way for insurrection)—these lie at our door!

We cannot evade responsibility for our past deeds. For twenty,five years the people of Macedonia have suffered for our action at Berlin, as for the twenty-five years before they had suffered for our action in the Crimea. If ever one people owed a debt of honour to another, the people of England owe it to the people of Macedonia..

- At the present moment an 'opportunity seems, in God's providence, to be extended to England to retrieve the blunders of the past with regard to these unhappy people. Surely she will recognise her responsibilities towards them, and will not be satisfied with paper reforms, the execution of which is committed to the Turk in the comfortable certainty that they never will, and never can, be carried out by him. All the Macedonian peasant asks is the elements of a tolerable government security for life and property, and the honour of I his womenkind. Surely that at least we owe him whom we have so long treated as a pawn in the game of international interests. Over and above the national responsibility there are, of course, those proper to us all individually. In both respects our honour is surely . engaged to protect fellow-Christians who to-day, as' for generation after generation, suffer persecution for the faith, whose churches are -defiled, and whose children are forcibly converted to Islam. Nor can we individually feel without responsibility towards men and women and little ones who are now suffering the very extremes of cold, and hunger, and nakedness as they huddle together in the hills, or under poor roofs of hasty thatch among the blackened walls of their homes—people, too, with whom we are united by the triple cord of humanity, national obligation, and common faith.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

" HAMLET " TERCENTENARY.

Next year will be the three hundredth anni- .4,ersary of the first appearance of Shake- speare's " Hamlet " in print as the author left it finally to the world, which has since then read it so widely. In reality it made its -first appearance in 1602, and was followed by a quarto edition the next year; but " Hamlet," as we know it, dates from. 1604. Therefore, it has been decided to celebrate the -tercentenary of this great work next year, and, •though Hamlet announced "something rotten .in the state of Denmark," that forgiving country promises to play a leading part in the celebration. In honour of the event it has been decided to erect a memorial of the immortal William Shakespeare at Elsinore; and this may be regarded as a whole-hearted tribute of the Danish people to the greatest figur- Eniziish literature. It has received

A L1*.:.2! AP' 4.,L, • sT±:,

KRONBERG CASTLE AND THE RAMPARTS ON WHICH HAMLET SAW HIS FATHER'S GHOST.

the warmest support on all sides from Briton, Dane, and :‘ merican alike; and Queen Alex- andra, our link with Denmark, has encour- aged the scheme in every way, amongst other things subscribing a hundred pounds out of her own privy purse. The memorial is to take the shape of a statue of Shakespeare by .the Danish sculptor, Louis Hasselriis, of .'Rome, and the bard will be tepresented in -stone in a sitting posture with a book across his knee and a pen in his hand. Elsinore. where the statue will be erected, is full of literary associations, as the castle of Kron- berg is always supposed to be Elsinore. On its ramparts it was that the ghost appeared to Hamlet, and within sight of it there are to be found two memorials as immortal as the play itself. One is Hamlet's grave, an old 'pile of stones with a granite slab on top bear- ing the inscription. The other is the spot -where tradition says Ophelia was drowned. .A little brook, however, is all that is left, of 'the stream, but the tree still stands. The ceremony next year at Elsinore will be (51 'great literary interest, and is certain to at -.tract a great gathering of literary celebrities.

THE NEW MEMBER FOR MID-DEVON.

The new member for Mid-Devon, Mr. Henry Trelawney Eve, K.C., has resided in the con- stituency for a considerable time, and ac- quired various properties there. He, in fact, owns nearly 3,000 acres of land, and when in Devonshire, for which county he has only recently been ,placed in the commission of •the peace, lives atßoveyTracey, where, as a re- creation fro his work in court • and chambers, he employs him- self in farming 'extensively. He is still well un- der fifty years of age. The only son of a Jamaica mer- chant, he was -educated pr i- 'yately and at 'Exeter College, ••Oxford, and was called to the 'Bar at Lincoln's in iBBi, -soon obtaining a good practice at the pan cery Bar. He took silk in 1895, an was elected a Bencher of his Inn four years later tHe is now the leading .counsel in Mr. Justicc Swinfen Eady's court. He is also connect& with the south-western county by ties of mar riage, for he espoused, some twenty-five years since, the only daughter of Dr. Strangwav llounsell, of Torquay. When he is in towi- 'Mr. Eve resides at Hermiston, Middle Lane Hornsey, and his chambers are in New Square Lincoln's Inn.

H. T. EVE, M.P.

DANGERS OF THE STOKEHOLD.

The explosion of a 'boiler of the third- tlass cruiser, Walleroo, recalls once more how terrible are the daily sufferings and risks of the marine stoker. No one .who has been down in the stoke-hole of a cruiser can fail to appreciate the hardships endured by tilt., in who work during the long hours to feed the huge furnaces with the gaping mouths -bellowing smoke and _flame. Go hand over hand, gingerly planting every step, down the narrow, slippery iron ladder which is the only entrance to the raging inferno, a dozen feet below the engines, and see the brave fellows working there, girded at the waist and with their chests and arms bare. Inhale for a few moments, if you are able, the thick haze of smoke and coal dust, which seems to fill your very lungs' till breathing is impossible, and which adds intensity to a heat already nigh •to suffocating. Wriat chance have the men of their lives if the steam envelops them ? They are penned up in their little hades like rats in a trap, and if a boiler bun•ts they will be scalded tc;

-death long before there is time to climb the narrow stair to the engine-room. Yet they never think of danger, these men working :away in the heat. " Fancy doing,your work," says Mr. Rudyard Kipling in " A Fleet In being," "with an eight-inch steam- pipe Jig the nape of your neck, an' a dynamo buzzin' -up your back, an' the whole blessed shoct wizzin round in the pit of your stomach ! Then. we jump about and curse if they duf t give us enough steam. I swear 1 think their no end gdod men in tile engine-room." For -their own part, the men take no risks. as -they know what is the inevitable punish- ment of it. Their must be no dilly-dallying about orders, no careless gauging, no pro- arastination. It is no use thinking matters may be remedied. afterwardi. • There is no afterwards for the poor fell ,w who has beeu rash or guilty enough to neglect orders.

THE LATEST 111 MOTORS.

It seems rather paradoxical at first sight to construct a motor forge for the purpose of shoeing horses and repairing sadlery, but this is the latest addition to the equipment of the United States Army. Indeed- in these

S. MOTOR FORGE

days the motor is growing omnipotent, and we may 3hortly expact to have nntor any- things and everyth;ngs ! The motor-field forge is furnished with horseshoes and a complete blacksmith's outfit, in addition to carpentering . and sadlery requisites. and an auxiliary

engino on one side of the machinery works lathe, grindstone, and dynamo. It is con- structed of steel to resist shell and bullet, and is twelve feet long. It has a four cylinder 24h.p. engine, which propels the motor at the rate of ten miles an hour on its lowest speed, and there- is sufficient storage in the gasoline tank to last for three hundred and ninety miles. The wheels are of wood, strongly built on the "artillery" lines, and the tyres, which are three inches in width, are of solid rubber, as nothing pneumatic would be of much value considering the ground this field-forge would have to tra.verse on active service. It is naturally most stflongly and solidly built, and weighs 6,500 lbs. bare, while the equipment adds 'another 4,000 lbs. to the gross weight of the maChine in transit. It has been constructed in New 'iork, and, after it has been sent to Wash- ington for inspection, it will be despatched to fort Kansas for testing thoroughly with a view to use in war.

Mademoiselle has performed the feat in Berlin and Paris, and, after she has finished her engagement at the Palace, she means to perform it throughout the rest of the civilised world. Mounted on a bicycle, the girl slides down a steep shoot, some seventy feet long, like a flash, and then up a short incline. Then there- is a gap of forty- six feet wide in the track. A GLOBULAR BATTLESHIP.

A Mr. Stokes, of New York, has recently patented what he terms a "Globular Naval Battery," which he claims to be practically impregnable. This novel war craft is very much in the shape of an inverted bowl. This shape. while giving a maximum of buoyancy, and sc. of armour-carrying capacity, is, how- ever, destructive of mobility,, and it may be doubted whether her four propellers, placed equidistantly round 'the lower portion of her hull, would do more than turn her in the direction in which she wished to fire. For when we remember that the famous Russian circular ironclads, the Admiral Popoff and Novgorod, mere saucers of less than 4,000 tons displacement, required four and six pro- pellers respectively to give them a speed of six knots, all their propellers being placed close together on one side, it seems hardly possible that the globular battery, with her intended displacement of 11,337 tons, would

Like other ships she is unprotected below the belt, while an ordinary battleship would find little difficulty. with her immensely superior speed, in getting round outside the line of fire of the two heavy guns, and knocking a hole in her with the ram. The Examiner, Saturday, January 16, 1904.

THE CASE OF CANADA.

A FEW FACTS ABOUT TRADE.

IS PREFERENCE PROFITABLE OR

NECESSARY?

The Test Case.

- --- Ma. CHAMBERLAIN has based his whole case for giving a Preference to the Colonies upon the relations between this country and Canada. Canada reduced the duties upon British goods, and the result was that Germany ceased to give Canada the most-favoured:nation treat- ment. This took place in 1897. Suddenly, in 1903, Mr. Chamberlain declared this was an intolerable grievance, which we ought not to take "lying down." Not only does he now want us to attack Germany in return, but he wants us to go to war with tho trade of the whole world, and to tax our food and goods at the same time, in order to support Colonial -trade. Even supposing (1) all Mr. Cham- berlain said of Germany were true; even sup- posing (2) Mr. Chamberlain had taken up the case of Canada in 1897 instead of waiting until 1903; even supposing (3) Germany had treated Canada unjustly; even supposing (4) Canadian trade were more valuable to us than German trade—would all these things heaped together be a sufficient reason for taxing ourselves and going to war with the trade not only of Germany but of the whole world in' order to benefit Canadian traders ? Surely not. But let us take Mr. Chamberlain's pet case of Canada. First of all, Mr. Chamber- lain's sudden animosity towards Germany is in strange contrast with the attitude of the Government (of which he was the chief) in collecting German "debts in Venezuela and in supporting a German railway at Bagdad. Secondly, Mr. Chamberlain never even mentioned 'the case of Germany v. Canada until six years afterwards. Then he saw it would be useful as a part of his campaign, in stirring up ill-feeling against all foreign trades. Thirdly, Germany only treated Canada in the same way that Canada had treated Germany. Canada refused to give Germany most-favoured-nation treatment, and Germany replied by doing ditto. Fourthly, while Germany took from us 233,094,565 worth of goods last year, Canada only took £8,832,000. Thus Mr. Chamberlain's test case breaks down on all 'points.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
More Trade Figures.

THE Canadian Government Fits recently issued a Blue-book, giving the figures of its trade up to the end of their financial year, which ended with June, 1903. These figures enable us to see whore the bulk of Canadian trade comes from and goes to. The figures are. given in millions of dollars:

Canada's ' Canada's Country. Imports from. , Exports to. ' United States ... 138 ... 72 United Kingdom ... 59 ... 131 12 ... 2

Germany ...

France - • 7 Thus we see that, in spite. of the fact that Canada gives a preference to Bfitish baoods,she takes more than twice the quantity from the United States. The fact is, the preiarence is little more than a sham, as goods from tile United States are able to enter Canada upon better terms than British goods. The next point is that it is altogether unnecessary for us to tax our food to give Canada a prefer- ence in the British market, because we already take more Canadian goods than any other country, and we offer the most open market for her produce. if Canada wishes to increase her trade with the British people the remedy is very simple, and it is in the hands of the Canadian people to apply the remedy.. Let them take more of their goods from the Mother Country, and then they can export more of their produce to pay for them. As what Mr. Chamberlain calls the "balance of trade" with Canada is against us it is im- possible for us to take more from Canada until Canada takes more from us.

_ REMEMBERINCI that Canada granted a preference to British goods in 1897, it is very interesting to note the growth of exports to Canada from all countries from that date. Here are the figures since 1896. The figures are given in millions of dollars:

Just compare the increased trade between Canada and other countries from 1896 to 1903:

It is not surprising that even Mr. Chamberlain should have told the Colonial Premiers how very disappointing were the results of Prefer- ence. Until Canada and other Colonies are willing to let in British goods so as to compete an equal terms with their own manufactures all this talk of preference is nonsense.

Due from the Colonies. AT a time when Mr. Chamberlain has boon talking about the " offer " made. by the Colonies, and telling us of the dreadful things which will happen if we do not accept that offer, it is just as well that we shall remember before we tax ourselves for the sake of the Colonies that they owe us something which they have never yet paid. Before Mr. Chamberlain enter6d upon his preflent Pro- tectionist campaign, he had the courage to tell the Colonies they had something to make up to us before we could commence to do any- thing further for them. At the last Colonial Conference he told the Colonial Premiers: In 1897 I would remind yoti that the Premiers then unanimously undertook to consult with their collemues, and to consider whether a preference Alight not be given on their customs tariff for goods imported from the United King- cio:n. This was a proposal without any reciprocal obligation. It was regarded by the Premiers at the time as a proposal that might be made in con- sideration of the fact that the United Kingdom was the largest and the best and the most open market in the. world for all the products of the Colonies.

janada partially adopted this idea, but, as we lave seen, the preference given by Canada has lot been effective. Australia and New Zealand lave refused to lower their tariffs. And yet n the face of this Mr. Chamberlain has had he audacity to ask the British nation to tax heir food for the sake of the Colonial ;rowers.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

NORTHERN UNION COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP.

LANCASHIRE v. CUMBERLAND.

The Inter-county Championship engagement between Lancashire and Cumberland, played at Central Park, Wigan, on NVecinesday,!only attracted a moderate attend- ance, some 5,000 persons being present. Owing to the inclement weather the ground was in a wretched con- dition, and this in a large measure spoiled the game. In the earlk stages Cumberland were furnished with an abundance of defensive work, and the game was only a few minutes old when Phil Thomas gave Fish a smart pass, which the Warrington man made full use of, scampering over the line and running behind the posts. Fish took the kick, but moved the earth more than he did the leather. Fish, however, was soonagain prominent, as he looked like getting through when he was pushed into touch. Jackson, the visiting half, was responsible for the removal of the operations to the other end of the field, where Lancashire were penalised for an infringement in the pack, and Timmony essayed to lower the Lancastrians' goal, a feat he did not accomplish. Lomas and Baxter relieved, a clever piece of play ending near the Cumberland goal line where the visiting representatives were in turn penalised, Lomas landing a goal. After a spell of midfield play, Fish gained possession, and was racing away in rare style when he came into violent contact with Crane, with the result that both men were badly damaged, and took no further part in the game. Frater then went into the Lancashire three-quarter line. At half-time Lanoashire led by five points to nil. Immediately on the resumption, the leather was sent from the pack to Hogg, who bounded over the line, to enable Lomas to add another goal. Penned in their own twenty-five, Cumberland found themselves acting on the defensive for a considerable period. Time after time the leather went along the Lancashire three- quarters to Prater who, however, was either caught or pushed into touch ere he could reach the line. The hostilities continued in this fashion during the greater portion of the half, the Lancastrians missing many opportunities of enhancing their position. Ultimately Phil Thomas was given a quick pass,and overcoming all opposition he giounded behind the uprights, Lonia,s again piloting the leather over the bar. It was not until the closing stages that Cumberland were enabled in any way to menace the home county line, but the effort came too late, and they were decisively beaten. Result :--

Lancashire Cumberland

===== 0002601_19040116_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST DIVISION.

WARRINGTON v. HULL.

After a fortnight's idleness Warrington were at home on Saturday to Hull, whom they beat by a handsome margin. Both teams were practically at full strength and a crowd of over 4,000 assembled. The ground was in excellent order though a trifle soft. Mr. Edmondson of Bradford was referee, and the teams lined out as follows :

Warrington—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son, and Hockenhall, three-quarter backs ; Davies and Bate, half backs; Mousson, Boardman, Swift, Jolley, Thomas, Cook, Naylor, and Edmundson, forwards. Hull—Taylor, back • Parry, Evans, Brown, and Car- viii, three-quarter backs;i Thompson and Goddard, half backs ; .1, Lewis, Harrison, Hardaker, Rhodes, Har- greaves, Spenceley, Small, and Ellerington, forwards. Harrison kicked off against the wind. Davies replied well, and the first scrimmage fell well in the Hall section of the field. A passing bout between Hockenhall and Thomas almost led to a score the first minute, but Taylor succeeded in grassing the latter. Fish was almost in the next minute, Taylor bowling him in touch, close to the corner. Hull were very hard pressed, and had all their work cut out to prevent a score. The Yorkshire forwards were well beaten, and could not get out of their own quarter line. A mark by Davies was taken by Fish, but the ball'fell short,and D ivies was hurt in try- ing to drop a goal. The first time Hull crossed the half-way line was by a good run by Harrison, but Hoclaenhall dribbled, and Fish had an ineffectual drop at goal. A peculiar incident now occurred. Hull were penalised, and Fish took the free kick. Instead of kicking for goal he touched with his foot and than picking up ran over the line and grounded the ball. Amid loud hooting by the crowd the referee disallowed the try, and ordered a scrimmage instead. A few minutes later Dickenson transferred to Isherwood, and the latter passing the ball on to Fish he rounded Taylor and scored a capital try and kicked a goal. Scarcely had the game been restarted when Fish almost scored again after a splendid run. Warrington were having all the game, and Hull scarcely had a look in. They, however, played five three-quarters, and this prevented Warring- ton from extending themselves. Near the interval Hull made a sprint, and got to the quarter-line. They, how- ever, failed to do anything, and when the whistle blew for half-time Warrington were pressing again. Half- time :—Warsington 5 points, Hull nil. Dickenson re-started, and Hull at once pressed. Goddard by a splendid dribble covered half the length of the field, and put his side in a much more favourable position. Flockenhall replied by an equally creditable dribble. Play now hovered at centre until Goddard by a dashing run collared )Fish close to the line. Warrington had an anxious time and a splen- did round of passing between Hockenhall, Dicken- son, and Hallam covered a large portion of the field. Hallam was hurt when tackled, and was off the field for some time,Davies going back. Warrington, however, still had the best of matters, though a man short, and presently Hallam returned. When it seemed odds on no further score, a sensational try came. Isher- wood got the ball from Davies and broke through the opposing quarters. When challenged by Taylor, he passed to Fish, and the latter outpaced all opposition and scored a try and kicked a goal Just on time Davies scored a try after a forward dribble, and Fish kicked a goal. The final whistle blew without restarting, War- rington winning by 3 goals 2 tries (15 points), Hull, nil.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
BROUGHTON RANGERS v. RUNCORN.

This fixture took place at Broughton, before 7,000 people. Some tall punting characterised the early moments of the game, and subsequently play went in favour of Broughton, for whom Wilson scored from a pass by Hogg. NV. James failed at the place. Follow- ing the drop out, Wilkinson, the Runcorn full back, was called upon to clear, but missing the ball, Harry rushed up, dribbled over the line; and scored. W. James again failed at goal. From the scrummage, Sam Jones neatly dropped a goal, while from a penalty Farmer landed a goal for Runcorn. Half-time score—Broughton Rangers 1 goal and 2 tries (8 points), Runcorn 1 goal (2 points). On resuming the game still favoured the Rangers From a long kick by W. James, Hogg got possession and scored an unimproved try. Subsequently Runcorn dribbled over the home line, and in the scramble Walker was credited with a try, Farmer failing at goal. After- wards the Rangers almost pressed continuously, and further tries were scored by Hogg, Harry and Harris, but W. James failed to add the goal points. Result —Broughton Rangers 1 goal and 6 tries (20 points), Runcorn l goal and I try (5 points). Teams : Broughton Rangers :—Fielding, back: Hogg, Wilson, Harry, and Harris, three-quarter backs; S. James and W. James, half backs; Trotter, Scott, Oram, Beetham, Whitehead, Winskill, Thompson, and Ruddick, forwards. Runcorn :—Wilkinson, back; Flanagan, Richardson, Price, and Jones, three-quarter backs; Jolley and S. Walker, half backs: Farmer, J. Walker, A. Richardson, Washburn, Williams, Dixon, Messenger, and Tomlinson, forwards.

Referee, Mr. J. H. Smith, Widnes. Touch judges, Messrs B. Ashton, Oldham, and J. H. Houghton, St Helens.

WIDNES vt WIGAN.

At Widnes, before 3,000 spectators. Widnes in the opening stages had the best of matters, and Tilley made two attempts at goal, both of which were unsuccessful. Leytham then got away, but near the quarter flag he was pushed into touch. A further penalty to Widnes enabled Tilley to place a goal. Wigan then invaded the home territory, but a long kick by Hughes put his side on the attack again, and Mason, the visitors, full back, had an anxious time of it. At half-time Widnes led by 2 points to nil. Webster opened the second half for Widnes. Shar- rock mulled badly, and almost let in Peacock. After a good run by Leytham, Hughes had an opportunity, but lost the ball. When the play was at centre, Aspey kicked badly, with the result that Leytham was given a chance, and running strongly along the touch line, he beat Tilley and Hughes, and scored at the corner. The same player failed at the goal kick. Result :—Wigan 1 try (3 points), Widnes 1 goal (2 points). Teams:— Widnes.—Tilley, back; Wailing, G. Aspey, Peacocke and Hughes, threequarter backs; Lally and Douglas, half backs; S. Aspey, Lynch, Leyshon, Cockram, Webster, McDonald, McCabe and Rigby, forwards. Wigan.--Mason. back; Leytham, Harris,. Rees, and Sharrock, three-quarter backs; Davies and Anderson, half backs; Collier, Brown, Barton, Whittaker, Ellison, Welfenden, Vickers, and Ball, forwards. Referee, Mr. J. Priestley (Salford). Touch judges, Messrs.F. Little (St. Helens), and T. H. Robinson, (Runcorn).

===== 0002601_19040116_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
KEIGHLEY v. SALFORD.

At Keighley, before 3,000 spectators. The visitors played down. hill, but made little impression till a penalty gave Lomas an easy goal. Keighley lost Howker injured, and R. Pearson also. After changing ends, Iseu Myers equalised with a grand drop, and, al- though so severely handicapped, Keighley showed superior play, only just missing victory. Result— Keighley, 1 goal (2 points) ; Salford 1 goal (2 points).

===== 0002601_19040116_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
'HUNSLET V. LEEDS.

Played at Hunslet. The game opened in the Leeds half, but eventually, after a fine passing bout, Evans scored. Littlewood missed the goal. Before this, however, he kicked a penalty goal. Goldthorp also landed a penalty goal. Half-time—Leeds, 5 points ; Hunslet, 4 goal (2 points). Afterwards Grace scored from a bad mistake by Hannah. Littlewood landed a goal for Hunslet. The home side scored twice, goal kicks failing. Result—Leeds, 2 goals 2 tries (10 points) ; Hunslet, 1 goal 2 tries (8 points).

===== 0002601_19040116_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
BATLEY v. LEIGH.

At Batley, Leigh immediately attacked, and Bennett scored, Johnson converting. The same player shortly after kicked a penalty goal. On change of ends Leigh still kept up the attack, and Arrowsn,lith and Bennett each scored tries, Johnson failing at goal in both cases. Hodgson for Batley scored, Wattle Davies converting. Result—Leigh, 12 points; Batley, 5 points.

SWINTON v. BRADFORD.

Played at Swinton. A splendidly-contested game ended in a deserved win for Swinton. Bradford opened the scoring through Breer, the goal kick failing. Lewis then scored for Swinton, and Preston kicked a goal. This performance was repeated by the same players in the second half of the game. Result—Swinton, 10 points; Bradford, 3 points.

HULL KINGSTON ROVERS v. HUDDERSFIELD.

At Hull, before 4,000 spectators. The Rovers had much the better of play in the first half, and Gordon and Phipps scored after clever movements. Carmichael kicked a goal, and half-time found the Rovers leading; by 8 points to nil. The home team continued to out- play the visitors in .the second gall, and Kemp added two tries. Result—Rovers, 2 goals 4 tries (16 points) ; Huddersfield, nil.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
OLDHAM v. HALIFAX.

Played at Oldham. The first half was fairly even,and there was no score. Early in the second half Ferguson dropped a fine goal from a centre, and Dixon and Tyson added two more dropped goals. The Halifax defence was good, and but for this Oldham would have scored much more after the interval. Result—Oldham, 6 points; Halifax, nil.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
INTER-WORKS LEAGUE.

ST. HELENS CABLE CO. V. CROSFIELD'S.—On the ground of the latter and resulted in a win for the Cable Co. by 14 points to nil. LONDON AND 'NORTH-WESTERN RAILWAY V.RYLANDS Bnos.—These teams met for the third time in the above competition, on the ground of the former. Previous matches have resulted in victories for Rylands, but on this occasion the railway men whipped together for once their strongest team, with the result.that the wire men received a check to their onward career. The visitors winning the toss, the home team kicked off, but for some informality a scrimmage was ordered on the centre line, where from the pack Timmins secured, and passing out to Lloyd. the latter cleared his men, and at the opportune moment transferred to Jackson, who raced away and scored_ a grand try, Short failing at the place kick. This sensa- tional start put the- visitors on their mettle, and they quickly made tracks for the home line, but the defence was sound. The game was fast and exciting, neither side claiming much advantage and at the intes- val no further score was recorded. From the restart the game travelled from end to end, Rylands were the better forward and as a consequence Floyd who played a neat game gave his backs plenty of opportunities, but any attempt at combination was effectively bottled up by Hewitt who was playing a powerful game at centre. Jackson and Derbyshire each crossed the line, for the sailway, as also did Morris for Rylands but for alleged infringements the points were disallowed and eventu- ally an interesting game ended L. and N.-NV. R. 1 try (3 points), Rylands Eros. nil. •

INCOMPLETE MATCHES.

It was reported that the Leeds and Broughton Rangers' match was stopped after 73 minutes' play. The committee decided that the match should hold •good, while a similar decision was given in connection with the Oldham and Batley match, in which 75 minutes were played. MEETING OF GENERAL COMMITTEE.

A RULING.

Question: The attacking side is allowed a free kick three yards from their opponents' goal line, and near touch line, and the kicker retires 25 yards for the kick. He kicks diagonally across the field in the direction of his opponents' goal, but the ball does not travel 25 yards forward parallel with touch line, and consequently does not" reach the line of the mark," and the attaok- ing side follow up and carry the ball and ground it over the line, the player grounding the ball being on- side. Should a try be allowed or the ball brought back and scrimmaged? Answer : The ball shall be scrum- maged, as per Rule 10. ENGLAND v. OTHER NATIONALITIES. NEW DATE FIXED.

This 12-aside fixture, which had perforce to be post= poned on January 6th owing to the Oldham ground being considered unfit to play upon, was arranged by the committee to take place on Monday, February Bth, at the same venue. The same teams will do duty, with the exception ,that Dechan (Scotland), left wing three- quarter, on the Other Nationalities side, will play instead of NV. Evans (Wales). The teams are as follows :

A NEW RUGBY CHALLANGE CUP.

The final match for the new challenge cup, presented by Mr. A. F. Stephenson, J.P., of the "Oldham Standard," was recently played between Heyside and the Rochdale Rangers, and resulted, after a keenly con- tested game, in a victory for Heyside. The Cup, which was manufactured by J. V. Benson, Ltd., of Ludgate Hill, London, is a triumph of taste and skilful work- manship. It is wrought in silver, and stands, with its ebony plinth, 27 inches high, is urn-like in shape, and surmounted by the figure of a footballer in the act of "passing." The base is surrounded by a beautiful floral and leaf design, and the front bears the inscrip- tion, "Oldham Standard Challenge Cup," withatre monogram of the donor. In every respect it is a barna- some trophy wpll calculated to fulfil its purpose -of encouraging Rugby football in Oldham and district. Besides the cup the winners were each presented with a solid gold medal of neat and effective design, while the "runners up" vere awarded silver medals with gold centres. The medals, like the Cup, are of Benson's manufacture.
