LATCHFORD ANGLING SOCIETY.

Annual Meeting.

I The annual meeting of the above Society was held on Monday evening last at the headquarters of the Club, the Brook Inn, Latchford, Mr. Harry Taylor pre- siding over a full attendance. The annual report was given by the Chairman, who said that the Society was formed at the end of 1902 with the idea of getting Itogether a band of jovial spirits who would benefit largely in health by taking trips into the country on fishing excursions. That object had been attained. The preliminary arrangements completed, certain fishing privileges were next acquired. The opening picnic was undertaken to Stretton where a friendly game of bow 3mesulted. With the opening of the season for 1 co urse fish, fishing contests were arranged, the first tak- ing place in the Bridgewater canal the' match being a big success in every way. Two eiening contests were also fished in the same canal whilst the two special olub , matches were decided at Northwich, the drives there .and back being defrayed by the club. Taken all round I these club contests had proved very enjoyable though lin one of them, at Northwich all the prizes were not ' won. Several members had fished in various open com- petitions, bat not with any great success, whilst the 1 teams entered for the shield competition in connection with the Warrington A.A. hope for better success this year , than that which attended their efforts last season. They ; had also received several handsome presents for compe- -1 titions . and best thanks were due to their friends. I Thanks were also due for the good advice and instruc- • tions which has been given to all members by several experienced anglers who are members of .the Society. They might also congratulate themselves on the hand- some balances which have accrued to the funds of the Club from the dance and hall which were osganised and held in the Parish Room. The specimen fish caught by members during the past season compared very favourably with those caught by members of other societies in the neighbourhood. The season was concluded by a dinner given to all members out of the funds of the Society and the thanks of the Club were due to all members who had worked Iso willingly and energetically to bring the season's work to such a satisfactory and glorious close." The Secretary, Mr. Alf. Starkey, next read the balance sheet, the total income being £29 Os. 2d. and the balance in hand being Li 3s. sd. This-was thought to be highly satisfactory, and the balance sheet was adopted. The election of officers was as follows :—President, Mr. Harry Taylor; Vice-Presidents, Messrs. A. Holland (Northwich), T. H. Houghton, R. Gorton, Fred J. Emson, and J. Waywell ; committee: Messrs. W. W. Starkey, J. Allcock, T. Wright, T. Chorley, A. Maddock. H. Green, and T. Boardman; svcaetary, Mr. Alfred Starkey; treasurer, Mr. J. W. Starkey. The whole of the rules of the Club were revised and also a few addi- tions made. It was decided that all members shQuld become affiliated to the Warrington Angling Associa- tion, and also that the Club be limited to fifty members. Hearty votes of thanks were accorded to the Chairman the officials and committee. The first Club night of the year 1904 will be held on the last Monday in January.

A DREAM.

I dreamed one night that I was dead, And for a while did stay, Beside the form they gazed upon. Then went in tears away. The world went on justtis before. But things that used to be So loved and treasured in my life, Had lost their charm for me., Wealth and position mattered nought Nor fame had it been won, Only the feelings of my heart The good and evil ilone. Death did not seem the dreadful thing It does so oft appear, We know not how by death we gain % The dark unknown we fear. I saw the microscopic life On earth in sea and sky And thoughts which are impalpable Man's self that cannot die.

There was no seeming in that world To which we all must go, But unconcealed just as we are Do thoughts and feelings flow. The thoughts and feelings much indulge To which we now aspire There bring to us the harvest grown, Out of each strong desire. There rising from the shade of death The soul is born again, And blessed hope of happiness The pure in heart attain. Methought the orbs that shine in space So beautiful to see, Were worlds of vast evolving life That lives eternally. Through changing lives all creatures live Each life they something gain For in creation's onward march No life is lived in vain. So from the past may instinct rise Not from dead forms / ween But from a long experience Which in the past has been. I dreamed the universe was just And filled with love divine, For all things tend to happiness If true that dream of mine.

S. KENYON.

Warrington, January, 1904.

OUTBREAKTOF SMALLPDX.

At the meeting of the St. Helens Health Committee on Wednesday Dr. P. Drew Harris, medical officer, re- ported that a second case of small-pox had been dis- covered, and on the previous evening a woman had bees removed to hospital from Newton-road Parr.

THE EXAMINER.

!JANUARY 16th 1904.

THE issue raised by THE DUKE AND the 2orrespondence MR. CHAMBERLAIN between the Duke of

DEVONSHIRE andiVlr. CHAMBERLAIN iS simple enough. Which of these eminent persons do the Liberal Unionists desire to follow ? The DUKE affirms with perfect justice that the Fiscal question! overshadows Home Rule, and that it is impossible for the Liberal Unionist Association to serve its original purpose while it is fundamentally divided on the new policy in- itiated by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. In these' circum- stances the DUKE proposes to dissolve the Associa- tion quietly; but Mr. t.:HAMBERLAIN, confident that he has a majority of the members with him, is eager for a fight. He will summon a meeting of Liberal Unionists to decide between him and the DUKE on the issue of Tariff reform, and, having carried his point, he will reconstitute the Associa- tion as a branch of his League, and a deputy of his Commission. Naturally this does not suit the DUKE at all ; but it is difficult to understand what else he could have expected. The chances are that at the meeting which is to be called against his wishes he will be defeated ; but he will carry with him an Ilimportant minority. What does he intend to do Iwith it ? With. significant emphasis he tells Mr. CHAMBERLAIN that they are now divided by a controversy as important as that which forced them to separate themselves from Mr. GLADSTONE in 1886. Mr. CHAMBRRLAIN has declared for Pro- tection. With this action the Liberal-Unionists who remain faithful to Free Trade can make no compromise. They can scarcely suppose that the best way for them to serve the cause of Free Trade is to set up a separate Association in competition with Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S, instead of.frankly rang- ing themselves with the Opposition. As we have maintained all along, this is the only logical course left open to the DUKE. He cannot with any real advantage to himself or the Free Trade cause plant his following in an isolated position, and say to the country: " We are the only true Liberal- Unionist Association ; the other is made spurious Iby Tariff reform. We do not commit ourselves to common action with the Opposition; and we I should like to support Mr. BALFOUR if we could I feel sure that he is not a notectionist, but only a mild Retaliationist." This breaking-up qf the Unionist party causes much wringing of hands among politicans whu persist in believing that it might have been averted. "If only Mr. BALFOUR and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN had gone about this Fiscal business in a different way," they cry, "all this mis- chief might never have happened !" "The whole movement," .says the " Standard," "has been vitiated by the manner in which it was con- ceived. ' A calm, dispassionate appeal from the Government of the day for a deliberate recon- sideration of our system of taxation would have been received with tespect by all reasonable men." If only Mr. CHAMBERLAIN had been content with Mr. BALFOUR'S calm and dispassionate pamphlet, we should all have settled down to an amicable discussion of the question whether Free Trade should be given up. It would have been found that opinion, on the whole inclined to the preser- vation of the present system; and then Mr. BAL. F.HIR would gracefully withdrawn his pamphlet and everything would have gone on as before. Unfortunately, human nature, especially the human nature of political parties, is not framed for these tranquil exercises. But there is no limit to the power of illusion. The " Times " suggests that, when the Liberal-Unionist Associa- tion is captured by the Tariff reformers, "the catholicity of the party should be indicated by the choice of a president such as Lord LANSDOWNE, who is not committed to unqualified acceptance of any particular set of Fiscal proposals." The notion of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN accepting a president who is not of his way of thinking is amusing; but it yields the palm of mirth to the suggestion ' that Lord LANSDOWNE, as a member of the present Cabinet, should be regarded as a calm and dispassionate arbiter of Fiscal reform. To put it bluntly, the whole of the Cabinet is in Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S pocket; and any member of it who may be chosen to preside over the new off- shoot of the Tariff Reform League will be his devoted nominee.

Our readers will probably notice this week that the "Examiner" is printed on a nem paper of superior quality and bulk. We should be glad if those who sufficiently appreciate the change would send us a postcard to that effect along with any suggestions they may have for the Improvement of the paper generally. The " Examiner " has long been one of the most popular papers in the district, and with the co-operation of our readers we hope to increase that:popularity and add to the paper's attractiveness.

Since the decision of the Town Council last week that Warrington Bridge shall be widened by cantilevers, there has been an immense amount c:f discussion regarding the wisdom of the step—so much indeed that one has been unable to avoid thinking how much better it would have been if the same people had only expressed thAir views freely before the matter had been decided. It

I seems to be a phase of human nature that when ' people are asked to seriously consider some important question affeccing the welfare of the community they neglect to do so until it is too late for their suggestions to be of any value. Since Tuesday of last week the Latch- ford and Wilderspool tramc.rs have accommo- dated innumerable impromptu debates on the future of Warrington Bridge. Now that the Council has decided upon one of the schemes before it, the armchair—or tramcar—critics have been much in evidence. One has not much patience with them. The Bridge question is no new one, Ind the Council has not acted so hastily that the man in the street has had no opportunity of expressing his opinion at the proper time if he desired tt, do so. There is, perhaps, nothing to be lost by a discussion even now,;but it would have been far more valuable had it taken plaze prior ta the decision of the Council.

We have heard a good many of the objections to the scheme which stands adopted, but we are not greatly impressed with their logic or reason- ableness. The main idea of the critics seems to be that the widening can only be a temporary improvement, and for that reason we aie throwing three or four thousand pounds into the river. To this contention we are much inclined to say: Rubbish! Let us admit that the improvement will only be temporary, and that a new bridge will have to be built some day. These are not unanswerable objections.

Perhaps it will be agreed that onr rates at present are so high that an expenditure of 17,000 to £20,000 is not to be lightly sanctioned. In fact we are fairly sure that a plebiscite of the ratepayers would result in such an outlay of public funds being strongly discountenanced on any grounds but those of the direst necessity, and wa have heard no arguwent to prove that such a condition of things ob- tains. We have gone on so long with the Bridge in its present condition chat we could probably manage some time yet, but the urgency of taking measures to remedy the nuisance has recently been emphasized. Temporary relief at this point is a requirement that exactly fills the Bill. A new bridge could not yet awhile bethought of. The presence nf the railway bridge and level crossings at once disposes of the larger scheme, and until there is a reasonable prospect of. the Railway Company moving to some purpose it is no good Warrington crying for a new bridge. Meanwhile it behoves the town to do something, and that something taking the form of widening the bridge by canti- levers we think it will answer the purpose admir.:. ably. Even if the improvement only availed for five years it would not be wasted or the money thrown into the river, and we know of no reason to suppose that it would not answer the requirements of the traffic for a considerably longer period. When the oppor- tunity arises a huge improvement can be affected in the neighbourhood of Bridge Foot, but it is not the policy of an enterprising corporation to stand idly by or plunge recklessly into expendi- ture the limit of which cannot be foreseen. The cantilever scheme is, in our view, the only possible middle course and we are unfeignedly glad that it has been taken.

Warrington's response to the cry from Mace- donia has so far been very disappointing, and we would commend to the earnest consideration of our readers the Rev. John Yon ge's appeal for sub- scriptions to the relief fund which appears in our correspondence columns. The tale of the brutal tyranny and oppression—culminating in heart- less massacres—which the inhabitants of this ill-fated land have been forced to endure at the hands of the terrible Turk, has often been narrated in Warrington. Surely it cannot be • that it has fallen upon deaf ears? The need for relief is as urgent as ever, and it does not redound to the credit of our town that only £7O has been raised. A more serious effort is called for and We hope that Warrington people will once more rise to the occasion and show that they are willing to aid the cause of suffering humanity even in a far-away region like Macedonia. A special article on Macedonia will be found on our second page.

Many are looking forward for a revival of trade in Warrington. At present the state of trade is far from satisfactory and much distress prevails. It has not become necessary to open soup kitchens and in some respects Warrington has been more fortunate than many Lancashire towns where the shortage in the cotton supply has caused acute dis- tress. Nevertheless the condition of things in Warrington is far from .satisfactory and the re- port on pauperism and distress which Mr. Sutton, the Clerk of the Warrington Board of Guardians has prepared, is dismal reading. Compared with the preceding year,there are over9o more inmates in the Workhouse, and the sums distributed in out-reli eihave gone up by over per week. Mr. Sutton considers the state ofthings to-day to be much worse than it was twelve months ago and sees no immediate prospecu of any material im- provement of the adverse conditions. We hope he will soon have occasion to take a more rosy view of things.

Few ratepayers in Warringtnn are aware of the excellent work which is being done by the War- rington Board of Guardians in the Clttage Homes at Padgate. If the Warrington Union was not the pioneer, it was at least amongst the first unions of the country to recognise the advantages of training pauper children in cottage homes, and equipping them for the battle of life, away from their old haunts and evil influences. The homes have been in existence for a number of years, and some idea can now be gained of the results obtained. It is satisfactory to learn that on the whole they justify the action taken by the Guardians. Many of the boys and girls who have passed through the homes are now doing well in following occupations for which they were pre- pared under the guidance of the Guardians. A few have lapsed into their former conditions, which is perhaps not surprising when one considers the strong hereditary tendencies to follow the evil steps of their parents. It is a com- pliment to the foresight of the Guardians of a generation ago that Unions in different parts of the country, after trying other methods, are now resorting to the school system which has proved so successful atPadgate.

The third test match, which commenced at Adelaide yesterday, must be considered on both sides of the world the most important of th? series. Haying already won two of these contests, the i mother country s in the happy position of know- ing that she has only to emerge triumphant from the present encounter in order to regain possession of the coveted ashes. On the other hand the Aus- tralians are fighting with their lives in their hands. They must win this match to have any chance at all of securing the honours. At the present moment they appear to have an excellent prospect, for with a score of 355 runs for 6 wi:-..keta they neld a position which will withstand a severe assault. Still there is no reason yet to fear for the English representatives. They may nave a score of four or five hundred to Lice, but we all know they are capable of doing so with a light- hearted confidence begotten of success continued right through the tour. It should be recalled just now that Australia won the toss at Sydney and had ultimately to take second place. IVlemories of the encounter make us all rejoice that Forster is back in the team. With Trumper contributing his usual century on the home side,it behoves that equally brilliant batsman from " Forstershire "to go and do likewise. Should the wicket keep good a great struggle for my is assured, and we are not prepared to sauclpmriecyeetthat there are even slight odds against the representatives of the Motherland.

--- Free Traders may legitimately regard the Mid- Devon election.result as one of the most striking proofs of the unpopularity of Mr. Chamberlain's tariff proposals. The fact that while the Liberal poll has increased by •'S•10 the Conservative poll has actually fallen off supplies solid testimony to the presence of a disturbing factor among the electorate. Were there any doubt as to the cause of this vast improvement in the position of

===== 0002601_19040116_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
the Liberal party in the Ashburton Division it would be removed by the frank admission of the defeated candidate, Sir Richard Harrison, that the activity of the Tariff Reform League in the con- stituency had the effect of reducing the Conser- vative vote. He admitted further that the fear of taxed food is a pa werful factor among the rural electors. S 3 long as the agricul- tural labourer maintains this attitude of stub- born hostility to the • food-tax proposals Mr. -Chamberlain's scheme cannot make much head— way.

When wheat was fetcling ".565. Bd. per quarter in the English market—that is to say, in the good old days of high Protection, from 1832 to 1841— Mr. Robert Bowman, farmer and vice-chairman of the Board of Guardians, Caine, Wiltshire, wrote: "In the great majority of cases the labourer has only the man's wage (83. to 93. a-week) to live on. •On that a man and his wife and family of four,five, or six children must live though it is a mystery to me how they do live." Mrs. Wiltshire, wife of a farm labourer at Cherhill, said: "Our common drinir is burnt-crust tea. We also buy about half a pound of sugar a week. We never know what it is to get enough to eat. At the end of the meal the children could always eat more. Of bread there is never enough; the children are always asking for more at every meal. I then say,' You don't want your father to go to prison, do you ? " Those who tell us that the distress in the rural districts in the Corn Law period bad no relation to the food taxes will find these testimonials hard nuts to crack.

Exhibition at the Parr Hall. As will be seen from our advertising columns, an industrial exhibition will be opened in the Parr Hall an Tuesday afternoon next, and will contimie until Friday, the 29th inst. Admission to the hall is free, the object of the exhibition beip.g to bring prominently before all classes important food preparations and methods of cooking them, domestic, sanitary, labour- saving, and scientific appliances, and to provide wholesome entertainment. Various welllinown firms are sending exhibits, and the attractions each day will include promenade concerts by a ladies' orchestra, cinematograph entertainments and cookery lectures. The Black Dike Brass Band. On Sunday two sacred concerts were given in the Parr Hall under the auspices of the Warrington League ot the Cross Band. The performances of the Black Dike Band under the conductorship of Mr. J. Gladney were made the feature of the programme and it is due to say that in brilliantly rendering some splendid selections from the standard composers they fully sustained their high reputation and locally gave infinite pleasure to audiences fairly numerous in some parts of the hall. Messrs. Peter and J. M. Bate gave . pleasant variety to the concert in the singing of some songs and duets. The songs by Master J. Mather were also worthy of special mention in so far as they betokened unusual musical ability in one so young. The pianoforte accompaniments were tastefully played by Mr. J. McA.voy. WATERS.—Advt. St. John's Literary and Social Society. At the weekly meeting of this Society, held in St. John's schoolroom on Tuesday evening, the Secretary introduced the annual manuscript magazine. The magazine was composed of the following articles, all contributed by inembers of the Society, viz. ;-1, ." Literature" ; 2, " Dreams " ; 3, " Series of prophetic events for each month 'of the year " ; 4, Sunday labour, or one day's rest in seven" ; 5, " The Model- Church " ; 6, "The present condition of. Ireland ";7, " Drunkenness—its prevention."—The President and Vice-President read the contributions, all of which were highly interesting, and showed, on the whole, considerable literary merit. Some of the -subjects were keenly discussed by the Members pre- sent, the meeting having to be unduly prolonged. Warrington Chamber of Commerce. At a meeting of the Council held on Monday last the President (Mr. J. Charlton Parr) in the chair, it was unanimously resolved "That the Corporation be respectfully requested to reconsider the proposal to widen 'Warrington Bridge by cantilever footways, with a view to substituting a new bridge which shall be adequate for the accommodation of any probable future developments in both road and river traffic." •Glonsiderable discussion took place with reference to, -the Warrington Dock, and a resolution was proposed and strongly urged requeAting the Rivers Committee to endeavour to obtain a clause in the Ship Canal Bill -shortly to be introduced to Parliament extending the time for its construction; but the prevailing opinion 'appeared to be that, in face of the fact that practically nothing had been done to foster a local traffic during the last ten years it would be useless to apply for a further extension. The Secretary reported that negotiations were in•progress with the leading steam- ship lines to Manchester with a view to getting them .to stop at the Warringt6n lay-by, and that several of -them had expressed their willingness to do so if a reasonable tonnage could be guaranteed. He suggested that the Chamber might act as intermediaries in the matter, and seek to gradually build up a local traffic. A resolution was also unanimously adopted in support nf the scheme for growing cotton within the British Empire.

Fire in Turner-street. s At 4.30 on Wednesday afternoon the Fire Brigade received information by telephone from Messrs. Radd's Wire Works that a fire had broken out at the residence of Mr. Joseph Taylor, 70, Turner-street. A number of men turned out under the direction of Superintendent Turner, but on arrival at the house found that the flames had been extinguished. It appears that owing to the illness of a child, a bed had been brought downstairs and placed near the fire. The bedding became ignited and was destroyed, but it was fortunate that the child was not in the bed at the tinie. A number of valuable birds were lost. A Sad Case. The Warrington police have received information of a sad case of attempted suicide.. Edward Heesom (55), a widower, residing with his son:in-law, John Pemberton, 11, Sharp-street, went out at 8.30 On Thursday morning for the purpose of seeking work. At noon he was brought home suffering from a stroke on the right side, and Dr. Curtis attended him. Mrs. Pemberton was going upstairs at 7.15 in the evening and heard an unusual noise in her father's bedroom. Accompanied by her husband she entered the room and found Heesom had cut his throat. He was taken to the Infirmary, and detained as an in-patient. He is progressing satisfactorily.

Institute of Journalists.

The annual meeting of the Warrington sub-district of the Institute of Journalists was held at the George Hotel on Saturday evening, Mr. E. V. Walker (retiring chairman) presiding at the outset.—On the proposi- tion of Mr. H. Isherwood, seconded by Mr. E. V. Walker, Mr. J. Hawthorn was elected chairman for the ensuing year. Mr. E. V. Walker was appointed vice-chairman and Mr. A. H. Walker was re-elected secretary.--Matters affecting the status of the pro- fession were discussed, and it was suggested that steps should be taken to establish a more general recognition of press privileges at various functions. Gratification was expressed at the increase of membership during the year, due to the decision of journalists in Runcorn and Widnes to join the sub-district.

Manchester and Liverpool District Banking

Co., Ltd. An ordinary meeting of the shareholders of this company will, be held at the head office, Spring Gardens, Manchester, at noon on Wednesday next. In their report the directors state that the gross profit for the half-year ending 31st December last, after providing for all bad and doubtful debts, is £225,784 Ss. id., to which has to be added the amount brought forward from 30th June last, viz.: £32,961 12s. 5d., making a total of £258,746 Os. 6d. From this sum the following deductions have to be made :—Total expenses at the head affice, London office, and branches, fncluding salaries, stationery, taxes, &c., £78,674 12s. 5d.; half-year's provision for income tax, £1,780 11s. 2d.; leaving a net profit :of £175,290 16s. 11d. The Directors recommend the declaration of a dividend of 21s. per share which will take £134,260, leaving a balance of £41,040 16s. 11d. The directors recommend the declaration of a divi- dend of 21s: per share, which will take £131,250; leaving a balance of £44,040 16s. 11d. Of this it is proposed to carry to the reserve fund £lO,OOO, and to next account £34,040 16s. 11d. The reserve fund, with the above addition, will then amount to £1,785,000. The directors have, from the bank's inner reserves, written down the Consols from £9O to £B5 and the other investments of the bank to present market prices. The directors whose term of office expires on the 20th inst., are Messrs. William Tipping and George Gordon Warren. They are eligible and are recommended for re-election. UNDER the CLOCK or GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. Fairfield Debating Society. An open meeti a of the Fairfield Literary and Debating Society was held at the Fairfield schools on Monday evening. There was a very god attendance of members and non-members who were attracted no doubt by the fact that the President of the Society (the Rev. A. S. Cameron) was to give a paper on the Macedonian question. The meeting was presided over by Mr. B. Hall, who at the commencement expressed the pleasure at being asked to take the chair at such a meeting. He then called upon Mr. Cameron to intro- duce his subject for discussion that evening. Mr. Cameron, who was well received, occupied some twenty minutes in describing the present position of affairs in Macedonia. He pictured vividly the nature of the atrocities that have been and are being com- mitted, and showed that the Turkish officials in many ways cruelly oppress and:wrong the Macedonian Chris- tians. Mr. Cameron contended that the Macedon ans would never secure justice and fair treatment until the Turkish Government wtre forced by a stronger power than themselves to change their methods and actions. It is the duty, he further stated of the Christian nations to combine and make themselves the stronger powers, and no doubt; when the Turks see that active steps are being taken, then they will give the Mace- dcnians a proper form of government. Mr. Cameron finally moved the following resolution, viz :--" That unless the Turks are coerced the right government in , Macedonia cannot be secured!' The opposition was' undertaken by one of the members of the Society who ' attempted to show that the Macedonians worked viol- ence amongst themselves for the purpose of provoking the Turks to take action, knowing that if the Turks were compelled to send in lame bodies of troops excesses would assuredly be committed which would justly excite general horror. In this way, he stated, the Macedonians were play- ing upon the sympathies of the world. He also quoted cases in which the Macedonians had been offered certain reforms, which if accepted, might have spared the world the horrors they have since witnessed, but instead of considering them and frankly accepting the same the Macedonians appeared to do their utmost to drive the Turks into taking military action and so deferring any reforms.ln conclusion the reader of the 'opposition paper moved an amendment to Mr. Cameron's resolution to the effect - " That the present is not an opportune time for intervention in Turkey by any or all the Powers." A very interesting discussion followed in which most of the members present took part. After the leaders of the debate had replied the Chairman (Mr. Hall) summed and com- mented on both sides of the question. Finally he put the amendment • and resolution to the meeting when it was seen that eleven members voted for Mr. Cameron's paper and seven for the amendment. A hearty vote of thanks to the Chairman for presiding brought t very enjoyable debate to a close.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

"FIGHTING AND „ARGUING."

At Widnes on Thursday, Mary Ann Walsh, 61, Mid- wood street, summoned her husband Michael Walsh, for persistent cruelty, and applied for a maintenance order. Mrs. Walsh stated that on New Year's Day he pulled her hair out in handfuls, and had abused her for the whole of her married life.—" in fact, he had been fight- ing and arguing for twenty seven years." The case was adjourned for a week to enable Mrs. Walsh to bring witnesses.

TRANSFER SESSIONS AT WIDNES.

A special court of the Widnes magistrates, Alderman S. Quinn (in the chair), Messrs. Neil, Wareing, Deacon, Brown, and Smith, was held to consider the question of licences.—Mr. G. W. Knowles applied, on behalf of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co., for the transfer of the following licences, which were granted :—Albion Hotel, Waterloo-road. from Alfred Charlesworth to Thomas Alfred Mareley ; Derby Arms, Widnes-road, from Thomas Robinson (deceased) to his son' Thomas; Wellington Hotel, Ditton, from Joseph Davies to James Henry Dutton ;. and the Ship Inn, Victoria-st., from William Jones to Thomas Thornton. The bench went into a series of alterations to twenty-three houses in the town and neighbourhood which were granted.— The whole of the alterations were sanctioned with the exception of the Horse and Jockey, Buckley-road, Widnes, which must be held over until the next licenging sessions.

ST. HELENS.

SCALDED TO DEATH WITH TEA.

Winifred Lea, aged two years, died in the St. Helens Hospital on Wednesday, as a result of being scalded with hot tea. It appears that the child ran to meet her father coming in from work on Wednesday morning, when a pan of tea, which had been made ready for him, was accidentally upset on to her, with the above sad result.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
POLICE INSPECTOR'S 'RESIGNA.T lON.

Inspector William Steele, the senior inspector of the St. Helens. borough force, on Wednesday handed his resignation to the Watch Committee, and was granted a pension after completing twenty-five years service. Mr. Steele has been inspector at St. Helens for upwards of sixteen years. He was previously in the Lancashire County Constabulary, where he spent most of his time in the office as clerk. At St. Helens he has had charge of most of the important cases and the preparation of statistics for the licensing justices. SEQUEL TO A COLLIERY FATALITY. Edward Balmer, collier, of Elephant-lane, was charged at St. Helens on Monday, by the Sutton Heath and Lee Green Colliery Company for a breach of rules. Mr. Pearce, who prosecuted, said that Balmer had a young man named Ashton working with him at the colliery last week, and he allowed him to do certain work which he was not qualified for, with the result that he (Ashton) was killed. The coroner's jury had censured Balmer.— Mr. Tickle, for the defence, said that Balmer had been a. collier for over twenty-four years without a complaint. —The magistrate fined him £1 and £2 is. costs. REMARKABLE INQUEST.—JURORS AND THE SUICIDAL _MIND.

"There is evidence in the first letter that he knew perfectly well what he was doing," was the reason urged by one of the jurymen who was in favour of returning a verdict of "Febo de se" at the inquest held at Denton's Green on Tuesday, on the body of John Nicoll, aged fifty, a storekeeper at Messrs. Pilkington's platet.glass works. Nicoll had beenaddicted to drink. On Saturday morning his young daughter found him unconscious in bed with a bottle of carbolic acid on the table, under which was a note which read, "Can't stand it any longer. Am going mid. What trouble! It is something fear- ful! —The Coroner said deceased had left two letters which he would not allow the Press to publish. —Police-constable Ho.vells said deceased smelled very strongly of carbolic.—The jury discussed the matter in private, and afterwards the foreman announced that he had taken a show of hands and there were seven•for a verdict of suicide while insane, and five for a verdict of sound mind.—The Coroner said the jury must agree. The five jurors must know that they were in favour of a verdict of felo de se. There was no doubt that a suicide knew what he was doing, but his mind might still be wrong. If the jury could not agree they could leave the question open. The jury thereupon returned a verdict of" Suicide," but that they were not satiqfied with the evidence as to the state of the man's mind.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

DEARTH OP MAGISTRATES.

Only one magistrate (Mr. John Unsworth) put in an appearance at Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday, and in consequence a number of eases had to be adjourned ntil to-day (Saturday). SMALLPDX CASE AT EARLESTOWN.

Dr. Watkins, medical officer of health for Nowton-in- Makerfield, reported yesterday morning a serious case of smallpox at Earlestown, the victim being a young married woman residing in Vitriol-square. The case has not yet been traced, but arrangements have been made for the woman's immediate removal to the Fazackerley Infectious Hospital at Liverpool. ALLEGED FOWL STEALING AT E A RT., ESTOWN.

At Newton Occasional Court on Monday, James Melia who for the past two months has lived in Manchester- row, Vulcan, Earlestown was charged with the theft of two fowls from the cote of Mary Peak, Vulcan-brow. From a statement made by Inspector Foster it appeared that a coachman named Litherland was working in his stable when his dog commenced to bark. On going to see the cause he found prisoner coming out •of Mr. Peak's hencote, and the two fowls produced were on the ground dead. Litherland secured the man and sent for P.C. Hughes. Whitewash .was found upon his jacket, which was caused with accused rubbing against an. iron bar which had been newly whitewashed. On prisoner being charged he denied the theft, but he now admitted his guilt.—lnspector Foster asked that accused should be remanded, in order that inquiries could be made into his antecedents. Prisoner was accordingly remanded until Thursday. Owing to a ::learth of magistrates at Newton Petty Sessions on rhursday the hearing was further adjourned until to day (Saturday). THREE MONTHS FOR " PREVENTING WITII INTENT". At Newton Occasional Court, on Monday, two St. Helens young men named John Wayman and John Fox, were charged with loitering with intent to commit a felony in Crow-lane, Newton, on the 6th inst.— Prisoners pleaded guilty.—P.C. Mellor stated that a complaint was made to him that two suspicious men were in Crow-lane. He hid in a hedge and saw the prisoners acting in a very suspicious manner outside gentlemen's houses. He arrested them after a chase, and they at once admitted that they intended to commit a burglary.—lnspector Foster informed the Court that Wayman was an associate of thieves and had been locked up at Newton once before, along with other men on a charge of loitering at Haydock. Wayman, however, was discharged to give evidence against the, other men. Since he had been locked up on that occasion they had ascertained that both he amd Fox were wanted at Leigh fora burglary at Leigh on the sth inst. It was proved against Fox diat he had been sent to prison for six weeks for theft at Ormskirk.—The Chairman (Mr. R. Stone) said there was no doubt that prisoners were pair of thieves prowling about the country. Thea would each be sentenced to three months,hard labour.

Councillor Maginn and his Workpeople.

There was a pleasant gathering in one of the commodious and well-lighted rooms at the Alliance Box Works, Orford-lane, on Thursday evening when, to celebrate the completion of the extension of the works, Councillor Magnin entertained his workpeople and their friends. About eighty guests sat : down to an excellent supper prepared by Mr. Collins, of the Spread Eagle Hotel, Lyntn. Councillor Maginn pre- sided. The meal over, the remainder of the evening was spent in a convivial manner, songs being ren- dered by Messrs. Taylor, Gleave, Sheppard, Rabbit, Lloyd, Newns, and Burns.--A hearty vote of thanks was acsorded to Mr. Maginn, on the proposition of Mr. S. Wright, J.P. Fire Precautions. The Fire Engine Committee of the Corporation are considering the ,advisability of providing hand fire-extinguishers in various public build- ings in the town, and yesterday afternoon an interesting demonstration took \ place on the Fair Ground, Queen-street, with the object of proving the utility, of appliances manufactured by the Minimax Syndicate Limited. The Mayor (Alderman Bolton), the' chairman (Alderman Smethurst), and several members of the committee, Superintendent Turner and members of the Fire Brigade, and Corporation officials were in attendance. Large boxeS" were ignited and in most cases the hand fire-extinguishers were sufficient to put out the flames in a remarkably short time. •

The Liberal Club. What promises to be a very successful " euchre " competition has just been commenced at this club. Thirty two entries have been received and on Thurs- day evening the opening contest took place. An excellent game resulted in a victory by Messrs. D. Francomb and R. Woods over Messrs. J. A. Chappel. and W. H. Baker by a single, point. The competition will be concluded on or before Saturday, 23rd. The billiard handicap is draw ing to a close and on Tuesday. Mr. J. Sankey defeated Mr. W. Ford by five. One of the billiard tables has recently been altered to the latest style and fitted with low, frost-proof cushions. A welcome addition has been made to the club library in the shape- of Mr. John Morley's " Life of Gladstone" which has been presented by a member of the sub- scription room. Local Oddfellowship.

The annual meeting of the "Prince of Wales" lodge of the Independent Order of Oddfellows (Man- chester Unity) was held at the Pelican Hotel on Thursday evening under the presidency of N.G. Whitham who was supported by P.G. Holford and P.G. Bradshaw. The Secretary (P.G. T. Wadsworth) presented the balance sheet which showed that the capital worth of the lodge, was ,2,700. There had been an increase in: capital during the year of :£lBO. The sick pay distributed had totalled over £250 and the average age of the lodge was 31 years. Brothers T. V. Bradshaw and G-. F. Freeman were elected auditors. A jewel was voted to P.G. Bradshaw for his services as Noble Grand and the duties performed by him in connection with the Coronation festivities, the recent bowling competition and the lodge dinners. P.G. Gartland was re-appointed sick visitor. Votes of thanks to the chairman and secretary concluded a notable meetin Unique Record of Service.

An interesting event took place in the office at Cockhedge Mills yesterday morning. Messrs. Armitage and RigbyLimited had noted the completion ofso years service, from the time of commencing apprenticeship of Mr. Henry Kirk the spinning mill engineer, and gave him a surprise by handing him a splendid watch and chain, in a handsome case, supplied by Russells of Liverpool. Mr. S. It. Armitage J. P. (with whom were Mr. Godfrey Armitage and the managers Messrs C. H. Hibbert and J. Ainsworth) wished him good health for many years to. come and trusted he would long be spared to wear it, and that the watch would be as good a-time keeper as he (Mr. Kirk) had been. Mr. Kirk expressed his thankfulness for their kind- ness and thoughtfulness. The watch was suitably inscribed and bore tegtimony to the firm's appreciation of his labours. It is interesting to note that . Mr. Kirk was working for the lirm 3 years before his apprenticeship began in January 1854, he having commenced working in 1831. He. is still as active as ever and seems likely to follow his employment for • several years to come.

PADGATE WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.

Annual Tea Meeting.

A very pleasant and successful function was the annual congregational tea meeting in connection with Padgate Wesleyan Sunday school which took place in the schoolroom on Thursday evening. About 160 mem- bers of the congregation and friends partook of a capital tea, the lady workers, as usual, presiding at the tables. At the subsequent entertainment the Rev: R. Wilkins Rees occupied the chair. Councillor W. Brough had arranged an attractive programme and the various items met with cordial appreciation. The opening item was the duet "Anchored', by Mrs. H. Cottrell and Mr. Pearce. Songs were effectively rendered by Mrs. Cottrell. Miss Sumner, Mr. E. H. Pearce and Mr. Levi Richardson, and there were pleasing duets by Miss Lena Duckwarth and Miss E. Hutchin, and Mrs. Cottrell and Mr. Richardson. Sergeant Maior Cowen played two flute solos in creditable style. Miss C. J. Bennett was an efficient accompanist. During the evening the annual report on the work of the Sunday school was presented by the Secretary (Mr. John Jackson). The following is a summary :—We have had 219 scholars uppn our registers (87 boys and 132 girls), which is an increase of 14 upon the previous year. The average attendance throughout the year has been 74 in the morning and 131 in the afternoon, against 72 in the morning and 127 in the afternoon; last year. Ninety-four scholars had gained prizes for their punctual and regular attendance throughout the year. We are fortunate in having an excellent staff of teachers, who have been most devoted and regular in their attendance, the average being 9 in the morning and 13 in the after- noon. The Savings Club in connection with the school which is under the management of Leonard Bennett and Mr. H. B. Nicholson, has again been very successful. The amount paid in has been £l5B 12s. 6d., which is an increase on the previous year of £2O. The Treasurer commenced the past year with a debt of 4s. 31d. The receipts during the past year have amounted to £27 6s. 511, and the expenditure had come to £24 19s. 10d., which leaves a debit balance of £2 2s. 4d.—The officers appointed for the present year are as follows :—Superintendelits , Mr. H. Ward and Mr. F. Wright; treasurer. Mr. James Gandy; auditor, Mr. Percy H. Bennett*; secretaries, Mr. G. H. White and Mr. John Jackson; savings club treasurers Mr. Leonard Bennett; librarian, Mr. H. B. Nicholson. T am pleased to state that we have 4re-formed the Young Men's Select Class in connection with our school, and that Mr. Brough has been appointed teacher. A junior branch: of the Wesley Guild has been formed during the year, and already a large number of scholars have beenenrolled, and we hope that there will be many more yet. From the junior branch of the Guild, the members will pass on to the senior, and from that we hope to strengthen our church with young people,which is the way: and only way, to build up a strong church. The Chairman delivered an interesting address on the work of the Sunday school teacher. The proceedings closed with a hearty vote of thanks to the artistes, chairman, and all who had assisted. on the proposition of Mr. William Bennett, seconded by Mr. James Gandy. Last evening the children had their annual tea party and the prizes gained during the year were distributed.

THE PARISH CHURCH RITUAL.

AN INNOVATION AND A PROTEST.

For many years, Warrington has enjbyed an im- munity from dispute on ritual but on Sunday there was a break in this very desirable state of affairs. It was brought about by an innovation which the Rector made in the ceremonial observed at the Holy Communion at the Parish Church on Sunday morning. It is common knowledge that the Rector's tendencies are towards High Church teaching and practice. Some years ago he introduced an altar cross into the Parish Church and the cross has remained on the altar ledge ever since. There was some slight dis- satisfaction expressed at the time but it gradaully died down and the cross continues to serve its symbolic purpose. The Rector has now gone one step further. For some time, the Parish Church Mission of St. Clement's in Bank-street has had the distinction of being the only Warrington church in the Liver- pool diocese which has had altar lights. The innova- tion was made without • any protest being raised and the Rector has long had a strong desire to make a similar innovation in the parent church. An opportunity presented itself by a gift of some chaste and beautiful candlesticks. the gift was mentioned at a meeting of the Church Council six months ago, but nothing more was heard of it until the 4th inst when the Rector announced- that he had taken upon himself the responsibility of accepting the offer and placing candles on the altar. He requested that tliere should be no discussion, and the matter dropped after a protest by Mr. F. G. Taylor, one of the sides- men and Superintendent of the Sunday school. In view of the Rector's attitude it is interesting to recall the ruling of the Bishop of Liverpool on ritual. In the course of his charge to the clergy of the diocese in November, 1902, be qtioted the well-known Lambeth decisions of 1890, which included the pro- nouncement that the burning of two lighted candles on the Holy Table ill broad daylight during the Holy Communion was not illegal. These decisions his Lordship did not dissent from, but proceeded to discuss methods of dealing with clergy who did nbt abide by them. • In aocordance with the Rector's decision two candle- sticks and candles were'placed on the altar in the chancel and two others on the altar in the Boteler private chapel, which is now used for week-day celebrations. The two candles in the chancel were lighted on Sunday morning at the early celebration of the Holy Com- munion and are intended to be symbolical, we believe, of the special presence of Christ, the Light of the World. It not being the practice to use the large altar lights at the usual services of matins and evensong, the candles were not used after the early service had concluded. Standing on the altar ledge, these silent emblems were innocent of the righteous indignation which their presence had caused to burn in the hearts of some members of the congrega- tion, and this indignation found expression towards the close of the servi,:e. It is customary for the wardens and sidesmen, after handing the collecting plates to the clergy at the altar to kneel while the Benediction is pronounced. Mr. F. G. Taylor refused to do so and his walking away down the aisle caused a momentary pause in the pronouncing of the Benediction. Mr. Taylor has explained his reasons for objecting to the introduction of the candles in a letter to the Rector resigning his positiog of Superintendent of the Sunday school. In the first place he maintains that the Rector should not have placed candles on the ' altar without consulting the congregation beforehand, as they "form in no way an essential point of religion, but are simply an act of ritual:" Secondly. Mr. Taylor thinks the Rector in having done so committed "a dis- tinct breach of faith, as several times from the pulpit he has promised he would not thrust his views upon the congregation." As to the legality of the lights, Mr. Ta_ylor has nothing to say In the afternoon, Mr. Taylor gave further evidence of his disapproval of the step taken by the Rector, by seeking to address the scholars in the Sunday school on the reasons which had led him to resign his position of superintendent. The Rector would not grant permission and after school, Mr. Taylor addressed the boys outside the building. At the evening service he repeated his protest in church. Among the 'congregation there is a divided opinion as to the wisdom of the Rector's action and a protest has been signed by several members of the Church Council, pointing out that the parishioners should have been consulted and that the Church Council ' should not have been treated with ridicule by being told that " the matter had been decided upon." Our reporter paid a visit to the Rectory with the object of ascertaining the views of the Rector on the situation which has arisen. He declined to make any statement at present, but stated that when the proper time and place came, he would be prepared to defend the action he had taken. He is much pained that the controversy has arisen, but admits that those who object to the step he has taken have done so from the kindliest motives. It is his ;wish that no bitter- ness should be introduced.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
TO THE EDITOR.

Slr,—May I ask, through the medium of your columns the opportunity of making known, to such of your readers as are my fellow worshippers, the reason for way action with regard to the recent change of ritual introduced iuto the Parish Church, and thereby putting an end to the many garbled reports which have been cir- culated in the town, and by the outside press. The history of the so called "candle controversy "so faras lam concerned is as follows. On the 6th July last, at the close of a badly attended Church Council Meeting, the Rector announced that an offer had been made to him of a pair of candlesticks, and, undoubtedly he then invited the opinion of the Church Qdicers present upon the subject of their acceptance.' Some expressions of opinion followed, and the matter dropped by the'Rector promising to bring the subject up again. From that date until the receipt of the summons fora Church Council quarterly meeting on the 4th inst., nothing was heard of the matter, although more than one meeting had intervened. At the meet- ing on the 4th inst. the Rector announced that he had decided to take upon himself the responsibility of accepting the offer made to him, and of using the "lights" and requested that no discossion on the sub- ject should follow. T protested, without avail. So much for the Rector's attitude to myself, as one of his Council, which exists for the sole purpose of being consulted upon parish matters. Upon the sth inst., knowing that the "lights" were to be first used upon the 6th inst, I wrote the Rector by way of further protest, resigning my post as Sunday School Superintendent. This letter was delivered before 1 p.m. and very fullyset forth the grounds of Imy action, 'viz., that candles being, in no possible manner, an "essential" in religion, but only ritual, the congre- gation ought first to be consulted, and, secondly, that by carrying out his intention, the Rector was breaking faith with his congregation, after several times promis- ing from his pulpit not to thrust his views upon his people. The candles were introduced, and lighted, on the 6th, and the Rector, in acknowledging my resigna- tion, chose to absolutely ignore the cause of it, and, in the course of subsequent correspondence, in two further letters only once refers to the matter by briefly saying "When the proper time and place come,' will say what- ever I think to he necessary." I shall not publish this correspondence as the Rector objects(though I am quite at liberty to do it, as I warned him in mY first letter that 1 reserved the right to do so), but I shall be happy to let any of the regular worshippers at the Parish Church see it at any time—they bei.,g interested equally with myself.

My private protests having thus been completely ignored, in order to further emphasise my belief that the "time and place " mentioned by the -Rector should have been beforehand (and not afterwards), and that he is not the sole arbiter of what is " necessary " to be said upon a point of ritual, T, on Sunday last, made the further open protest. If the Rector will yet take the course of removing the ornaments, and calling a con- gregational meeting to astertain their wishes, it will probably be the means of ending this unfortunate matter. If not, he must exp3ct in the future, protests similar to mine of last Sunday (which I trust no one present con- sidered irreverent.) from those who have no wish to see the Parish Church, the third Church • in the town to be emptied by the Ritualistic party. In such-an alternative there is no other way of recording their objections. The Church belongs to the worshippers as well as to the parson, and, on points of ritual, the former have every right to be consulted—and I hope that the Rector will yet recognize this. As the Rector has preached since the introduction of his n.w ritual without referring to the subject, I con- clude that he sees the propriety of not now selecting the security of the pulpit as the "place" from which to say "whatever he thinks to be necessary."—Yours FREDERICK GEORGE TAYLOR, 274, Manchester-road, Warrington, I:ith January 1904.

"THE IDLER."

Successful Periorman3cs by 03 Warrington Dramatic Society.

Those who braved the rain storm of Thursday evening for the purpose of witnessing the first per- formance of " The Idler" by the members of the Warrington Dramatic Society, were well.re-paid, for the performance must be counted as one of the most successful in the annals of the society. In the autumn •of last year when " Our , Boys" was perforated the high expectations which were indulged in were. hardly realized and the. audiences were a trifle dis appointed, but in the presentation of " The Idler,'- the members of the society have risen to the occasion' and the play is produced .in a manner which is alike pleasing and satisfactory to all concerned. In past years the funds of the Infirmary have benefited from the labours of the slciety but on this occasioa the local branches of the Royal Society for the Preven- tion of Cruelty to Children and the Children's League of Pity are to receive much-needed financial assist- ance. Sir Gilbert aril Lady Greeaall had promised to be present at the initial perbrin.ance, but at the last monient Sir Gilbert telegraphed from Woolsthorpe "Greatly regret impossible for us to return for per- formance. Wish it every success." "The Idler " is an original modern play in four acts by Mr. C. Haddon Chambers and on its produc- tion at the St. James' Theatre, London, Mr. George Alexander won some distinction in the character of Mr. Mark Cross (" The Idler ")—a single man of leisure who amuses himself, and alarms his widowed mother by mysteriously dis- appearing for months at a time. The plot, if so it may be termed, may be summarised as follows. stark Cross, " Gentleman Jack" and Simeon Strong are three young adventurers who meet together in the "Golden Valley of the States.": As the out- come of a quarrel, "Gentleman Jack" 'shoots dead a brother of Simeon Strong and makes good his escape to England. Here he unexpectedly inherits the baronetcy- of a cousin and becomes a member of Parliament as Sir John Harding, M.P. He marries Helen Merryweather, a beautiful woman and daughter by his first wife of General Merryweather. Lady Harding had . previously had an ardent admirer in. Mark Cross, who cm return- ing to L-mdon, in the hope of winniug her hand, learns that she is married to Sir John, whom he finds- to be no other than " Gentleman Jack," the slayer of Simeon Strong's brother. • Sir _John endeavours .to explain away the shooting of Strong's brother by asserting that it was the result of an accident, which he very much regretted, and repents having run away. In the meantime Simeoa Strong, who has become an American millionaire, and intends in his own words " to knock corners off this earth," visits London rind On meeting Sir John at once recognises him and Informs him of his sworn deter- mination to avenge his brother's death. He means to have justice, and to:let.Sir John try the credence of an Ametican.jury with his version of the story." The situation is explained to Lady Harding by her old lover, Mark Cross, whose love for her has " torn his senses away," and knowing his influence over Simeon Strong by reason of having on one occasion saved his life, he extracts from Lady Harding, in a weak , moment, a promise that she will forsake her husband in order to save his life. At first Simeon is obdurate, and will not accede to the entreaties of lark to abandon his revengeful policy against Sir John, but eventually Mark induces him to" break the oath of a hasty revengeful heart." Having succeeded in his object, Mark claims from Lady Harding the fulfilment of her promise, but she refuses on the ground that it is "better to break a bad pledge than do an infamous thing. Her midnight . interview with Mark in his chambers is interrupted by the appearance of Sir John on the scene. In the eyes of Sir John both his wife and Mark have compromised their honour and he will hear' of no explanations. Ile challenges Mark to a duel with revolvers, and the fateful moment has arrived when they are about to fire at each other when Simeon Strong interposes. A reconciliation takes place and allends happily except perhaps for Mark, "the idler," against whom the fates seem to have con- spired. He determines to pack up for a long journey towards the north—a decision which gives joy to his faithful manservant, Bennett.

The 'characters w4re as follows :--- Mark Cross Mr. Charles H. Wood Sir John Harding, M P., Mr. Sidney Wallhead Simeon Strong (of New York) . Mr. Harry Mosley general lierryweither Mr. Gsaffrey Dodd 3nnett Mr. Victor Roberts Lady Harding Mrs. Cross Mrs. James Fairclough Mrs. Glynn Stanmore Mrs. Jennings Kate Me r rywe ather The cast, ;t will be sem, is not a numerous one Most of the artistes' namls are familiar ones to those who have taken an interest in the career of the Warrington Dramatic Society Each one has appeared on the stage before and may therefore be considered as having overcome any weakness to stage fright. The acting on Thursday was so uniformly good that any adverse comments must savour of the captious and hypercritical. The opening seene was perhaps a little too slow, but it enabled the audience to get a good grip of the story. In undertaking the leading role of iVlark Cross, Mr. Wood had a heavy task but he discharged it with marked ability. times his voice showed signs of wearing hut on the whole he capably sustained his part to the end: In the scenes with Sir John and Lady Harding he showed dramatic power of a high quality, which the audience were not slow to appreciate. Miss Margaret Pierpoint had a difficult and exacting part to play as Lady Hard- ing,and one which is plrhaps of a more onerous character than any she has hitherto attempted. he acquitted herself with a dignity and personal charm- which won high commendation. Her finely-modulated voice stood her in good stead and she made the most of the sensational dramatic situations. A fitting climax WAS reached when she fell swooning to the floor under the baseless charges of her husband, and the audience were constrained to demand the raising of the fallen curtain. Mr. Sydney Wallhead as Sri. John Harding, M.P., was also seen at his best in this scene, and his scorn knew no bounds when he taunted Mark Cross with having "saved my life and killed my heart." Mr. Harry Mosley has already gained a high reputation in the town and district as an amateur dramatist and in his characterisation of Simeon Strong he may be said to add to his laurels. He gives a typical American flavour to this character, and his voice and manner are such as to become almost infectious. His love making with Kate Merryweather,the General's daughter,is one of the choicest bits of the play. Miss Bessie Pierpoint adds charm to the situation by her vivaciousness and coquetry. Her childlike and bland way of asking him how she mustanswer his proposal of marriage, receives an amusing set-off when he guesses that an answer of "Yes," and the granting of permission to allow him to kiss her, would be a good start. Miss Bessie Pierpoint also distinguished herself in company with General Merryweither. Her curiosity with refer- ence to his intentions respecting 'marriage for a third time is so persistent as to lead him to the conclusion that her " impertinence is only equalled by her intrusive- ness." Mr. Geoffrey Dodd plays the part of the old general with becoming gallantry. The amusing situation in which Mrs. Glynn Stanmore, a fashionable widow looking out for a husband, confesses that the general's "exquisite refinement had touched and Axon her." when as a matter of fact his intentions lay in the direction of winning the hand of her friend. Mrs. Cross, is happily hit off. Mrs. Jennings played the part of the widow with taste,and her discreetness in withdrawing from the situation which her outspokenness had created Was a triumph. The character of Mrs. Cross, the mother of Mark Cross, could not have been placed in ' better hands than those of Mrs. James Fairclough. Her interview with Mark in which she tried unsuccessfully to fathom the secret which waslroubling the heart of her son was an example of true motherly anxiety, and her parental solicitude found expression in the sentence "You don't understand that helping to bear the child's pain is the mother's greatest joy." Mark was unres- ponsive, despite her pleading that there was "a medium between wearing his heart on his sleeve and con- cealing it in his boots." Mr. Victor Roberts as Bennett proved himself a loyal man-servant for Mark Cross. There was a bit of worldly wisdom in his remark that "a servant as has the truth always .on the tip of his tongue Ain't no use." "He should always," continued Bennett, "have a lie ready behind his teeth—and one behind each tooth for that matter." The stage manage- ment was in the capable hands of Mr. C. H. Wood. There were one or two unduly long pauses between the 'acts, but Mrs. W. B. Roberts' orchestra pleasantly whiled away a portion of the time of waiting by playing selections of music.

The performance was repeated last evening, and the concluding performance will take place this (Saturday) evening.

NEW BANANA STEAMERS FOR MANCHESTER. A new fleet of banana boats is to be put on the ser- vice between Port Limon and Manchester by Messrs. Elders and Fyffes, Ltd. The Matina will be launched to-dav at Newcastle, and the Miami and the Mitnistree will follow. The four present steamers are to be put on theßristol traffic. The new boats will accomplish the journey from Costa Rica in about sixteen days against the nineteen days occupied at present, and on each voyage will bring 45,000; bunches (about 120 to 150 bananas in a bunch), against 35.000 now carried. The vessels will be specially adapted for , carrying fruit.

Dr. Braide, medical officer at the Workhouse, wrote as follows :--5, Bold-street. 311 y ask you formallyto bring before the Board my application for an increase of salary. I have been medical officer now for 11 years, and during that time-the amount of work and responsi- hility has greatly increased as you are aware. Eleven years ago there were only two nurses employed at the Workhouse hospital. Now we have a superintendent nurse, three charge-nurses, and 12 assistant nurses. The number of patients has doubled, and the number of lunatics certified as fit for residence in the Workhouse hal.., almost trebled. ' We also have two male lunatic attendants under my supervision. The above facts prove that my work has greatly increased, while my salary has remained stationary. I, therefore, ask you to bring the matter before the Board, and feel sure that they will grant my request. The resolution was carried. • . AN INCREASE OF PAUPERISM. THE CLERK'S REVIEW OF THE SITUATION. Local Government Board, January 2nd. • 2, Westbourne-road, • Southport. Sir,—l should be much obliged if you would send me a short report as to (a) the state of pauperism in your Union at the present time (b) the existence of any serious or unusual want of employment (c) the special measures, if any, which have been taken by the Guardians or other local authority for dealing with the unemployed (d) your own opinion as to the outlook for the next three months. Any information you may be able to give should be posted to my private address as above not later than Wednesday next. Faithfully yours, 11 JENNER .Tux., general inspector. Warrington Union Offices, 4th January, 1902.

Dear Sir,

Our -returns show considerable increases of number of paupers and of outlay of relief. The 'umber of pauper inmates of Workhouseas compared with last year exceeds by over 90 for the last several weeks. The out-door relief exceeds in the same period by about £6 weekly. The large works are on short time, and many men are out of work. At Earlestown (Newt9n) the number of unemployed men seetns heaviest. Destitu- tion is, however, being liberally eased by means of voluntary contributions. Upon the whole I view the state of things to be worse then in previous years' winter months, and whilst hopeful for good weatherand better employment, I am unable for the present to see the prospect of material improvement of the adverse conditions. The Guardians have not found it necessary to adopt any exceptional measures. If the need for such ' should arise they will undoubtedly exercise their powers of amelioration as the conditions stay require. —Yours faithfully, J. C. SUTTON, clerk. The CLERK said he had received a further letter from Mr. Fast, thanking him for his report on pauperism an/ distress.

THE NEW OVERSEER. The CLERK said he had received notice of the appoint- ment, of Mr. James Fairclough as an overseer in place of Mr. Joseph Shaw Green, deceased. This was all the business of public interest.

TRADE DEPRESSION IN PRESCOT UNION" The Prescot Guardians on Thursday considered the increase of pauperism, the Clerk reporting an increase in the number of inmates of the Workhouse.—Mr. Middlehurst said if this went on they would have to enlarge the house again. He thought they might revert to the old practice of granting out-door relief, rather than admitting to the Workhouse.—The Clerk pointed out that the Union was suffering from trade depression and that considering the local circumstances the pauperism in the Union was not exceptional. As a matter of fact, if they looked into the figures, they would see it was really less than twelve•months ago.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

E STRAIGHT TALK To A PRISONER.-A FORGIVING WIFE. •

"You will be a mean scoundrel if, after your wife has pleaded for you as she has done, you do not justify her expectations." This was the remark addressed by Mr. T. Birchall to Charles Booth, shoemaker, 118, Winwick-road, who appeared before the Warrington magistrates on Monday on the charge of neglecting his childrea.—Mr. A. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C.,and said the prisoner came under the super- vision of Inspector Hackett in June, 1902. He was warned time after time by the Inspector, Mr. Taylor, police court missionary, and Constable Lewis, but on the 13th February last he came before the magistrates. During the week previous to that date he had made such an improvement that at the request of the wife the Bench decided to give him another chance. On the under- standing that prisoner would sign the pledge the case was adjourned for three months. Prisoner signed the pledge but did not keep it, but the wife said his conduct was so satisfactory during the three months that the case was not proceeded with further. On September 15th prisoner bolted from the town, leaving the wife and children without an, means at all, and Inspector Hackett took out a warrant. When he was apprehended it was found that he sent his wife 5s one week,and for a number of weeks he forwarded sums of ten shillings. His wife had been begging very hard for him not to be sent to prison and she said he would turn over a new leaf. If it had not been for the kindness of their grand- mother the children would have been starved to death. For a whole week prisoner was never sober, and he had run the wife and children out of the house.—PriSoner's wife pleaded so hard for him that the magistrates decided to adjourn the case for two months. They inti- mated that if Booth came before them again he would be sent to gaol.

FACTOR::: ACT PROSECUTIONS.-SUNDAY WORK AT WARRINGTON.

A number of prosecutions under the Factory Act were heard at the Warrington Police Court, yesterday, Mr. Lewis, chief inspector of factories for the district, conducting the cases.—John Bayley Isaac, King. street, was summoned for having employed Agnes Highley and a boy named Robert Littlewood, on Sunday, December 20th.—Mr. Lewis said defendant was liable to a fine not exceeding £3 in each case. Mr. Isaac had all the information in regard to the Act at his disposal and must have undertaken the risk with his eyes open.—Mr. Isaac said that although an abstract of the Act was displayed on the premises he had not read it. He did not ask Miss Highley to do any work on Sunday, but as they were exceptionally busy she came voluntarily. He was aware that the boy was working.—A fine ef 2s. 6d. and costs in each case was imposed.—Joseph Partington, tailor, 43, Bridge-street, was summoned for not having an abstract of the Act displayed in his workshop and for employing a youth named Joseph Mahoney after the legal limit of time— Mr. Lewis said Mahoney was found working at sixo'clock on a Saturday:evening whereas he should laaveice,asedwork. at four o'clock. An abstract of the Act had been sent to the defendant but it was not affixed to the wall—Asked what he had to say, Mr. Partington replied" Least said soonest mended." He was fined 2s. 6d. and costW in each case—Messrs. Bennett and Son, Derby House con- fectionery works, Lovely-lane, were summoned for not displaying an abstract of the Act and for employing six females on Sunday December 20th.—Mr. Lewis said that in this case an abstract had been served on the defendants with a requisition that they should affix it to the wall. On the date in question it was found that the abstract was not displayed and six females were working as if it had been a week-day.--The Mayor: Do von think this is systematically carried on?—Mr. Lewis: If there is pressure they think very little of Sunday. There is no virture in being good when there is no tempta,_ tion.—Mr. Bennett said the Christmas pressure wa, responsible for the employment of females on Sunday. —A fine of 2s. 6d. and costs in each case was inflicted.'

THE UNEMPLOYED.

The two words at the head of this article form what printers call a standing line. Never a winter passes without this melancholy title for a record of want and suffering appearing daily in our journals. It is a pitiable thing, an evil that the wisest statesmen and the richest and most generous philantropists at their best seem only able to alleviate. To discover a com- plete remedy for the trouble passes the wit of a man, and whoever looks forward to a period when there shall be profitable work always ready for the hand of him who is willing to labour is regarded as a dreamer. But in our country there is a second vast army of unemployed whose sufferings are never discussed in the newspapers, viz., the thousands who have work to do and are anxious to do it, but who are kept away from office or workshop by ill health. The amount of work (and therefore of money) lost in this way each year is incalculable. Nevertheless it is an evil which might in large measure be prevented, for Mother Seigel's Syrup cures indigestion, a cause of immense suffering and loss, as well as a forerunner of many other complaints. "About two years ago," writes Mr. David J. Thomas, of Central House, Drefach-Llandyssil, Carmarthenshire, 30th September, 1903, "my appetite failed and I became incapable of work. A dull pain at the stomach, particu- larly after eating, greatly troubled me. Though I was extremelycareful as to what I ate, this symptom kradu- ally grew worse nail' was never free from it. I was troubled, too with wind and dizziness, which sometimes caused rue to stagger about. Although very ill, Iso far contrived to work through it all. A doctor here prescribed some medicine for me, but it did not touch my complaint, and I was not a bit the better for it. After that another doctor nearrarmarthen attended me with the same result. "After enduring this misery for about six months,and seeing no chance of recovery under the treatment I was receiving, I one day happily thought of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup.' had often heard it spoken of favourably and resolved to obtain a bottle of it.l had not takenimuch of the medicine before I discovered that it was benefit- ing me considerably. My appetite and strength in- creased daily. All the pain and dizziness left me, and in five weeks I was well and strong again, able to work with pleasure." . -

_ . To be able to work with pleasure is a sure sign of health, and people in that happy condition are always the last to be numbered among the unemployed.

EDUCATION IN CHESHIRE. The Question of Religious Instruction.

A meeting of the Cheshire County Education Com- mittee was held at Crewe on Thursday, Mr. C. E. Thornycroft presiding. It was decided not to provide books necessary for religious instruction, other than Bibles, for voluntary schools which had not beenhanded over to the Committee; and that managers of all non- provided schools be informed that the Education Com- mittee disapproved of the taking of children our of the schools for the purpose of giving religious instruction during school hours. Dr. Hodgson brought forward a report from the Higher Education Sub-Committee, which recommended that the County Education Committee should offer a number of bursaries for boys and girls of twelve years of age and upwards, who,lat the age of sixteen or there- abouts, will be willing to become candidates for engagements as pupil teachers; the bursaries to be toneble at secondary or other day schools and pupil teachers' centres.—ln the course of discussion, Judge Bradbury said there was a dearth of elementary !teachers, and.in the future that dearth might be increased.—The eport was adopted.

Protecting the Children. During the Quarter ended 31st December, 1903, the Warrington and District branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, (local office. 34, Parker-street, Warrington), inquired into 82 complaints, of which 79 cases were found to be true, and affected the welfare of 253 children. There were 108 offenders. Action was 4taken as follows :—Warnings were given in 69 cases: I cases were dealt with by transfer, Sze., and 6 cases were found to he of such a gross nature as to necessitate prosecution. Six months, two weeks of imprisonment with hard labour, and £5 in fines was [imposed. The Society's Inspector made 442 visits. of. supervision; 5 of the children died before or during inquiry.

HOW EGGS ABSORB ODOURS.

Very few people are aware how rapidly eggs absorb the odours of articles they are often placed near, such as onions and kerosene, or even the filth of the poultry house. The shell is porous and in a few hours the yolk of an egg will become tainted by near proximity to strong odours. Strictly fresh eggs that bring the high- est prices to local customers are removed shortly after they become separated from the hen, and are kept in a clean receptacle in a clean room.

The Rue de In Republique, Lyons, France, is paved with glass. The blocks are eight inches square, and so closely fitted that water cannot pass between the inter- stices. Greenland glaziers average, according to a recent report, one thousand feet in thickness, move fifty feet a day, and discharge into the sea foursquare xiles of ice yearly one thousand feet thick.

WARRINGTON.

Local _Landlady's _Sudden Death.

Mrs. Hart, wife of Mr. Thomas Hart, landlord of the Beehive Hotel, High-street, Northwich died sill- denly last Friday night. The deceased, who was only 31 years of age, had been ailing for some time, but on Friday night she was in her usual cheery mood. She partook of supper with her husband, and having done so she placed her hand on his shoulder and suddenly expired. Dr. Marsh, her medical adviser, was sum- moned, but his services were of no avail. Dec9ased and her husband are b lth very well known in the town, having resided at the Beehive Hotel for upwards of three years. She was a daughter ef Mr. James Dooley, of Hatton, near Warrington, and well-known in that village. The interment took place on Tuesday at Daresbury Church.
