Lecture on Sugars.

At the fourth meeting of the session in connection with the Literary and Philosophical Society. Mr. J. Allan (Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons) lectured on "Commercial Sugars" with some notes on their manufactufas and uses. The President (Mr. F. (1 Ruddock, F.1.C.,) presided over a small attendance Mr. Allan observed that the term sugar was origin- ally applied to all bodies having a sweet taste, and thus came to be indiscriminately used in speaking of a very large variety of substances possess- ing few points in common, and in many cases having no relation to each other After tracing the history of the sugar cane which appeared to have been Common in India in very remote 'times, Mr. Allan pointed out that the first French factory was established at Lille in 1810. The peace following npon the downfall of Napoleon allowed cane sugar to be again imported into Europe, and, to save the beet industry, a heavy import duty was placed upon the cane. product, while the home material was produced free of duty. That preferential treat- ment had the desired effect, for by allowing time for selection and improvements in the cultivation of the beets, it placed the production of beet-sugar on a firm - foundation. - The growth of this industry had been most extraordinary, for the modest 1,090 tms produced in 1827 had grown to 4,000 tons in 1838, and in 1902 the world's production of beetroot sugar reached the huge total of nearly seven million tons, a figure which was no less than nearly three million tons greater than the world's production of cane sugar. The artificial conversion of starch into an uncrystallizable form of sugar known as glucose, was first accomplished by Kirchoff, of St. Petersburg, in 1702, and of late years that discovery had been turned to practical use, enormous quantities of glucose being prepared from starchy bodies in America, the Continent and to a lesser degree in this country. Nothing definite was known of the history of the other sugars obtained from the maple, sorghum and various palms. The separation of sugar from green maize stalks was an. old Mexican industry whilst among recent ad- vances might be mentioned the preparation of sugar from melon juice in the United States and of milk- sugar in Switzerland. Natural products such as the honey of bees and other insects and vegetable exuda- tions, such as manna, had probably been used far beyond historic periods. Mr. Allan proceeded to give details of the treatment of sugars from a scienti- fic standpoint. He was cordially thanked for his lecture, the discussion being taken part in by Messrs: W. B. Roberts, J. Fulton Smith, J. Moore Murray, Charles White and the President.

WARRINGTON TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

The Unemployed Question.

The monthly meeting of the Wewrington Trades and Labour Council was held in Bold-street Wesleyan Schoolroom on Tuesday evening under the presideacy of Air. J. Podmore junr. There was a good attendance including Councillor F'. Stott the secretary.

THE PRINTING TRADE,

The SECRF:TARY read a letter from Mr. German, the secretary of the Warrington section-of the Typogsaphi- calAssociation, thanking the Council .for the efforts put forth on behalf of the printing trade in Warrington and congratulating Councillor Stott upon his election.

TRADE UNIONISTS AND NEWSPAPFAS.

The Manchester and Salford Trades and Labour Coun- cil forwarded a copy of the following resolution which they passed at a recent moeting, in the hope that some practical way might be found of putting it into opera- tion :--`‘ That, in the opinion of this Council, the time has arrived when the working classes should support only those newspapers giving prominence to speakers on behalf of labour, equal to that given to speakers on behalf of other parties." No comment was made upon the letter. BASK E r-m.A K ERS' D ESPUTE.

The Committee decided to give its credentials to an appeal about to be issued on behalf of the Union basket-makers employed at Mr. Johnson's works who have come out on strike.

THE TAXATION OF LAND VALUES.

The SECRETARY presented a report of the Proceedings at a conference recently held at Manchester under the auspices of the Lancashire branch of the Land National- izationsociety.lle said 200 delegateslwere present,repre- seating nearly 300.000 members of Trades Councils, Liberal Associations; Reform Associations and the Independent Labour Party. He was much struck with the enthusiasm and unanimity displayed on tile questton of land reform. Mr. George Harwood, M.P., of Bolton, presided. It was considered that the present was a very opportune time to raise the question, because they who had studied the question knew perfectly well that the land question was the most important subject they had to deal with. The Chairman suggested that there should be a 10 par cent. tax on land to commence with. At the present time almost a third of the income of the country was paid to the landlords for the use of the land, and it was also contended that the excessive railway rates were in a large measure due to the high prices paid for the land over which the lines were constructed. The value of land had grown very rapidly in consequence of the enterprise of muni- cipalities and it was only fair that some of that in- creased value should go to the rates. The present depression in trade was principally due to the land system. There was a general unanimity of opinion that the most important thing to do in the matterat present was to educate the people, and it was dectided that it was advisable to form a Land Reform Council in Man- chester, which could be used for the purpose of organizing lectures, distributing literature, Sx. They must not ally the movement with the Liberal or Con- servative parties but take entirely independent action. Before they could get any large measure of land re- form they would have to develop the various organiza- tions which had been formed for the purpose of obtaining stronger labour representation in the House of Commons and on local goverding bodies. There was a very strong feeling in favour of municipaliza- ion rather than nationalization of the land. The fiscal question was mentioned at the conference and the tdelegates were almost unanimously against any measure of Protection.

The report was adopted. THE PRICE OF GAS-A PLEA FOR USERS OF SLOT

METERS. In accorlance with notice of motion, Mr. BULLOCK moved "That seeing that the Gas Departments are making large profits and have conceded a reduction in price to consumers other thAn prepayment consumers, we request the Corporation to concede a like reduction to prepayment consumers through slot meters." He considered that the present system was unjust and that users of slot meters were fully entitled to a reduction. Mr. EATON said he understood the Gas Committee had the matter under consideration.

The SECRETARY said the matter had been under the consideration lof the Committee for some months, Councillor Maginn having raised the question before he entered the Council. He (the sp&ticer) was of the opinion that no profits should he made out of gas, because they were always given back towards the reduc- tion of the rates, and the working classes did not benefit to the same. extent as the employing classes who were large ratepayers. If they could reduce the price of gas to the average cost of manufacture, the working classes would benefit in the same proportion as the employing classes. He felt almost sure that some alteration would be made in the slot meter system, and that if the consume;re did not get any premium they weed have more feet of gas for the same price. The resolution was carried. DIRECT EMPLOYMENT OF LABOUR BY CORPORATIONS. The SECRETARY stated that he had received a pamphlet with reference to direct employment of labour by Corporations. He was strongly in favour of such employment, as instead of being swindled by con- tractors, the Corporations wouldhave a guarantee of the work being well done, and only, the best materials be- ing used.

The CHAIRMAN moved that copies of the pamphlet be obtained, and said he held the direct employment advocated would do much to prevent difficulties in re- gard to the payment of the standard rate bf wages by public bodies. In connection with Corporation con- tracts a great many revelations of jerry work had lately been made. He did not say in Warrington, because he did not believe that it was the worst town in the world, but if they could get direct Corporation employment adopted, it would b 3 a very good thing for the town and would prove a paying departure i the long.run. (Hear, hear.) It was decided to order copies of the pamphlet. ENGINE AND BOILERSIEN.--SUGGESTED IMPORTANT TESTS.

The Council instructed the Secretary to vote in favour of the following resolution which will be intro- duced at the next meeting of the Lancashire n.nd Cheshire Federation of Trades and Labour Councils,:— "That all persons having charge of steam engines and boilers shall pass a practical examination of fitness for such duties and .on passing such examination shall be granted certificates of efficiency; and that no person be allowed to take charge of an!,' steam engine or boiler who does not hold such certificate." The resolution, if adopted, will be communicated to the Home Secretary.

TILE UNEMPLOYED QUESTION. CORPORST[ON._ASKED TO PROVIDE WORK

The SECRETARY of the Warrington branch of the , National Union of- Gasworkers and General Labourers wrote stating that at their last meetings long discussion took place on the unemployed question, and it was thought that the Town Council might be asked to do .something in the way of forming a labour bureau in Warrington, as it was the only means of knowing the number who were really in want of work. Although from one side it might seem to be playing into the hands of the employers, yet on the ,other hand they had to admit that the Town Council had the power to provide direct employment in times of distress. He understood that the Eccles Town Council had promised a deputation that they would commence some improvements im- mediately and only employ those men who lived in the borough. The trade unionists of Warrington had the advantage/of many other towns in having a Labour representative on the Town Council who was always ready to champion their cause and watch the interests of the working classes generally. Mr. HANDLES (of the Gas Workers) said that in War- rington a great many unskilled labourers were unemployed and tit@ number was on the increase. He moved "That this Council asks the Corporation to open a labour bureau for residents in the borough, and further ask that if exceptional distress is proved the Corporation open out as much work as possible, such as streets, sewers, parks, and tramways or any other work that will absorb some of the unemployed." It was also suggested thatif the Corporation commenced the Work required the labour should be directly employed by the Council, Mr. ROWLANDS seconded.

Mr. Ma.LoNE said he did not know whether the Cor- poration allowed their employees to be sent out on the "hire system," but on Christmas Day there was a procession through the town of gas meter inspectors, tramway inspectors and conductors, and others carrying postmen's bags upon their backs, at the pay of Is. an hour, whilst ratepayers were looking on and doing nothing. He objected to men doing a day's work at their ordinary employment and afterwards working for the post-office. That Council had been asked to agitate for a reduction of hours of labour, and he considered that when a man worked eight hours on the tramways ho had no right to Oork for another department. Mr. Su mr considered they would be playing into the hands of the employers in asking for the establishment of a labour bureau as such a system lent itself to "free labour." "JERRY BUILDING" AND RHEUMATISM.

In summer you walked past a field of corn. To-day that same field has given place to a row of houses. People live in them.. They must livesomewhere ! How about the damp from ground and clouds? Does jerry building Increase rheumatism? If not how do you account for the increase of rheumatism in recenryears? Rheumatism is just now gripping thousands of workers. Are you one of its victims? If so whether you've got it through a damp house, a damp bed, or through the recent damp weather, doesn't matter so much as how you can get rid of it. Chas. Forde's bile beans will rid you of it sooner than anything. Mr. Hy. PGole, of 1, Vicarage-road, Watford (Herts.), first felt the pains of rheumatism in his shoulder. Then it went to his hips and the lower part of his back. It made him only able to walk a few yards even with the aid of a crutch. He couldn't sit down to his meals; but actually had to kneel on the floor and have his plate placed on a chair or a sofa. He consulted four differ- ent doctors and went to different hospitals, but got no relief.

He next went to Buxton for special treatment; and there met an old man who told him to try bile beans instead of the special treatment. It didn't sound promising. Nevertheless he tried bile beans, and tried and tried again, until he noticed an improvement. "In a few weeks," he says, "1 was better than I had been for a long time, and wasable to get about without, pain. So I persevered with bile be4ns until I was per- fectly cured. I have put my crutch away now and am walkingablut as well as ever T did in my life." Chas. Forde's bile beans are obtainable from all medicine vendors, but don't take any other preparations offered you as " just as good." No such prep iration has yet been discovered.

MEETING OP THE PARISH COUNCIL.

The Penketh Parish Council met in the Wesleyan school, on Tuesday evening,when there were present—Dr.Murray (in the chair), Messrs. J. S. Snoddy, J. G. Withinsha.w, John Midwood, and C. E. Parker, and the Clerk (Mr. E Foster.)

THE LIGHTING OF THE VILLAGE. A deputation reported that three lamps had been erected in nuerdley-road, Stocks-lane, and one in Grey- stone-road,and also that N 0.3 Kern incandescent burners had been affixed to every lamp in the village, and were a great improvement. MR. DODDS-RESIGNS FROM THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

The Clerk read the following letter, from Mr. James Dodds :—"Penketh House, near Warrington. Lancashire Education Committee, Local Sub-committee's District. Dear Sir,—l have had reluctantly to resign the position of a member of this sub-committee, as I find it quite impossible to attend the meetings at Earlestown on Thursday afternoons, having almost invariably im- portant business engagements on that day of the week. I have advised the Director of Education at Preston of my decision, and he will doubtless advise you. It was kind of the Council to nominate me, and I much regret to be unable to honour the nomination for the required period." The Chairman remarked. they would probably have forgotton that the gentleman recommended for election would have to serve on the Penketh Infants' School Management Committee, in Mr. Dodds's place. Mr. Withenshaw proposed the Chairman. Mr. Parker seconded, saying that he did not think there was any one better for the position as Dr. 'Murray had alwa3s taken a great interest in education. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman said he would consent, but said he thought they might find some one better. Upon being put to the meeting. it was unanimously resolved to recommend Mr. Murray to the County Council to fill the vacancy occasioned by the resigna- tion of Mr. Dodds. _ THE LAMPLIGHTER'S WAGES.

Mr. Midwood brought up the question of the Peuketh lamplighter's wages. He said that eight years ago the lighter had nine lamps in his care for which he received is. per week. A little later his salary was increased to 10s.. and now, in 1904, when he had nineteen lamps to light he only received the last mentioned sum. His duties took him about eighteen hours per week. On that scale he Mr. Midwood contended, that he should receive 14s. per week. The Chairman proposed an Licrease of 2s. 6d. per week.

Mr. Parker seuonded, and it was decided to recom- mend the increase to the District Council.

THE FUTURE OF THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

Co-ordination of Higher Education in Warrington.

Important proposals affecting the future position of title IVarrington Grammar School will shortly engage the attention of the Warrington Education Committee. At a recent meeting of the School Management sub- committee the following correspondence was submitted:— Board of Education. South Kensington, London S.W., 14th December, 1903. Sir,—l am directed to enclose, for your information, a copy of a letter addressed to this Board by the Clerk to the Governors of the Warrington Grammar School, together with a copy of the Board's reply thereto. An application for the inspection of the school under section 3 of the Board of Education Act, 1899, has been received from the Governors, and the inspection will be held as early as is practicable in the ensuing term. The Board would be glad to be infqrmed whether the Local Education Authority have yet formulated any scheme under section 2 of the Education Act, 1902, for the supply of education higher than elementary and the co-ordination of all forms of education within their area, or have taken any steps towards carrying out the duties imposed upon them by that section. It appears to the Board to be desirable, with a view to the con- sultation referred to in the sections that one of their officers should meet the Local Education Authority or representatiVes of that body, before the inspection of the Grammar school takes place, in ().der to discuss, in an informal manner, the general proposals of the Auth- ority in regard to secondary education, with special 'reference to the future position of the Grammar school, *hich must necessarily fill an important place in any icheme of co-ordination for the Borough. If this proposal meets with the approval of the Local Education Authority, the Board will instruct Mr. A. Cardevv, one of their Senior Examiners, to visit War- rington at an early date after Christmas in order to hold the proposed conference, and I am. to enquire whether Thursday, the 21st January,would be a conveni- ent day for the purpose. I am, Sir, Your obedient Servant, (Signed) W. N.BRUCE. The Director of Education, Technical Institute, Warrington. (Copy of letters referred to in foregoing letter). Market Place, Warrington. 9th October, 1903.

R,3 WARRINGTON GRAMMAR SCHOOL:

Sir,—faM instructed by the Governors of the War- rington Grammar school to write you with reference to the present position and future administration of the school. The school is an old established one but, owing to the fact that Warrington has become such a large manu- facturing town, and to the changes brought about by the Education Act and from other causes, the circum- stances of the school have considerably altered; and the Governors are of opinion that the timp has arrived when something must be done to strengthen the posi- tion of the school. _ _ _

If its present efficient condition is to be maintained its financial position must be enquired into and re- adjusted. In former years the school was a boarding school, but now owing to the situation of the sehool buildings in the midst of works and in apoor quarter of the town, parents will not send their boys as boarders, and the number of scholars atten,ling the school is consequently not growing. The Warrington Corporation too, have recently erected a very large school for technical and secondat7.l education purposes, and it is feared that this will in the future compete with 'the Grammar school. Under these circumstances, the Governors, at a meet- ing yesterday, wished me to suggest to you that the school should be inspected by one of your officials with a view to advising the Governors what course to adopt. If you would kindly send someone to Warrington, I will ask the headmaster to meet him, and will after- wards make arrangements for a meeting of the Govern- ing Body to receive his report. am, etc. (Signed)'VEßE B. DAVIES, Law Clerk and Receiver to the Governors of the Warrington Grammar school. The Secretary, Board of Education, South Kensington, LONDON, S.W. Board of Education South Kensington, S. W. 4th November, 1903.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Founder of the Glories of Chatsworth.—ln olden times a royal visit not nfrequently ruKint ruin to the host, but though the Duke of Devonshire must have expended a very large sum in entertaining the King and Queen at Chatsworth, it is not likely to involve his Grace in financial embarrassment. The Cavendishes owe net a little of their great wealth to the famous "Bess Hardwick," who was married four times and acquired additional wealth with each of her husbands. It was her husband the Earl of Shrewsbury who was the gaoler of Mary, Queen of Scots, who was imprisoned for thirteen years at Chatsworth. The several seats of the Cavendishes also had their origin in the ambition Which "Bess Hardwick "had for building. She be- lieved a fortune-teller, who said she would die when she left off building. So she went on building until she was ninety, and then justified the prophecy, as during a hard frost in the winter of 1607, when all building operations were stopped, she died. A Famous Society Resort.—The presence of radium in the medicinal springs at Bath may possibly revive the social glories of the famouslcity, which gives its name to a brick, a bun, a chair, and, as the unutterable purists might add,atowel. At one time Bath was the rendez- vous of all that was fashionable in society—gay with beaux, and belles, who congregated there to take the waters, to gossip and shew off their fine airs and clothes. But though society has found othercentres of attraction, the beautiful city remains, and is steadily increasing in favour as a residential district for the well-to-do, and wealthy classes. It is quite possible however that the discovery al radium in its waters will add to the attractions of the city, as a health resort. It is true no doubt that radium has al ways been there, but no one knows as yet as to what medicinal uses it may be put under new methods of application or treatment. In Memory of Joan of Arc.—The story of the heroic Maid of Orleans has al says appealed to the admiration of the people of this country, and it is therefore inter- est,ing to learn that after nearly five centuries, she is to have the full recognition of the Roman Catholic Church, and to be beatified and canonised. Born in, tor about ,the year 1410, she was not much more than twenty years old, when she suffered death at the stake at Rouen in 1431, ostensibly on the charge of heresy, but in reality because her enemies feared her. History is not very reliable in apportioning the blame. but her death was disgrac ful to everyone concerned in it, and not least to the English who permitted her to be burnt at the stake. The name Joan of Arc will however always be honored by the people of this country, and it is gratifying that her memory is to be perpetuated by the ceremonies at Rome. Leap Year Proposals.—This being leap year—the first one for eight years—women are supposed to enjoy the privilege of making matrimonial proposals. It is a very ancient privilege, and dates so far back that its origin is lost in the misty records of the past. Possibly it was regarded more seriously in the old romantic days than it is now, as we are told by former writers that it was not gentlemanly for a man to treat a proposal with slight or contumely. If he refused it he was in honour bound to buy the lady a new silk dress. The Silk Gown of Olden Times.—The silk dress of olden times used commonly to pass, with the necessary alterations from mother to daughter. This was at least the case amongst the non-wealthy classes, and in aristocratic families, the silk gowns all found their way through the upper servants to a lower strata of society, and no doubt ultimately attained the same hereditary rank. But in these days of adulteration, and of cheap and shoddy imitations, the silk dress does not last so long, and one of the medical journals in discussing this subject, rejoices over the fact that it quickly wears out. It has not the time, it says, to collect the host of microbes that must hare existed in the old hereditary silk dress, before it finally became dissolved in nothing- ness.

Dame Fashion declares for the Japanesc.—Dame Fashion is always running after the soldiers, and when a war breaks out she invariably bedecks herself with some distinctive article or emtilern of the national garb of the party she is in love with. It was that splendid hero in war, Garibaldi, from whom we adopted the "Garibaldi "—a forerunner of the blouse—which was at one time in almost universal wear in this coun- try. Fashions of military vogue were also prevalent in the days of the Napoleonic and Crimean wars, and we all remember how the colour and fabric of Khalei sprang Into favour on the outbreak of the wax in South Africa. It is therefore not surprising to find Dame Fashion turning her attention to the dainty fashions of Japan.. Japanese buttons and embroideries are being largely used for the ornamentation of gowns and coats. Tea _jackets, and lounge coats of figured Japanese silks are also in :great request, whilst the convenient Rimono pattern is being adopted for evenink cloaks more mid more. It is much too early to be thinking of sun- shades, or else the Japanese sunshade would be, and may be yet, Dames Fashion's chosenemblem for shewing her sympathy for the little Island Kingdom. Children's Parties.—The leading form of entertain- ment at children's parties is dancing, the Christmas tree, conjuring, thought-reading, and threatricals be- ing quite out of favour for these particular entertain- ments. Another tendency, and one in the right direc- tion, is to provide a much more simple array of refresh- ments than has been the case during the past two or three seasons. In the matter of dress however, the idea is all towards elaboration and extravagance, and Party frocks for the girls are a serious item nowadays. The softest voiles, Japanese silks, and silk muslins, are tucked, and inlet and flounced with beautiful lace, to such a degree that they are little less costlythan the " grown no's" ivn. The notion of taking a large Party of little folks to the pantomime is this year con- spicuous by its absence. Last year, many Sociefy hostesses preferred to entertain their little guests in this manner, but possibly they found the responsibility of looking after their flock a heavy one, and there is some idea that the Chicago fire has something to do With the present disinclination to take a large party of children to the theatre.

The Velvet Costume.—kt the many smart weddings that have taken place lately, velvet has been the fabric most in evidence, and in the amethyst, and sage green tints. Rich Imperial purple has also been selected on several occasions, but it should be carefully seen to that the complex ion will stand the proximity of this sometimes very trying shade. For those who cannot afford the luxury of an entire velvet gown, there re- mains the cloth costume, trimmed with velvet. The prevailing mode is to trim dark cloth with velvet of a very much lighter shade, and surround it with cord or braid. _ _

MAD GE.

A pathetic story was told at Durham on Wednesday at an inquest held in the County. Hospital on Emily MgLaughlin, the five-years-old daughter of a Kimbles- worth miner. The poor child died on Sunday from burns received through her pinafore being ignited, as, in the absence of her mother, she bent over the fire to show some other children "where Santa Claus came clown." The coroner referred to the pathetic feature in the case that the child believed in the pretty poetic fiction about "Santa Claus," and lost per life through it. The jury found a verdict of accidental death. •

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

THE winter sales are one of the golden opportunities not only for that insatiable individual, the bargain-hunter, but for all those who are anxious to replenish the con- tents of their wardrobe so as to bridge the hiatus between the winter and the early spring fashions. Indeed this is with many people the time to which they look forward as the most suitable for acquiring furs for, in- deed, we nowadays do not have much frost or snow until January, when the cold seems to strengthen as the days lengthen. Very often one can pick up real bargains in furs at these winter sales,for many firms are anxious to get rid of their winter stock in order to make room

for daintier goods. Fur coats are perhaps amongst the most tempting of the" reduced" articles and are, generally speaking, a very good and safe investment provided all that is very [pronounced in regard to style is avoided as it dates itself and will therefore soon re- quire alteration, the consequent outlay adding to its original cost and nullifying any pretensions it might have had to being a real bargain. Moleskin was one of the furs which was most !fashionable at the beginning of the season, but has been so closely imitated in plush that the exclusiveness consequent on its costliness is gone and it is included among the many short - lived fleeting fancies of fashion.

The fur coat illustrated is of the 'blouse shape and is made of soft black caracul with a shaped yoke which is extended across the shoulders and for a short distance down the upper part of the arms, of stitched and tucked black satin. The cuffs are also of satin embroider- ed in the same dull gold as the collar. KATING COSTUME,

year's coat, which possibly boasted a deep collar and bell-shaped sleeves. By removing the collar and substituting the satin yoke for that of fur and adding the deep gauntlet cuffs, together with the embroidered collar, and introducing a touch of lace, a startling trans- formation and improvement could be brought about in a garment which had no other claims to fashion. There are lengths of lace both piece and insertion which it is always wise to secure provided the, price is really reduced, as they will be found invaluable throughout the ensuing months as their uses are too numerou to mention. Lengths also of brocaded satin or flowered chine silk in some of the quaint blurred patterns with which we are so familiar make up into dinner or theatre blouses, or if there is not sufficient material, into fascinating little coats for wear over an embroidered or lace vest. These dainty 'little coats are gener- ally WOlll with a deep swathed waist band, and elbow sleeves while the fronts are left open. The pretty skating costume sketched is of a warm tone of brown made with a deep yoke and front of velvet of the same colour, while a row of gold buttons finish the front. It is worn with a brown fur toque trimmed with a chou of orange-coloured chiffon and an osprey. The skirt is cut rather short to allow of free- dom of movement and has inserted pleats at the hem at each seam to give it sufficient

A SMART HAT.

spring. There is a touch of orange velvet in the collar band (with which is worn an em- broidered turn-over collar) and at the waist. Much of the success cf the toilette depends on the selection of colours which contrast in a pleasing and artistic fashion as it is not every tone of beaver which may be placed next to orange with success. A gown and hat like this are not necessarily confined to skating only, for nothing could be smarter for a walk- ing toilette worn perhaps with some nice furs. Of course, if one can afford a costume to be used solely for skating then I would suggest hav:ng the skirt cut a' little shorter and edged with brown fur, which could also be intro- duced on the bodice and sleeves. A smart hat, such as is given in my third sketch, could be,substituted for the fur toque if •desired, for the Marquise or tricorn shape in felt is much and well worn this season in any colour. The hat sketched is of brown felt trimmed with gold cord, while the crown is swathed with silk to match the hat and a stiff military aigrette in brown and gold gives an undoubt- edly smart effect. It is unfortunate that the increase in the popularity of this shape of hat is.sure to bring its banishment from the ward- robers of the leades of fashion for it is a style which proves becoming to a great number of laces au' has a smartness all its own. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

11re monthly meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday afternoon, under the presidency of Mr. Joseph Stokes. There were also pre- sent :-114essrs. A. R. Norman' S. Woodward, T. Wright, J. Edwards, E. Clarke, T. Woods, J. Mounlield, S. Taylor, C. E. Linaker, T. Lea, T. Baxter, T. Lewis, J. Spruce, G. Pilling, J. L. Jones, T. Bolslaaw, T. Darling- ton, W. H. Bradford, E. Bretherton, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. R. Hough (assistant clerk), I'. Cooke, I'. Riley, and W. De Prez (relieving officers),and J. Cunnah (workhouse master).

PROPOSED ALTERATION IN TIIE TIME or MEETING.

_ Mr. Norman said it bad occurred to many of them that there was a great waste of time between luncheon and the hour of the 'clock meeting. That day they finished lunch at one o clock or immediately after, and they were waiting until two o'clock before they could proceed with the business. While they were anxious to do justice to the work they did not want to waste any time. He gave notice that at the next meeting he would move that the Board meetings be held at 1.30, and the meetings of the Relief Supervision Committee at one o'clock.

TUN DISTRICT AUDITOR.

A letter was ;•-e;a.; from the Local Government Board certifying th appointment of Mr. F. M. Adams as dis- trict auditor for the Cheshire audit district.

THE BOARD'S SUBSCRIPTION TO CONVALESCENT HOMES.

The clerk reported that in accordance with the in- struction given at the last meeting he had made in- quiries respecting certain convalescent homes in the vicinity of the union. He ascertained that the amounts of subscriptions required to enable the board to send patients to the institutions were as follows :—South- port convalescent home, 33s for three weeks' main- tenance; Devonshire hospital, Buxton, 21s. annual sub- scription for each patient up to five, beyond that number casual subscription of £2 12s 6d, per patient ; Liver- pool convalescent institution, Woolton :—West Derby subscribe £25 per annum and pay 15s per head per fort- night ; Parkgate convalescent home authorties did not Mr. ;.Woodward alluded to the Buxton institution reply. and asked if the Board could send patients on the terms specified if they did not become regular subscribers. The Clerk: lam not prepared to say definitely, but I should not think so.

Mr. Cunnah said that when patients were sect now recommendations had to be obtained. The Chairman: it was stated at the last meeting that we ought to subscribe to some institution in order to establish our right. Mr. Pilling said he believed the Warrington Union subscribed three guineas to the Buxton hospital and they were entitled to send two or three patients with- out any further cost. The home was intended princi- pally for those suffering from rheumatism and similar ailments. _ _

Mr. Edwards proposed that the Board sehd a subscrip- tion of one guinea. Mr. Woodward moved an amendment that two guineas be subscribed. It would, he said, be to the interests of the Board to become subscribers, as they would then need no recommendations and patients requiring treat- ment could be sent to the institution at once. Mr. Mountield seconded. _

Mr. Eciwa—rifs wi-cire-N-‘• his motion and the proposal to subsoribe two guineas was unanimously agreed to.

STOCKTOi HEATH'S REPRESENTATION.

_ The Clerk of the County Council wrote in reference to the application of the Rural District Council for an order alteriag the number of rural councillors for the parish of Stockton Heath, asking whetlacir the matter had received the consideration and approval of the Board of Guardians. _ _ _

The Clerk said the matter had not been before the Board but it had been discussed by the District Council who recommended the increased representation. Mr. Mounfleld moved that the Board signify their approval of the proposed alteration.

• "Mr. Pilling seConCled. Mr. Woodward said the matter was really one for the Runcorn guardians to consider as the other members of the Board .were else members of the District Council which had agreed to the scheme. The proposition was carried. PARLIAMENTARY COMMITTEE'S DECISIONSL_

The -di;;:C;:ei;rt-e-ci-t,"lud at a meeting of the Parlia- mentary Committee it was decided to support the reso- lution of the Cockermouth Union urging the Local Government Board to introduce legislation extending the power of Guardians to enable them to recover in certain cases the cost of relief rendered necessary through immorality and to detain persons in the Workhouse in certain cases. Letters from the Staine's Union and the State Children's Association, with reference to vagrancy and the bringing up of children as paupers were discussed and it was agreed to support them. It was resolved that a letter from the Kensington Union with respect to the detention of feeble-minded persons in the Workhouse lie on the table. A letter asking the Board to pass a resolution urging the Local Government Board to promote legis- lation ,making mothers of girls of sixteen Bailie to prosecution for neglect in certain cases not at present provided for was considered but the committee could not see their way to support it.

The decisions wen c-onfirinid. FINANCIAL.

Mr. Woodward in moving the adoption of tlui minutes of the Finance Committee, said the payments for the month included the following for out-relief:— Mr. Cook, £80; Mr. Riley £24 ;Mr. de Prez, £3O. The vaccination accounts for the quarter had bean received, the sums being: Dr. McDougall, .239 18s. 6d,; Dr. Carruthers, £3 15s. 6d.; Dr. Burton, £l4 17s. ; Dr. Bower, £l7 ss. 6d. ; Mr. Cook, £lO 12s. 4d,: Mr, Riley, £4 9s. 2d. e Mr. de Prez, £5 4s. 2d.; total £96 2s. 2d., as compared with £97 4s. 11d, for the corresponding quarter last year. The County Asylum account amounted to £389 us. 3d. which was about normal. The total payments were £686 15s. The balance in hand last month was £1978 10s. 3d., and the receipts amounted to £404 6s. 10d., leaving a balance available of £1696 2s. id. The actual balance in the treasurer's hands was £B9 105./4d. more than that as some cheques had not been presented. The report was adopted.

DEFAULTING TOWNSHIPS,

Mr. Woodward'ldii—tii;i:i; were still three defaulting townships in the matter of the calls due last November. Acton Grange owed £79; Moore, £93 ; and Sevelioaks, £2O. He moved that another demand for payment be made upon the overseers. This was agreed to. At a later stage of the meeting Mr. Woodward said he was informed that last year Sevenoaks failed to pay the May call until September. The amounts for May and tAugust were paid together. He thought it was becom- ing a serious matter and he felt it his duty to report the case specially. He understood that repeated applica- tions were made to the overseer last year, and a threat had to be used. It was a serious matter that a public official should be so dilatory in collecting and forward- ing the money to the treasurer. SCNREET FEVER IN THE HOUSE.-PROMPT ACTION Ey

THE MASTER.

Mr. Wood presented the minutes of the House Com- mittee which stated that two cases of scarlet fever had occurred in the Workhouse. Both, however, were of a mild character and the patients were progressing favour- ably. The committee tendered their thants to Mr. James Taylor, Heathercliffe, Helsby, for a gift of .£5 sent to provide an extra treat for the old people and children. The number of vagrants admitted to the House during the month was 143 against 129 for the same period last year. The resignation of Nurse Rea had been a epted and it was decided to apply to the Northern Workhouse Nursing Association for a successor, :the remuneration being £3O per annum with uniform &c. The Clerk was also instructed to give tS'urse Rea a testimonial.

The Chairman said great credit was due to the master and matron for the energetic steps taken to prevent the spread of scarlet fever in the institution. • The minutes were approved.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

Authors of letters intended for publication must inall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not Later than Thursday.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
Passive Resistance.

Sir,—As it is once more becoming our painful duty, for conscience sake, to enter the arena. of conflict against the Education Act I shouldbe glad if you could allow me space to draw attention to the Passive Re- sistence League's advertisement appearing in this issue of your paper. And to add that the committee desire to urge the great importance of definite action in this matter, upon all who are in sympathy with the move- ment ; earnestly hoping that all such sympathisers will join the League either as passive resisters (if rate- payers) or associates (if non-ratepayers) and so strengthen both their own position, by mutual co- operation, and the protest which the League is deter- mined to maintain, in. concert with the hundreds of kindred associations throughout the country, so long as this unjust Act remains unaltered. Yours etc., on behalf of the committee,

WILLIAM CLATWORTHY (hon. sec.)

===== 0002601_19040116_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
Temperance work in Warrington.

.Sir,—Permit me to correct a mistake mad; by your c *rrespondent "Observer" in his letter on drunkenness in Warrington in your issue of Saturday last.He stated that with the exception of the WYcliffe Temperance Society there were no adult Temperance Societies in the town whose objectlwas to reclaim the drunknrd and save others from falling into the drink evil. Now sir there are geveral such societies meeting in this town that I. know of and probably Ido not know them all. There are two Good Templar Lodges, one at Ebenezer Church, Buttermarket, and one at Latchford Temperance Hall. Then there is the Bold-street Wesleyan Temparance Society, the Charles Garratt Temperance Society, which I amtold meets at Ellesmere-street, and several Rechabite tents in various parts of the town and the Warrington Temperance Federation now re-organised is holding public meetings in the various places of wor- ship throughout the town. These are a few I know of and "Observer" must observe that the Bands of Hope that are held in almost every place of worship in the town are supported by adults and have a very large adult membership and many of the total abstainers attribute their total abstinence to the education they received in the Band of Hope. Thus "Observer" 'will see that while Warrington is painted black enough with its drunkenness there are some in the town who are doing their utmost to reclaim these poor unfortunate beings whom he is so sad about and if he has their wel- fare at heart and thinks it his duty to do something to mend matters, I am sure that any of the above societies would only be too pleased to welcome him in their midst and find him a work to do to reclaim the fallen and save others from falling.—Yours Scc., MERLIN'.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
Alderman Heaton and ." Dirty Filthy

Jews."

Sir,—ln your paper of last Saturday I read that Alderman Heaton,in discussing the Markets Committee's report, made use of the above words—" Dirty filthy Jews."

As a shopkeeper in Warrington, I agree with him as to some of the goods sphl in the market to the detri- ment of the tradesmen in the town; but as an English- man and a citizen of Warrington, belonging to the Jewish race and religion, I wonder what his Worship the Mayor was thinking of when he allowed i such a remark as the above n the Council Chamber, without making the worthy Alderman retract his words which might have been in ignorance or prejudice. I was always of the opinion, that the Mayor was chosen to represent and protect the liberties of all sects and societies, when they are known to be lawful and loyal citizens. If the Jew and his religion are disliked by Alderman Heaton, I should like him to know that it was a Jew Lieutenant of the Lancashire Regiment -Lieutenant Raphwl of Spion Kop fame who was once a, member of the small Jewish community in Warring- ton. _ _ _

In justice to the few Jewish residents of Warrington I hope tkis will be the last time such offensive remarks concerning them will be heard in the Council Chamber. Thanking you in anticipation for inserting this letter, —Yours &c., _ _ _

I. GOLDSTONE.

January 11th, 1904. P.S.—Has not Alderman Heaton heard of a Jew Lord Mayor of London or Liverpool. and even Prime Minister of the party he belongs to?

Sir,—lt is reported that in discussing the minutes of the Markets Committee in the Council Chamber on Tuesday last, Alderman Heaton alluded to the " dirty, filthy Jews, with their dirty, filthy clothes." I would like to know why Alderman Heaton should make such a coarse, insulting and abusive statement about the Jews. I have made careful inquiry and I find there is not a single Jew in Warrington Market who sells second- hand clothes. There are, I believe, dirty and filthy • clothes sold, but they are sold by Alderman Heaton's own country people. Alderman Heaton might have taken the trouble to make sure of his facts before ven- turing to damage the reputation of apeople who are as anxious as he is about the health of the town.— Yours, &c.

B LOVE,

3, Dial-street, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19040116_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Bridge.

Sir,—Since the fatal accident near the Warrington Bridge last year, I have often wondered when a move- ment would be made in some direction, either to widen the present bridge or construct a new one, and hearing about six weeks ago that the matter was to be brought before the Council, I wrote to the Town Clerk, asking him to get for me from the Engineer the quantities of iron and steel which would be required for the construc- tion of the proposed bridge 60,ft. wide including foot- paths, and the cost of same, and also the cost of canti- levers to be put to the present bridge. The estimated cost of the new bridge 60ft wide, including footpaths. is £17,789 10s., but since this estimate was made, I find that iron has droppd in price about £2 per ton, and as 473 tons are required in its construction, this quantity at £2 per ton, through iron being lower in price, will save £946 if built now, so that taking £916 from £17,789 10s., the present cost would only be £16,813 10s.. He also informs me that the original price for cantilever's with footpaths ift. 6in. wide, would be £3,017 10s., but if they are to.be made 10ft, wide and to carry certain pipes or mains which the Corporation now require. the Engineer states they will cost 50 per cent. more. This will give a cost of .d,571 ss. if the cantilevers required by the Corpora- tion are to be affixed to the present bridge, and if done doubt if a load of hay or a furniture van can pass the tramcars with safety, and I think it will be found very soon that the present bridge altered will not be wide enough. Then About £4,500 will have been wasted. From my experience I find that most cities and towns have under discussion the widening or re-construction of their bridges for the following reasons :—First, because most of them require two lines of trams, and room for vehicles to pass over the bridges at each side of these lines. Second, when the bridges were con- structed, engineers and authorities did not take into consideration that a bridge has to take all the traffic from the surrounding district. Take for instance War- rington. The main roads, Winwick-road, Manchester- road, and Liverpool road, converge at Warrington Bridge, for all the Midland and Southern Counties. When this matter is more carefully considered, I am under the impression that many, if not all of the Town Councillors who voted for the cantilevers to be affixed to the present bridge, will come to the conclusion that it will be unwise to spend four or five thousand pounds on cantilevers, when you can have a new bridge for sixteen to seventeen thousand pounds.

I have no doubt that the engmems can make pro- vision that, if at any future time the bridge which it is now proposed to build 60 ft. wide, should be found too narrow, the footpaths could be taken in for vehicular traffic. and cantilevers be attached at a very lowcost to carry footpaths say 10 ft. each. This would make a bridge giving 60 ft. for vehicular traffic and two foot- paths each of 10 ft., a total width of 80 ft. If this is done, Warrington will be able to boast of a Bridge second to none in Great Britain,—Yours Stc. EDWIN WOODS.

Latchford, January 13th. 1904. .. Relief for Macedonia.

Sir,—You were good enough a few weeks ago to allow me to call attention in your columns to the cry of distress and need which comes from the desolated and devastated people in Macedonia. With your kind permission T desire once again to remind your readers of this pitiable case, and to implore them to render generous help. The fund in Warrington, opened by the Mayor in response to the resolution of the town's meeting in Woveniber last, has, T regret to say, only realized a little over £7O. This has been .grievously disappoint- ing to those of us who are interested in this case, and we still hope that, now Christmas festivities and expenditures are over, it may be possible to secure some increased contributions before the local fund is closed. I only wish that your space, and the natience of your readers, would allow of some detailed information being given in this letter. That, however, is perhaps too much to ask. But it may be briefly stated that the Committee of the Macedonian Relief Fund in London basso far received, up to December 31,a sum of 219,021, and this mosey is being expended, by the hands of care- fully-accredited scents, in trying to mitigate the dis- tress of about 100,000 people, who are homeless and penniless in large districts of Macedonia. Let it be remembered that these are the survivors of hideous massacres which have been perpetrated by the soldiers of the Sultan. They have seen their men slaughtered, their women dishonoured. and their little ones thrown into the flames of their burning homes! They

have been able to escape with their own lives, but their dwellings have been destroyed and their farms ruined. They are absolutely homeless and helpless, and unless relief is furnished they will perish in large numbers of cold and want. _ _

The facts of the case are abundantly guaranteed by the most reliable authorities. Our Ambassador in Con- stantinople, Sir N. O'Conor, G.C.8., vouches for the statements made, and says" it is earnestly to be hoped that more ample funds will be subscribed." The Arch- bishop of Canterbury said in a letter to the "Times,' "I have no hesitation in saying that the Macedonian Relief Fund is, in my judgment, entitled to ask sup- nort from us all." The Bishops of London, Rochester Hereford, and Worcester have also appealed on its be- half. Leading Nonconformists have joined in com- mending the appeal. A new impetus has now been offered by the generous offer of an anonymous donor, that he will contribute £l,OOO to the fund, and Mr. Noel Buxton offers also £5OO, provided that the amount of £2:5,000 is raised by the last day of this present month. May I venture to hope that Warrington people will yet rally to this cause by additional subscriptions, so that the town's contribution may reach a more worthy total than a little over £7O ! Money can be paid either to the Boroueh Treasurer or to Parr's Bank, or to the Rector of Warrington, the Rev. Father Wilson Mr. Alderman Bennett, or to myself. Surely we can double this £7O? I earnestly beg every reader who scans these lines to render some personal help, and to endeavour to incite others to like generosity.—Yours, Scc. JOHN YONGE.

19, Wilson-Patten-street, January 13, 1904.

STOCKTON HEATH GARDEN SOCIETY.

Mr. Vera B. Davies presided at the annual meeting of this society on Tuesday evening. There' was an encouraging attendance, among those present being:— Messrs. W. H. Woodcock, Plinston, Ryan, F. Timperley, A. Broady. F. Pearson, Vernon, W. A. Timperley, Hallows, Welsby, M. G. Hearne, Acton, and T. R. Westbrook (hon. treasurer).

ELECTION OF PR ES I D ENT.

Mr. W. H. Woodcock said he rose with great pleasure to propose that Mr. Vere B. Davies be re-elected presi- dent for the ensuing year. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Davies had taken very great interest in the garden society and it was a pity, now that they had interested him for one rear, if they did not do it for another. He was hoping for a good season and a good show in the autumn— .(hear, bear.)—and he was sure Mr. Davies would give them the same kindly support as he had given in the past. (Hear, hear). Mr. F. Pearson seconded.

Mr. Davies said it was understood last year that they were to have a new president this year. ("No' no.") Mr. Woodcock said when they were tired with the old one they would certainly propose a new one. (Laugh- ter.) . The proposition was carried with enthusiasm. The Chairman said he was exceedingly obliged to them. He certainly took the greatest possible interest in the society, and as they had re-electedhim president he would do his utmost to further the interests of the society. He certainly understood that they were to haven new president each year, but notwithstanding the fact that they had broken their own rule, he would have pleasure in taking the position..

THE SECRETARY'S REpORT. The Secretary (Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins) in his report stated that the past season had been for all lovers of horticulture an especially depressing and unsatisfactory one. For a young society it had been especially dis- heartening as not only was the whole season one of almost continuously wet and unseasonable weather, but the show day was ushered in by storm and rain whick continued during the whole day. Notwithstanding the adverse state of the weather they had every: reason on other considerations to congratulate the society. There was at the show and in the outdoor classes a marked increase in the entries and exhibits over 010 previous year. The entries for the outdoor classes for the year 1902 numbered 25. Those for the same classes for the season of 1903 numbered 37. In the entrieefor the show exhibits there was a more marked increase —indeed• an extraordinary increase—over that of the previous year. For the season 1902 the show exhibits numbered 56. Those for 1903 numbered 154. This result having regard to the inclemency of the season, spoke well for the vitality of the society. The membership for the year 1902 was 120, as against 102 for the year 1903. The thanks of the society were due to those who gave donations. Though few in number, their kindness was more appreciated. Mr. Plinston proposed that the report be adopted. and that Mr. Jenkins be thanked for his services. (Hear hear.) Mr. F. Timperley seconded, and the proposition was carried unanimously.

THE FINANC IA L STATEMENT. Mr. T. R. Westbrook (hon. treasurer) submitted the annual balance sheet. It appeared that the year com- menced with a balance in hand of .£8 17s. 3d, and a prize fund of £1 16s. Gd. in addition. At the close of the year the prize fund balance had been absorbed, and the balance in hand reduced to £3 10s. 7d.

This report was also adopted, on the proposition of Mr. Woodcock, seconded by Mr. Welsby.

RE-APPOINTMENT OF TREA.SURER.

Mr. Plinston proposed the re-election of Mr. T. R. Westbrook as treasurer of the society. No member,he said, had the interest of the society more at heart than Mr. Westbrook. A more agreeable and painstaking treasurer they could not have. (Hear,•hear.) Mr. Pearson seconded and coupled with the proposi- tion a voteof thanks to Mr. Westbrook for his past services, and it was unanimously agreed to.

RESIGNATION OF THE SECRETARY. Writing to Mr. T. R. Westbrook, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins (hon. secretary to the society) said :—" I should be exceedingly obliged if the Society would appoint a young man to take the position of Secretary—someone who has more freedom in regard to time. My time is not my own and it is impossible for me to attend the meetings of the society as often as I should like al- though I have done my best to do so. lam sorry that lam obliged to ask the society to re:ieve me of the duties."

The resignation of Mr. Jenkins was accepted with regret. On the proposition of Mr. Hallows, seconded by Mr. Pearson, Mr. W. A. Timperley was unanimously appointed hon Secretary. THE COMMITTEE. Ten names were submitted for the seven vacancies on the committee. The following were appointed as the result of the voting Messrs. Woodcock, Plinston, Onslow, Ryan, Pearson, A. Broady and F. Timperley.

VICE-PRESIDENTS. Mr. Woodcock said the society had not previously elected vice-presidents, but by doing so they could show their good feelings towargis Mr. Jenkins and also recognise the assistance they nd had from two other gentlemen who had materially aided the work of the society,namely Mr. Leonard Davies and the Rev. H. V. Pigot. He therefore proposed that the Rev. H. V. Pigot,Mr. Leonard Davies and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins be the vice-presidents. Mr. Plinston seconded and the proposition was agreed to.

-I\fr..Tenkins, in acknowledging the compliment, said he would continue to take the deepest interest in the society and do what he could for its welfare.

S UGC F,ST lONS. Mr. E. England wrote making several suggestions. He advocated the encouragement of cultivating flowers 41 boxes, as was the case at Liverpool and other places. Mr. Vernon made several suggestions for re-arranging the class list at the next show and stated that he had received anumber of promises for special prizes. Mr. Timperley thought the committee ought to fix a time limit for persons joining the society and not allow some to wait until the last minute before the show. Last year some competitors simply waited until the last moment to see what exhibits they had before they would speculate a shilling. The society did not want memly?rs of that kind. (Hear, hear.) He also thought a small inspection committee should be ap- pointed to visit gardens a few times before the judges went round. (Hear, hear). Mr. Woodcock stated that by the rules a member of the committee was unable to take more than three prizes. He thought no restriction of that kind should be enforced as it militated against the show. Mr. Acton proposed that therestrictions 'be withdrawn. This was agreed to. ,

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

By " Ovalist."

Fifteen points to nil was the extent Warrington's victory on Siturdav when the Hull team were the visi- tors to Wilderspool. This is a good start for the new year, and augurs well for Warrington's progress in the league. They have been showing very good form of late and there was no question about their superiority over the East Riding men. Hull have generally suc- cumbed to Warrington, and as the latter captured two points on the Boulevard ground earlier in the season the result of Saturday's game was practically a fore- gone conclusion.

Hull have, for years; bceii styled the " all blacks," but the sombre-hued jersey has given place to one with broad blue and white bands,and the men were scarcely recognisable when they stepped into the . enclosure. The change of colours does not seem to have brought any luck for Hull can only point to six successes out of 18 games and one does not wonder that there is serious talk of running the sister code in the port. Fortunately the Rovers. are going strongly and maintaining a keen interest among their own supporters in the Northern Union game. The Hull men themselves seem to have lost heart and they played ia very slack fashion on Saturday. One could not help thinking that they had no 'hopes of winning the match, and therefore muddled through anyhow. It was, consequently a very disappointing game from a spectacular point of view.

Fish was again the hero of tho hour, and provided. numerous sensations which saved the game from absolute dullness. He was nearly a scorer in the first five minutes, Taylor just stopping him at the Corner. Hull resorted to off-side tactics to save . the line, and were promptly • penalised. Then followed sensation No. 1. Fish placed the bail and instead of kicking for goal he tipped it a yard forward with his foot, picked up and ran in a try on his own. At least most people thought it was a try, but Referee, Edmondson didn't, and promptly disallowed the point amid groans from the crowd.

The decision was perfectly correct, however Fish's notion was all right, but he should have kicked the ball a bit further. It must be kicked beyond the mark or place where the ,penalty was given. As the, Hull men stood on the mark and Fish retired a few yards for the kick, he clearly did not Comply with the rules .in just tipping the ball forward a foot or two. He co-ld have kicked in any direction so long as the ball travelled beyond the penalty mark, and completed the try in his own way. Wattle Davies at Leeds ,a few weqks ago presumably made no error of this kind for he registered a similar try. Fish was consider- ably disappointed, for be could easily have landed a goal if he had chosen to kick,

This little set-back caused Warrington to press home the attack with renewed ardour, and it was not long ere Fish scampered past Taylor after good com- bination. He also landed a goal and Warrington obtained a lead of five points which .had to suffice until well on in the second half. Play was of a very desultory nature, and the slowness of the referee did not improve matters Hull resorted to a deal of off-side play, and it was ultimately their undoing. Parry got quite out of his place in covering Fish with the result that Isherwood broke through,and serving Fish at the right moment another five points was the result. Warring- 'ton showed their best from after this and just on time Bate neatly tipped the ball over a scrum on the line, and Davies grounded far out. Fish landed a third magnificent goal and raised the total to 15-0. During the last two games he has contributed just four goals, four tries—a nice round twenty points. Salford and Hull keenly appreciate his effectiveness.

It is just as well that Fish has .come back to his best form, for the other wing has not caused the scorers a great deal of trouble since Harris's injury. Hockenhallr though reliable as a rule, does not possess either the speed or dash necessary for a wing player. He missed a glorious chance given him by Dickenson on Saturday and it will be a good thing when Harris is fit or when Kenyon qualifies for the post. Another scoring man would increase the effectiveness of the team immensely. One sighs for Leytham of Lancaster whom Warrington let slip and who has proved a great catch for Wigan. The longest purse won here, as it generally- does.

Warrington are .doing so well in. the League tourney that only. Leeds and Oldhanfean show a better record. The championship is still open and it is worth while considering Warrington's play with a view to improvement. In my view the chief fault lies at half back. Davies has come on a lot, while Bate shows good judgment, but still possesses the serious fault of holding the ball too long. If,when he cannot break away on his own, he would Sling the ball Promptly to the centres instead of " weigh- ing " it, we should see more quick passing and run- ning, with more tries as a result. Our three-quqrter line can beat any other in the League if properly i nursed, and the halves must see to t that the ball goes out gulch more quickly. At present the centres seldom get the ball until they are covered, and the fault lies entirely with the half-backs.

- Meanwhile there is something for the committee to do, and that is to , strengthen the forward line Though the scrtunmagers have done well of late they still lack weight for struggles with the Yorkshire teams and I shall be glad indeed when the long promised couple of big north-countrymen make their appear- ance. Hunslet, Bradford, Leeds, and Salford all emphasized Warrington's weakness.

PADGATE COTTAGE HOMES. Concert and Prize Giving.

The 200 poor children who are under the care of the Warrington Guardians, and who are so comfortably housed in the Cottage Homes at Padgate, had a happy time on Wednesday evening when the annual children's concert and prize giving took place. The homes resounded with the merry laughter of the youngsters whose bright faces and cheery smiles bespoke their pleasurable anticipations on this annual occasion. The programme was provided entirely by 50 of the children who acquitted themselves. admirably. A number of invitationshad been issued to members of the Board of Guardians and their friends as well as to some of the past inmates of the institution. The room had been tastefully decorated, the Christmas decorations having been added to and made more effective. The decoration of the platform and it surroundings was extremely pleasing. On a white ground, Led stripes had been placed diagon ally, and in the interstices (floral emblems were dis- played. Overhead were festoons of coloured ribbon,• and on the walls were such mottoes as "Health and proSperity to our Guardians," "I Greet You," .` Glory to God in the Highest," "The Season's Greetings./' One of the emblems bore the monogram " T.H.A." in honour of the uhairman of the Schools Committee.

Mr. T. H. Ashcroft presided, and amongst those pre- sent were the Mayoress (Mrs. William Bolton), Rev. W. Pollock Hill (chqplain of the school),Mrs. Clay, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, S. Davies, William Hickman,James Payton George Mather, R. Forshaw, A. Jolley (Guardians), Dr. Peacocke, andiAr. Shearer. The programme opened with an action song entitled "The cat in school,' followed by another song in which "the house that Jack built" was fully illustrated by the children with toys. Some amusement was caused by the too emphatic is of "h" by the children, but it s perhaps better for them to err in this direction than to bc, " aitchless "altogether. Three little girls sang very sweetly "The little cooks," whilst Master T. Thorpe made a typical schoolmaster when singing a schoolmaster's' song, during which his scholars had rather a had time of it. Miss H. Taylor and Master T. Farrell were heard to advantage in the duet "Joan and Darby," and Miss Taylor also sang the solo "Old Folks at Home, in which a num- ber of nigger boys sang the chorus. "Ten Little Nigger Boys' was the title of a song in which ten boys*ith blackened faces and nigger dress delighted the audience with their singing and perform- ance under the direction of Master Thorpe. The songs were agreeably interspersed with recitations and drill. Miss E. Clough recited with feeling "Tiss me good- night," and Master H. Skelland excelled himself when reciting "Rather warm," his enunciation and general demeanour being all that could he desired. The con- cluding items were a series of tableaux, in which many familiar incidents and scenes were depicted. The group- ing and posing of the figures was graceful and exact, whilst the living wealth of colour in the beautiful dresses of the children heightened the effect of the scene. The pianoforte accompaniments were played by Mr. Wright ti-e popular superintendent of the school. The beautiful dresses worn by the chil- dren had been made by the Matron (Mrs. Wright), Miss Ivey and Mrs. French (schoolmistresses.) In the training of the children' on which much care and attention had been bestowed, the superintendent had been ably assisted by Miss Ivey and the teachers. At the close of the programme, the Mayoress gra- ciously presented prizes to the following scholars. PRIZE LIST.

H. Welsby, proficiency in arithmetic, S ini ircl V[ book. Holy Bible. J. Flannaghan, proficiency in arithmetic, Standard V., writing case. Gilbert Welsby, proficiency in writing, Standard IV., Prayer Book. William Moran, proficiency in reading, Standard 111., book, "Pets of the Farm."

Maggie M'Mahon, school monitor, work box. Beatrice Bibby, school girl, work box. Ellen Bush, the most popular girl in school,umbrella. SPEECHES AND 'iVOTES OF THANKS.

Mr. Mather proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayoress in felicitous terms.

-- Mr. S. Davies seconded, and expressed the opinion that the Mayoress would go away very much pleased with thelhealthy and happy appearance of the children connectedjwith the institution. He had been coming to the Cottage Homes for the last 15 years, and he did not remember such a splendid finish to the entertain- ment as that which they had just witnessed in the tableaux. It reflected the greatest credit upon those who had the management of it. He wished members of the Corporation would come and see how the children were cared for. • The Schools Committee from the Chair- man downwards, were doing their level best to make the homes as perfect as possible. They had a splendid staff andhe believed theinstitution would equal any in Lancashire. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Hickman heArtily supported the proposition and assured the ratepayers that the best money they spent was expended on that institution for the support of • children who would otherwise be waifs and strays and pests and nuisances in the town. It must be a source of satisfaction to the Mayoress to take part in that en- tertainment, (Hear, hear.) • The proposition was carried with acclammation. The Chairman said he was desired by the Mayoress to thank them on her behalf for the kind vote of thanks.

Mr. A. J. Jolley then propose 1 a vote of thanks to Mr. Ashcroft. The position of Chairman of a committee that controlled an institution like that, he said, was one that any man ought to be proud to fill. It entailed many responsibilities and on the other hand it afforded the Chairman an opportunity of doing lasting good. During the time he had/been Chairman of the committee Mr. Ashcroft had striven to discharge the duties in the best possible way. (Hear, hear.) Dr. Peacocke seconded, and the vote was carried with cheers. Cheers were also raised for Mr. Mather vice- chairman, and Mrs. Clay. In responding, Mr. Ashcroft said he had always en- deavoured to do his duty during the six years he had been coming to the schools. A remark was made recently that parents were obliged to send their chil- dren to the cottage homes. That was nntrue. When a poor person sought relief of the Guardians, the reliev- inp officer if he thought itadvisable, recommended that the children be sent to the homes. In many cases the children were being neglected and living in squalid sur- roundings, and the best way to render assistance was by relieving the parents of the children and receiving them into the homes. It was sometimes said "Why don't you adopt the boarding out system ?" The reply was that the Guardians had adopted a better policy by establishing those cottage homes. Here they had effec- tivecontrol of the children and the results obtained had been satisfactory. They were not sure that that would be the case if they sent the children to different homes. (Hear, hear.) Three hearty cheers were raised for the Master and Matron, and Mr. Wright briefly acknowledged the com- pliment.

A WARRINGTON SOLICITOR FOR THE FIJI ISLANDS Impending Departure of Mr. R. Crompton.

On Wednesday next Mr. Robert Crompton, a partner. in the firm of Messrs. Arthur Browne and Crompton, solicitors, Sankey-street, Warrington, will leave the town with which he has been associated from his childhood to enter upon a new but similar sphere of work in the Fiji Islands. He has entered into partnership with a legal firm at Suva, the capital of the Western Pacific, which is situated on the island of Viti Levu and he will set sail from Liverpool in "The Majestic" on Wednesday for New ' York. From the American seaport the will journey to Suva via Vancouver. Mr. Crompton has (rained a large circle of friends in Warrington and they will all wish him complete success in his new undertaking. Born in Russia,- Mr. Crompton received his early training in the Warrington Blue Coat School and the sound education imparted to him • under the superin- tendence of Mr. E. Rose, the respected head master, has stood him in good stead. When Mr. Arthur Browne commenced practice twenty years ago Mr. Crompton entered his employ as an office boy. Such progress did he make, that thirteen years later, in 1891, Mr. Browne gave him his articles and having passed the usual examination he was in April, 1900, admitted as a solicitor. Mr. Browne took him into partnership on January Ist, 1901. By" Old Blues" in particular i. Mr. Crompton held in high regard and a number of them have this week expressed their esteem and good wishes by presenting him with a handsome signet ring. Mr. H. L. Gerrard, who for the past ten years has acted as Mr. Crotnpton's confidential clerk, will con- tinue to serve in that capacity, and on Thursday he sailed for Suva from Liverpool by the " Medic." He will travel by the Cape route.

EDUCATION IN WARRINGTON,

Business of the Next Committee Meeting.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee will be held at the Town Hall at six o'clock on Monday evening when the business under consideration will include the following : FINANCE COMMITTEE.-A LOAN OF £6,500.

At a meeting of the Finance Comniittee circular letters from the Board of Education notifying the par- ment of grants to various schools were sut-nnitted and noted. The committee having discussed the question of payment of grants, it was resolved that the Board of Education be asked to continue or to adopt some similar arrangement for the payment of grants for the financial year from the Ist April, 1904. to the 31st March, 1905 as that which was in operation for the year 1903-1901 A letter, dated 6th January, 1904, from the Borough Treasurer,forvvarding copy of the sanction received from the Local Government Board to a loan of £6,500, for the provision of a working balance for carrying the new Education Act into effect, was sub- mitted and noted.

SCHOOL M A.NA.GEM ET CO'Si M ITT E E -CO UNC IL LOR MAG INN'S wc:Nic.

At a meeting of this Committee letters dated the 14th and 17th December, 1903, were submitted from Mr. Councillor 4laginn, requesting that notices be issued to the school's of his intention to provide a free pie-nic to the children from St. John's Ward who make the highe3t possible number of attendances at school between the Ist January and the end of June, but the committee, whilst recognising the value of the proposal as a means of teaching the children habits of punctuality and regularity, could not see their way themselves to issue the desired notices to the schools, but suggested that an efficient means of bringing the matter under the notice of the children interested would be by his com- municating with the head teachers.

SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE.

A circular letter dated the 24th December, 1903, from the Secretary of Henshaw's Blind Asylum, Old Trafford, Manchester, intimating that their terms for the recep- tion of children to that institution would, as from the Ist January, 1901, be increased from £2O 6s. Bd. per annum per child (including clothing) to £23 ss. per annum per child together with .23 for clothing, was submitted at a meeting of this committee and it was resolved to recommend that the increased charges as set out above be paid in respect of those pupils who are already in the institution as well as those who may be sent hereafter.

Reference having been made to the suggestion which was brought forward at the last meeting of the Education Committee as to the present system of obtaining medical certificates for children who are unable to attend school owing to sickness , it was, after some discussiou. resolved that the Director be requested to make inquiries as to the practice obtaining in other districts in this matter.
