MOTIONS.—APPOINTMENT OF scrtooL KEEPERS.

Notices of the following motions have been given:— By the Rev. John Yonge: "That Resolution A 16., July 20th, 1903, section r, be reconsidered, with a view to special consideration of its relation to cases of care- takers in charge of schools previous to appointment under this authority." By the Chairman: "That the next meeting of the Education Committee be held on the 22nd instead of the 15th February." At a meeting of the Committee on Thursday last, a letter having been submitted from the managers of the Silver-street school, drawing attention to an entry made by H.M. Inspector in the log book of that school, re- quiring the sub-division of the main rooms by suitable partitions, it was resolved that the Board of Education be asked to determine whether the provision of parti- tions for the purpose of sub-dividing rooms in volun- _ Wry schools is to be regarded as alterations and improvements in the buildings, and there- fore, under section 7 (1) (d) of the Education Act, 1902, to be paid for by' the managers out of funds provided by them, or whether the provision of such partitions is to be considered as the duty of the local Education Authority in maintaining and keeping effici- ent such schools, and, therefore, under section 7 (1), to be paid for by the Authority. A letter, dated the 19th: day of December, 1903, from the Hon. Secretary of the Warrington Head Masters' Association, forwarding copy of a resolution passed by that Xssociation, requesting the Committee to take into consideration the advisability of adopting a luniform time for the daily opening and closing of the elementary schools in the Borough, was read, and, after some discussion, it was resolved that, for the future, all elementary schools in the Borough meet from 9 to 12 in the morning and from 1-30 to 4 o'clock in the afternoon. A letter, dated the 23rd December, 1903, from the Hon. Secretary of the Association of Foundation Managers of Church of England schools in Warrington, asking that two rooms in the Secondary school may be used each morning for the purpose of giving religious instruction to the Church of England children attend- ing that school, was *submitted, but, inNiew of the terms of section 4 of the Education Act,1902, the Com 7 mitee were unable to entertain the request. A letter was read from the Colonel commanding the 8/40 Regimental District, drawing attention to the advisability of introducing some system of fire.-drill in the schools in the Borough, and the director MIS re- quested to report as to the best means of introducing some such drill into the schools.

• Death of Cheshire's Oldest Cricketer. 1 /At the ripe age of eighty one, Mr. Charles Harding who enjoyed the distinction of being the oldest cricketer in Cheshire,has passed away,at his residence Haslington, near Crewe. He was one of the pioneers of the Crewe Athletic Cricket Club, and played with the late Mr. Richard Trevethick., the great railway- engineer, and Mr. F. W. Webb. He was also the old- est railway official in Cheshire. Firemen's Annual Dinner.

The annual dinner in connection with the Fire Brigade was held on Thursday night at the White Hart Hotel, Market place. The Superintendent Mr. Frank Turner. and the Deputy Superintendent Mr. F. Turner, jun. were invit3d by the members of the Brigade. A substantial dinner was provided by the post and Hostess Mr. and Mrs. Teece. -Afterwards a social evening was spent, members of the brigade, Messrs. Vernon, Sergeant Monks, Cook, Dalton,Taylor Archer contributing songs. The health of the Super- intendent was proposed by Sergeant Monks and drunk with musical honours. The Superintendent suitably responded paying a tribute to the brigade for suc- ceeding in coping so well with the numerous fires during the year. The health of the Deputy Super- intendent was also proposed. Tke Deputy Super- intendent thanked the members and afterwards gave a resume of the work done by the Brigade during the year. The senior members proposed a vote of thanks to all those gentlemen and local firms who subscribed to the annual dinner.

_ SLA.CKNESS OF TRADE AT EARLESTOWN. Trade in'Earlestown is by no means improving, and in different parts of the town distress is felt acutely. The London and North-Western Waggon Works have not yet gone on full time, and during the last month several hundreds have been discharged und others pen- sioned off. On Thursday. seven pattern-makers em- ployed at the Vulcan Foundry, a large locomotive works at•Earlestown, received their notices, and that is counted upon as foreshadowing slackness at that Particular works. The building of waggons at the Viaduct Works has been completely stopped, excepting when the firm are replacing waggons which have been broken up, and the main work is in thrx repairing shops. The Relief Committee have still a number of cases of distress on their book; and are appealing for funds.

The loaves here shown are drawn to scale according to the following statements tor which the Government Inquiry Blue Book, 1903, is responsible: In England In Germany 3ss. 6d. Blue Book, page 120.

In England In Germany Blue Book, page 287.

From these figures it follows that an Englishman's hourly wage for skilled labour is 10!,d., while a German's hourly wage for skilled labour is _id. HALF=AN=HOUR'S SKILLED LABOUR IN ENGLAND PURCHASES THREE TIMES AS MUCH BREAD AS HALF-AN-HOUR'S SKILLED LABOUR IN GERMANY.

In other words the English (Free Trade) loaf is more than three times as large as the German (Protection) loaf.

In towns outside London and Berlin respectively, the wages are given: England, 365.; Germany, 225. 6d. Taking these into account does not alter the proportions of the loaves.

[By lermission of "The Daily News," London.

had found it to be of considerable help to him in hisl sphere of labour at Witton. The guild was a rallying ground upon which people of all shades of opinion thought and temperament could unite in their desire to, consecrate themselves afresh to God, to prepare them- selves for service, and to gain mutual inspiration. He did not think there was any other movement which brought people of varying thoughts and temperaments together like the guild undoubtedly did. Some time. ago when the Twentieth Century Fund movement was in progress, and they were looking forward to that immense sum of money being in the hands of the treasurers, they all began to wonder how it would be used, and the fact was brought to his mind that throughout the country they had their young people gatheriug together and culti- vating their powers and talents so that by and by they would be in a fit state to- carry on more success- fully and upon more constitutional principles the great work which lay ahead of their Church. There was always one source of fear and that was lest the social side should predominate in the movement. Of course' it was absurd to think that people must have "a long face" in order to be religious. There were times like the present season for solemnity, but there were also times when they could be joyous, and the more frequently they could be joyous and cheerful the better it would be for their Church and for the worldat large. He sincerely trusted that whilst the guild' throughout the country encouraged the social side and made full provision for it, they would endeavour to obtain that cheerfulness and joyousness which came from a heart that was right with God. If the guild fulfilled its object of making young Christians work to greater advantage it would not have been instituted in vain. On behalf of thel visitors from Witton he wished the Bqld-street guild many happy returns of the day and he tru 'cted that each succeeding year would be a year of increasing power, usefulness, joyousness, and stability andstrength for Christ, for their Church, and for the country at large. (Applause). The Rev. W. C. J. NEVILLE said he was glad to see so many present and he thought the attendance from the outlying districts was very creditable indeed. He- had a special reason for being pleased to see M . Eaton with them. When the Wesley Guild was instituted by conference he (the speaker) was stationed at Shanklin in the Isle of Wight and he formed the first guild estab- lished in the island. Mr. Eaton was goiug to be minister at Shanklin after the next Conference in September and he (the speaker) hoped he would enjoy his term of residence there as much as he (Mr. Neville) did, and that he would find the guilds in the circuit in good con- dition. Before he left the Isle of Wight eleven branches of the guild had been formed in the various towns and villages and he hoped they wereall flourishing. He be- lieved very strongly in the work of the Wesley Guild and in the pert it was destined to play in the Metho- dist Church. Referring to the production during the past- year of the Guild Charter, the speaker said he regarded it as acknowledging the right of the young people of the Church to hold meetings for their mutual improve- ment and the good of the Church. He thought that if the young people of the Church were encouraged, and the guilds were worked upon the constitutional lines. laid down by the Central Council they could not but prove of good to the Church. In a great many places it had been wondered whether the extra meetings of the. Wesley Guild would not detract from other meetings and injure the class meetings, week-night service &c. In. his opinion the Guild would not injure other meetings if it was worked strictly in connection with them. In the Wesley Guild they had the missing link between the Sunday school and the Church. He knew a. great many young people considered themselves, all too soon, to be too old to attend Sunday school, and the Wesley Guild wanted to retain them. He person- ally believed in young people remaining in the Sunday schpol in adult classes and was glad to think that when he left the Warrington circuit he would be going to a Church where there was a Sunday school of something like 900 and where there were senior classes. The speaker alluded to the pleasant holiday spent by the Guild members at Ilfracombe last Whitsuntide and pointed out that although they enjoyed themselves thoroughly, the devotional element was well to the fore. He hoped that in time they would have in the War- rington circuit one central rally to be attended by every guild in the circuit. (Applause.) The meeting then concluded.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Meeting.

This weekly meeting was held in Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. A. Downes, who spoke upon setting a good example. The speaker was Mr. A. E. Easterbrook, of Manchester and his subject was "Common Honesty as tie need of the nation." Honesty, he said was needed all round, socially and politically. If these was honesty between capital and labour we should see less friction between employer and employed. Then they ought to be honest to them- selves, to the Church and to God. Having received good at the P.S.A. meetings and been brought near to Christ, it was then the honest duty of all such to seek to join themselves to some Church and so honestly labour for the good of others.—Mr. Healey and M. Cotton took part in the meeting.—The Secretary urged the members to make the open meetinr,. next Sunday week, a great success.—Mr. Payton Mus.Bac., rendered an organ solo entitled "Bethlehem."

THE NEW VICAR OF CHRIST CHURCH No Innovations to be Introduced.

The Rev. G. C. Briggs preached his first sermon as Vicar of Christ church, Latchford, on Sunday evening, selecting for his text the words "Then Samuel took a stone and set it between Mizpeh and Shen, and called the place of it Ebenezer, saying, 'Hitherto hath the Lord helped us.' He could assure them, he said, that he did not leave Chester for Warrington with a light and fear- less heart. It was only after careful consideration that he decided to accept the living,not because he was tired of his work there, but because it seemed to him to be right to accept work of a far more responsible character The living was entirely unsought py him, but he felt that he had to consent. But he had two reasons for coming here which another man would nothave had. In the first place, he had kaown their previous Vicar intimately for a number of years, and he felt that on that account their welcome to him would belnone the less; and in the second place, he had, strangely enough, succeeded Mr. Screeton at St. Barnabas, Chester, where he (the speaker) had been working all the time their late Vicar had been with them. at Latchford. He therefore knew something of his methods and ideas, and he should not be likely to upset anything which Mr. Screaton had so success- fully carried on. Perhaps it would be as well for him to state at the outset of his ministry that he did not in- tend to makeany changes in the manner of conducting the services, of springing anything new on them in the way of ritual. What he found to have been the custom in the past he should rigidly adhere to. To his mind an incalculable amount of mischief was done in parishes by newly-appointed incumbents who, on their arrival in a parish, made petty and needless changes and alterations in the services, which only irritated and annoyed. There were more right ways than one and a thing was not right because they were accustomed to it. Still he ventured to think that when a new vicar was appointed he should as .far as he was able, and where no principle was involved, do his utmost to avoid making needless changes. That, he promised, should be his policy. He should endeavour by the help of God to carry on .the work of the church and parish to the best of his ability and power on the lines on which it had been carried on in the past. He appealed to them, and he knew he would not appeal in vain, for the same cordial sympathy and support which they had extended to their Vicar in the past. From what he had heard and already knew, he felt convinced they would be ready to extend to him.the same hearti- ness and goodwill which they had shown to his predecessor.

THE WESLEY GUILD.

Festival Services and Rally at Bold

Street Church,

Among the organizations which help to strengthen the ranks of Methodism. the Wesley Guild holds a high , place and there are several active branches in the War- rington circuit. On Sunday the members of the Bold- street Guild celebrated their annual festival and special services were held. There were large congrega- tions the church being filled in the evening. The Rev. T. A. Kinnings, of Southport, was the preacher and his eloquent, discourses were much appreciated. At the morning services he spoke upon music, his text being " Singunto the Lord with the harp; with the harp, and the voice of a psalm " (Psalat.93 v. 5). Music, he said was the channel by which they were led up to God, and the Christian found music everywhere. He alluded to the power of music, its mellowing influence, and its joyousness. Religion without joy was a world without light, In the evening the rev, gentleman based his ser- mon on the words "The prophet that hath a dream, let him tell a dream; and he that hath my word, let him speak my word faithfully. What is the chaff to the wheat? saith the Lord." (Jeremiah, 23c., 28v.). The preacher took occasion to refute some of the statements made by Mr. Blatchford in recent articles on Christian- ity.—The choir creditably rendered the anthem "The day Thou gayest". On Monday evening a united rally was held, the Rev. G. H. Carnson presiding. There was a good attendance of representatives of various guilds in the circuit and a contingent from Witton, Northwich, was also pre- sent. The Rev. T. A. Kinnings delivered a helpful address. Ambition was the theme touched upon. The speaker expressed his approval of men having the high- est ambitions. It was their duty to have the highest ambition for the mental as well as the spiritual culture of life, and he was prepared to say that the worthy life was the life of goodness, In life they should have the highest ideals for their ambitions. He was not one of those ministers wholbelieved that theiChristian life had not much joy and gladness in it. They had no right to decry the sports of the day, but what they had to decry was the young man who could find time to play football on a-Saturday, but who could not find time to go to the House of God on S'unday. (Applause.) They had to decry the wrong use and abuse of pleasures. He pointed out that the way to worldly honour was very narrow and very circum- scribed, but if the ambition was lofty and determined they would gain both honour and distinction. In con- clusion he said they should reconsecrate their lives to the highest and noblest purposes, and one day when the ambition had been realized on earth, the ambition would be realized in the eternal home, and God would bid them come to Him as "good and faithful servants." The Chairman said it had been a great delight to him to hear Mr. Kinnings' address. Although he had never heard him speak before,he had known him for many years and had heard of his energy, his enjoyment of life and , the way in which he influenced so many people. Speak- ing of the varying ages which the guildmembership em- braced, the Chairman said he believed that as long as I they lived they might retain freshness of spirit if they I trusted in God. It was decided that a special meeting of the committee be held on Thursday, the 21st January for the purpose of meeting the • repsesentative of the Board of Educa- tion. Suggestions with reference to the provision of higher education in the Borough,' which had been pre- pared by the,Chairman of the committee and the Director in accordance with the decision of the com- mittee at their meeting on the 29th October, 1903 were submitted, and it was decided to hold a special meeting of the committee at 2.30 o'clock on Monday, the 11th January, 1904, for the purpose of formulating a scheme to submit to the representative of the Board of Educa- tion at the meeting to be held on the 21st January. At the meeting on January 11th after deliberation, it was resolved that, having considered the suggestions for the provision of higher education' in the Borough, the committee desire to express their warm approval of them, and to place on record their gratitude to the Chairman of the eommittee and the Director for the trouble taken by them in the matter. . At a further meeting of the Committee on Thursday mat the matter was again under consideration. The Committee expressed their approval of the scheme, and recommended the Education Committee to adopt it.

DARESBURY PETTY SESSIONS. ---- TUESDAY.—Before Mr. H. Nield (in the chair), Col. Ridgway and Mr. Roger C. Parr. MiscELLf,.sv.—Walter Harmer, 12, Greenall's-avenuen Stockton Heath, was charged with leaving his horse and lurry on the highway without anyone in charge o 24th December.—The officer who proved the case stated that the defendant was away 20 minutes.—The defend- ant said he was away only a few minutes while he had a shave. He was fined 55.--Robert Large, of Narrow- lane, Statham, was fined 2s. 6d. for riding a bicycle on the highway at 5.40 a.m. on January 5, without a lighted lamp.-IVlary Jane Finn, acting as a pedlar at Latchford, on the 11th inst., without a certificate, was sent to prison for seven days. CRUELTY TO A. HORSE.—Joseph Eaton, of Runcor was charged with cruelty to a horse by working it whilst in an unfit state at Grappenhall, on December 16. Inspector Gibson, R.S.P.C.A., proved the case, and said the horse was very lame. It had since been destroyed. —Lithe circumstances the Bench ordered the defendant to pay the costs, 3s. 6d. GAME TRESPASS.—Jame; Davidson, Cartwright- street, Warrington, was summoned for having on the 2nd January, committed a trespass by entering in the day tine in search of game on land in the occupation of Henry Dodd, at Walton. Mr. Draper (Messrs. Robert Davies and Co.) prosecuted on behalf of tha owner of the land, Sir Gilbert Greenall, aAnd said the keeper was on duty in Walton Superior when he heard two shots fired. He went in the direction of the shots and saw four men one of whom was the defendant, in the field with a dog and two guns. The men were seen to pick up a bird,but the keeper was too far away to see what it was. The men than disappeared, and subsequently got into a boat and went away.—A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed.— Richard Hughes was summoned for trespassing in search of game on land belonging to Sir Richard Brooke, and in the occupation of Mrs. Done, at Kekewich, on the 29th October and the 31st October, 1903. The defend- ant was farther charged with using threatening language to Joseph Jones, keeper. Mr. Browne, prosecuted and stated that the defendant was summoned on the 10th November, 1903, with a man named Isaac Smith. The present defendant did not appear and a warrant was issued against him. Smith was dealt with at the last court, but the defendant Hughes was not apprehended until the previous day. Mr. Browne called a Mrs. Wkite (who saw the defendant on the land) and the keeper Jones. The latter stated that the men were very violent. Their pockets were bulky,as if filled with rabbits or hares, but he did not search them, deeming it not wise to do so under the kircumstances. Each man picked up a large stone, each larger than witness's fist, and threatened to knock, his head off if he interfered with them. Defendant was fined 40s. and costs, an advocate's fee being allowed.
